\abstractpage

Chapter 0
Introduction

Reinforcement Learning (RL) provides a mathematical framework for learning optimal sequential
decision-making policies through interactions with an unknown environment. The foundational
Markov Decision Process (MDP) abstraction has enabled RL to contribute significantly to fields such
as robotic control, autonomous systems, resource allocation, and generative modeling

[
117
,
14
,
19
]
. Recent advances in deep RL have
produced highly scalable policy optimization algorithms capable of operating in continuous state and
action spaces, including TRPO and PPO
[
189
,
212
]
, and have
fueled breakthroughs in complex tasks such as strategic gameplay, dexterous manipulation, and the
training of large language models (LLMs) via Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)

[
157
,
52
]
. These algorithms commonly rely on variants of likelihood
ratio policy gradients, trust-region optimization principles, and generalized advantage estimation to
achieve stable policy improvement.

Although deep RL has achieved impressive performance, the challenge of satisfying
safety
constraints
during the learning process remains largely unresolved. In many real-world systems,
agents must satisfy hard or soft constraints at all times in order to prevent catastrophic failures,
adhere to operational limits, ensure fairness, or guarantee that model behavior remains aligned with
human intent. For instance, robotic agents must avoid physical hazards, autonomous vehicles must
respect safety-critical rules, and LLMs must obey safety, toxicity, and honesty requirements. While
the control theory community has developed frameworks for safe control over several decades, the
design of safe, scalable, and statistically efficient RL algorithms is still an evolving research
frontier
[
75
,
40
]
. This thesis aims to address several
fundamental gaps that arise when constraints must be enforced in RL systems operating over long
horizons, finite horizons, or in environments where feedback is provided in the form of preferences.

A principled modeling abstraction for constrained decision-making is the
Constrained Markov
Decision Process
(CMDP)
[
16
]
. An average-reward CMDP augments the classical MDP with
cost functions
c_{i}(s,a)
for
i=1,\dots,m
, and the learning objective is to maximize a reward
criterion subject to constraints on long-term accumulated costs. For the average-reward setting, a
policy
\pi
is considered feasible if

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}c_{i}(s_{t},a_{t})\right]\leq d_{i},\quad\text{for all }i.

Although CMDPs provide the correct conceptual foundation, designing algorithms that can enforce such
constraints reliably, while remaining scalable under deep function approximation, is a challenging
problem. Lagrangian or penalty-based methods often suffer from weak constraint satisfaction or
unstable oscillatory behavior. Moreover, most existing methods are specialized for the infinite-horizon
discounted setting, and key analytical tools used in this setting do not generalize to alternative
objective formulations.

This thesis develops new theory and algorithms for constrained and preference-based RL across two broad themes:

1.

Explicit constraints
, arising from environment-provided cost functions and
thresholds (Chapters
1
and
2
).

2.

Preference-induced constraints
, arising from human or rater judgments that impose
implicit structure on the agent’s policy (Chapters
3
,

4
, and
5
).

The resulting algorithms introduce several novel technical ingredients, including a new average-reward
performance bound based on Markov chain mixing properties, a finite-horizon policy difference lemma,
Bayesian posterior sampling methods for preference learning, the first simple-regret guarantees for
preference-based RL, and multi-objective preference optimization strategies for large-scale alignment
of LLMs.

Average-Reward Constrained RL and the ACPO Algorithm.
Many real-world learning systems operate over long or continuous horizons, where the average reward
criterion is more suitable than the commonly used discounted objective. The average-reward setting
exhibits fundamentally different structure. For instance, the stationary distribution induced by a
policy plays a central role, and classical discounted tools such as the performance-difference lemma
degenerate when the discount factor approaches one. A key difficulty lies in characterizing how the
stationary distribution of an MDP changes when the policy changes. The discounted analysis implicitly
controls this effect through the discount factor, but such control is absent in the average case.

To address this gap, Chapter
1
develops the
Average-Constrained Policy
Optimization (ACPO)
algorithm, the first practical deep RL method for average-constrained MDPs
(ACMDPs). A central technical contribution is a new bound that relates the difference in average
rewards or costs of two policies to the
worst-case mixture level
of the Markov chain induced by
the policies. Informally, the mixture level characterizes the largest possible change in the stationary
distribution when the policy changes by a small amount. This bound provides an analogue to the
discounted performance-difference lemma but tailored to the average setting.

In particular, the thesis derives an inequality of the form

\eta(\pi)-\eta(\pi^{\prime})\leq L_{\mathrm{mix}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}}\left[A^{\pi^{\prime}}(s,a)\right],

where
L_{\mathrm{mix}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is controlled by the spectral properties of the transition kernel
under
\pi^{\prime}
, and
A^{\pi^{\prime}}
denotes the bias-based average-reward advantage function. This bound
leads to a trust-region constraint that enforces monotonicity in the average setting and supports
constraint satisfaction.

ACPO additionally introduces a new advantage estimator based on approximations of the bias function
rather than discounted returns, and incorporates sublevel sets of constraint functions to ensure that
all iterates remain feasible with respect to average constraint thresholds. A carefully constructed
line-search method provides additional stability. Empirically, ACPO achieves state-of-the-art
performance on Mujoco benchmarks, outperforming CPO
[
4
]
, PCPO
[
243
]
, PPO
[
185
]
, and ATRPO
[
250
]
. These results demonstrate that average-constrained RL requires fundamentally different analysis and algorithmic mechanisms compared to the discounted setting.

Episodic Constrained RL and the e-COP Algorithm.
Many RL tasks in robotics and generative modeling are naturally formulated as finite-horizon problems.
For example, episodic interactions arise in trajectory optimization, long-horizon manipulation tasks,
and diffusion-model denoising sequences
[
32
,
101
]
. In episodic
settings, the optimal policy is non-stationary and varies with the time step. Classical reinforcement
learning results that depend on stationary distributions or infinite-horizon contraction properties are
therefore not applicable.

A key technical obstacle is the absence of an episodic analogue of the infinite-horizon performance
difference lemma. Chapter
2
resolves this by introducing a new
policy difference
lemma for finite-horizon MDPs
. Informally, if
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are two policies for an episodic MDP
of horizon
H
, then the difference in their performances can be decomposed into a sum of expectations
over time-indexed advantage functions:

J(\pi)-J(\pi^{\prime})=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbb{E}_{s_{h},a_{h}\sim\pi}\left[A^{\pi^{\prime}}_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})\right].

Unlike infinite-horizon variants, the expectation is taken with respect to the non-stationary
trajectory distribution of
\pi
, and there is no discount factor involved. This lemma serves as the
foundation of the algorithmic design.

Based on this theoretical insight, the thesis develops
e-COP
, the first policy optimization
algorithm specifically tailored for finite-horizon CMDPs. The algorithm combines a KL-divergence-based
trust region, time-dependent advantage estimation, and a quadratic penalty method that avoids expensive
Hessian computations. The design ensures that constraint satisfaction is controlled at each time step
rather than only in a steady-state sense. Under appropriate scaling of the trust region size and
advantage estimation error, the algorithm satisfies a guarantee of the form

\text{cost}_{\pi_{k}}\leq d+O(\varepsilon_{k}),

where
\varepsilon_{k}
captures estimation error at iteration
k
. This produces stable convergence and
predictable constraint enforcement.

Experimental evaluations on Safety Gym benchmarks demonstrate that
e-COP
outperforms adapted
versions of CPO, FOCOPS, P3O, and PPO in terms of both reward and constraint satisfaction. The
algorithm’s design is compatible with deep neural network policies, making it a viable candidate for
safety-constrained RLHF applications in LLMs and diffusion models.

Preferences as Implicit Constraints: From Bandits to RLHF.
In many modern alignment settings, constraints arise not from environment-provided cost functions but
from human or rater preferences over system behavior. This motivates
preference-based RL

(PbRL), where the agent receives comparative feedback such as “trajectory A is preferred over
trajectory B” instead of numeric rewards. PbRL aligns well with human evaluation processes but
introduces new statistical and computational challenges, including rater noise, competence variation,
trajectory-level credit assignment, and the difficulty of inferring multi-objective trade-offs from
binary comparisons.

Chapter
3
introduces
warmPref-PS
, a Bayesian posterior sampling
algorithm for linear bandits that integrates offline preference datasets with online numerical rewards.
A central idea is the modeling of
rater competence
. Offline preference data may come from raters
whose reliability varies, and naively treating all comparisons as equally trustworthy can lead to poor
performance. The algorithm therefore maintains posterior distributions over model parameters and rater
ability, enabling selective incorporation of offline preference information. Theoretical analysis shows
that the algorithm obtains a Bayesian regret bound of order

\widetilde{O}\left(d\sqrt{T}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{rater}}}\right),

where
\alpha_{\mathrm{rater}}
quantifies rater competence. This is a significant improvement over
methods that ignore rater heterogeneity.

Moving beyond bandits, Chapter
4
develops
\mathsf{PSPL}
, a posterior sampling approach for
best-policy identification in preference-based RL. The objective shifts from cumulative regret to

simple regret
, which is appropriate for RLHF and alignment tasks where only the final learned
policy matters. A key challenge is that preferences are expressed over entire trajectories. To address
this, the algorithm jointly samples reward functions and transition dynamics, producing posterior samples
that induce candidate optimal policies. The chapter provides the first Bayesian simple-regret guarantees
for PbRL and introduces a bootstrapped variant that remains effective in high-dimensional or
function-approximation settings.

Multi-Objective Preference Optimization for LLM Alignment.
Large language models operate under multiple alignment objectives including helpfulness, harmlessness,
truthfulness, and non-toxicity. These objectives often conflict. Standard RLHF pipelines collapse these
criteria into a single reward model, which can obscure trade-offs and lead to reward hacking,
out-of-distribution failures, or undesirable emergent behaviors
[
237
]
. To address
these issues, Chapter
5
formulates alignment as a multi-objective constrained
preference optimization problem.

The resulting algorithm,
MOPO
, uses preference data to construct a concave constrained
optimization program. A primary preference objective is maximized subject to secondary objectives being
no less than user-specified thresholds. The optimization variables correspond to importance sampling
ratios and are updated using closed-form proximal steps. A behavior cloning step extracts a neural
policy that matches the optimal ratios. Under mild assumptions on the preference dataset, MOPO
approximates the Pareto front and avoids the need for inference-time preference conditioning. Extensive
experiments on multi-billion parameter LLMs show that MOPO achieves stable alignment performance across
tasks requiring multiple competing objectives.

Taken together, ACPO,
e-COP
,
warmPref-PS
,
\mathsf{PSPL}
, and
MOPO
form a unified framework for constrained reinforcement learning across explicit, implicit, and multi-objective constraint regimes. The technical contributions include: (i) new performance-difference bounds for average-reward CMDPs based on Markov chain mixture properties, (ii) a novel finite-horizon policy difference lemma for episodic MDPs, (iii) posterior sampling algorithms that integrate offline preferences with rater-competence modeling, (iv) the first simple-regret guarantees for preference-based RL, and (v) a scalable multi-objective preference optimization method for aligning large language models. These contributions address several key limitations of existing constrained RL methodologies and advance the theoretical and practical foundations required for safe, scalable, and aligned decision-making in high-stakes real-world applications.

Chapter 1
Policy Optimization for Average-Reward Criterion

Abstract

.
Reinforcement Learning (RL) for constrained MDPs (CMDPs) is an increasingly important problem for various applications. Often, the average criterion is more suitable than the discounted criterion. Yet, RL for average-CMDPs (ACMDPs) remains a challenging problem. Algorithms designed for discounted constrained RL problems often do not perform well for the average CMDP setting. In this chapter, we introduce a new policy optimization with function approximation algorithm for constrained MDPs with the average criterion. The Average-Constrained Policy Optimization (ACPO) algorithm is inspired by trust region-based policy optimization algorithms. We develop basic sensitivity theory for average CMDPs, and then use the corresponding bounds in the design of the algorithm. We provide theoretical guarantees on its performance, and through extensive experimental work in various challenging OpenAI Gym environments, show its superior empirical performance when compared to other state-of-the-art algorithms adapted for the ACMDPs.

1.1
Introduction

Over the last decade, we have seen an enormous impact of RL techniques on a variety of problems, from mastering complex games like Go
[
189
]
and StarCraft
[
212
]
to robotic control
[
117
,
14
,
19
]
. Many of these have used RL-policy optimization algorithms such as
Schulman et al., 2017
for discounted MDPs (DMDPs). These have come in handy even in generative AI, e.g., training large language models (LLMs)
[
3
]
. However, applications often need satisfaction of some
constraints
, e.g., physical safety of mobile robots
[
94
]
, safe language, image or multi-modal output generation. Furthermore, the
average criterion
when long-term rewards and safety are of consideration is more suitable. Using discounted cost formulations (as a proxy for safety) incentivizes policy optimization algorithms to search for policies that are short-term safe but not long-term because of future-discounting.

The planning problem for MDPs with constraints is often formulated as a Constrained MDP (CMDP) model
[
134
,
92
,
16
]
. Unfortunately, CMDP models do not satisfy Bellman’s principle of optimality, and hence dynamic programming (DP)-style algorithms cannot be developed for the setting. Instead, an alternative approach called the convex analytic approach
[
35
,
16
]
is used by way of introducing occupation measures that leads to optimization formulations. This can be done for both discounted (DCMDPs) and average-criterion (ACMDPs) constrained MDPs.

Theory and algorithms for RL deal with settings when the MDP model is unknown. While DP-inspired RL algorithms such as DQN, when combined with deep learning architectures for function approximation work remarkably effectively
[
145
]
, policy optimization algorithms such as TRPO
[
183
]
, PPO
[
185
]
have proven even more effective in solving high dimensional problems. Since the discounted criterion is sometimes not suitable, policy optimization algorithms such as ATRPO
[
247
,
217
,
124
]
have been developed for AMDPs. Furthermore, as already mentioned, certain RL applications have multiple objectives, one of which is to be optimized and the rest constrained. Thus, the Constrained Policy Optimization (CPO) algorithm
[
4
]
was introduced for infinite-horizon DCMDP problems. Unfortunately, as motivated above, not all such applications fit the discounted-criterion formulation: there are settings, for example where there may be safety requirements when the average-CMDP model is a better fit. No scalable RL algorithms are currently available for such settings.

We note that the RL problem is usually harder than the corresponding planning problem; average-MDPs are more challenging than discounted MDPs; and constrained MDPs are more challenging than unconstrained ones. In this chapter, we present the first practical algorithm for policy optimization-based RL algorithm for average-constrained MDPs. We propose ACPO, a policy optimization algorithm for an average-CMDP with deep learning for function approximation. Our approach is motivated by theoretical guarantees that bound the difference between the average long-run rewards or costs of different policies. It draws inspiration from CPO
[
4
]
(see also
Tessler et al., 2019
for DCMDPs), which uses a policy improvement theorem for the discounted setting based on the trust region methods of
Schulman et al., 2015
. Unfortunately, this result trivializes for the average setting and hence can’t be used. Instead, we derive a new bound that depends on the worst-case level of “mixture” of the irreducible Markov chain associated with a policy. Our proposed algorithm, ACPO is based on these theoretical developments. For experimental evaluation, we use several OpenAI Gym environments from
Todorov et al., 2012
, train large neural network policies and demonstrate the effectiveness and superior performance of the ACPO algorithm as compared to others.

Main Contributions and Novelty.

Algorithmic:
We introduce the first practical policy optimization-based RL algorithm for average-constrained MDPs with new and tight performance bounds and violation guarantees. The algorithm draws inspiration from CPO (for discounted-CMDPs) and ATRPO (for average-MDPs) but is not a straightforward extension of either. One may posit that setting the discount factor
\gamma=1
in CPO for the discounted setting may suffice but that does not perform well on average-CMDPs even with a large discount factor. Further, constraint violation and policy degradation bounds of CPO do not hold in the average setting and hence we develop novel bounds (in Corollary
1.3.5
). In fact, the advantage function estimation routine in our algorithm (line 4 and 6 in Algorithm
1
) is also different from that in CPO, since the discounted-setting procedure cannot be used for the average setting (see Appendix
1.6.3
): We first approximate the average-reward bias and then use a one-step TD backup to estimate the action-bias function. Furthermore, policy optimization algorithms for the average case
[
249
,
217
,
124
]
cannot incorporate constraints. We enable this by introducing sublevel sets of cost constraints. We also introduce an approximate but novel line search procedure that improves the empirical performance of our algorithm, an idea that may help improve performance of other policy optimization algorithms such as PPO.

Technical:
Since ACPO is a trust region method, one can expect some overlap in analysis techniques with other similar algorithms. Nevertheless, our analysis has several novel elements: Lemma
1.3.3
, where we use eigenvalues of the transition matrix to relate total variation of stationary distributions with that of the policies, and in Lemma
1.6.8
, we use the sublevel sets of constraints and projection inequality of Bregman divergence. Furthermore, several important results from CPO and ATRPO papers cannot be applied to the analysis of our algorithm.

Empirical:
We evaluate the empirical performance of ACPO in the OpenAI Gym (Mujoco) environments, a standard benchmark. We find that ACPO outperforms all state-of-the-art Deep RL algorithms such as CPO in
[
4
]
, PCPO in
[
236
]
, PPO in
[
185
]
, BVF-PPO in
[
181
]
and ATRPO in
[
247
]
. We use a large discount factor if the algorithm is not for the average setting, and a Lagrangian objective if it is not for the constrained setting, and in some cases both.

Significance:
ACPO is the first practical trust region-style policy optimization algorithm for ACMDPs with excellent empirical performance. ACMDPs are important models because they allow incorporation of long term safety constraints, which are important not only in the context of safe robot learning and control, but also safety-constrained RLHF fine-tuning and inference for LLMs
[
147
]
and Diffusion models as well. In the absence of suitable policy optimization algorithms for ACMDPs, researchers have resorted to using adaptations of PPO, etc.

Related Work.

Learning constraint-satisfaction policies has been explored in the Deep RL literature in
[
10
,
75
]
. This can either be (1) through expert annotations and demonstrations as in
[
166
,
74
]
or, (2) by exploration with constraint satisfaction as in
[
4
,
202
]
. While the former approach is not scalable since it requires human interventions, current state-of-the-art algorithms for the latter are not applicable to the average reward setting.

Previous work on RL with the average reward criterion has mostly attempted to extend stochastic approximation schemes for the tabular setting, such as Q-learning in
[
1
,
217
]
, to the non-tabular setting with function approximation in
[
222
,
249
]
.
[
44
]
deals with online learning in a constrained MDP setting, but their aim is regret minimization or exploration, both in tabular settings.
We are inspired by the work of
[
247
]
to develop techniques required to derive the policy degradation and constraint violation bounds in Section
1.3
.

The more recent works of
[
30
]
and
[
42
]
also fail to address our problem setting as
the former test on a 2x4 queueing network with maximum state space of 128, while the latter test on a grid of size 10x10 (maximum states of 100). In addition to that, the way they incorporate constraints during training is just via a Lagrangian formulation. In our chapter we show that simply doing this (in the case of PPO and ATRPO for example) leads to much inferior performance to ACPO, which can outperform current state-of-the-art algorithms in state spaces of upto
10^{96}
.

1.2
Preliminaries

A Markov decision process (MDP) is a tuple, (
S,A,r,P,\mu
), where
S
is the set of states,
A
is the set of actions,
r:S\times A\times S\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the reward function,
P:S\times A\times S\to[0,1]
is the transition probability function such that
P(s^{\prime}|s,a)
is the probability of transitioning to state
s^{\prime}
from state
s
by taking action
a
, and
\mu:S\to[0,1]
is the initial state distribution. A stationary policy
\pi:S\to\Delta(A)
is a mapping from states to probability distributions over the actions, with
\pi(a|s)
denoting the probability of selecting action
a
in state
s
, and
\Delta(A)
is the probability simplex over the action space
A
. We denote the set of all stationary policies by
\Pi
. For the average setting, we will make the standard assumption that the MDP is
ergodic
and is
unichain
.

In reinforcement learning, we aim to select a policy
\pi
which maximizes a performance measure,
J(\pi)
, which, for continuous control tasks is either the discounted reward criterion or the average reward approach. Below, we briefly discuss both formulations.

1.2.1
Discounted criterion

For a given discount factor
\gamma\in(0,1)
, the discounted reward objective is defined as

\displaystyle J_{\gamma}(\pi)

\displaystyle:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\right]=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi,\gamma}\\
a\sim\pi\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[r(s,a,s^{\prime})]

where
\tau
refers to a sample trajectory of
(s_{0},a_{0},s_{1},\cdots)
generated when following a policy
\pi
, that is,
a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|s_{t})
and
s_{t+1}\sim P(\cdot|s_{t},a_{t})\,
;
d_{\pi,\gamma}
is the
discounted occupation measure
that is defined by
d_{\pi,\gamma}(s)=(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\underset{\tau\sim\pi}{P}(s_{t}=s)
, which essentially refers to the discounted fraction of time spent in state
s
while following policy
\pi
.

1.2.2
Average criterion

The average-reward objective is given by:

\begin{split}J(\pi)&:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{N-1}r(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\right]=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi(\cdot|s)\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[r(s,a,s^{\prime})],\end{split}

(1.1)

where
d_{\pi}(s):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=0}^{N-1}P_{\tau\sim\pi}(s_{t}=s)
is the
stationary state distribution
under policy
\pi
. The limits in
J(\pi)
and
d_{\pi}(s)
are guaranteed to exist under our ergodic assumption. Since the MDP is aperiodic, it can also be shown that
d_{\pi}(s)=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\tau\sim\pi}(s_{t}=s)
. Since we have
\lim_{\gamma\to 1}d_{\pi,\gamma}(s)\to d_{\pi}(s),\forall s
, it can be shown that
\lim_{\gamma\to 1}(1-\gamma)J_{\gamma}(\pi)=J(\pi)
.

In the average setting, we seek to keep the estimate of the state value function unbiased and hence, introduce the
average-reward bias function
as

\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s):=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}(r(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})-J(\pi))\;\bigg|\;s_{0}=s\right]

and the
average-reward action-bias function
as

\displaystyle\widebar{Q}^{\pi}(s,a):=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}(r(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})-

\displaystyle J(\pi))\;\bigg|\;s_{0}=s,a_{0}=a\bigg].

Finally, define the
average-reward advantage function
as
\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a):=\widebar{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)-\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s)
.

1.2.3
Constrained MDPs

A constrained Markov decision process (CMDP) is an MDP augmented with constraints that restrict the set of allowable policies for that MDP. Specifically, we augment the MDP with a set
C
of auxiliary cost functions,
C_{1},\cdots,C_{m}
(with each function
C_{i}:S\times A\times S\to{\mathbb{R}}
mapping transition tuples to costs, just like the reward function), and bounds
l_{1},\cdots,l_{m}
. Similar to the value functions being defined for the average reward criterion, we define the average cost objective with respect to the cost function
C_{i}
as

\begin{split}J_{C_{i}}(\pi)&:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{N-1}C_{i}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\right]=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[C_{i}(s,a,s^{\prime})].\end{split}

(1.2)

where
J_{C_{i}}
will be referred to as the
average cost
for constraint
C_{i}
. The set of feasible stationary policies for a CMDP then is given by

\Pi_{C}:=\left\{\pi\in\Pi\;:\;J_{C_{i}}(\pi)\leq l_{i},{\;\;\forall\;}i\in\{1,\cdots,M\}\right\}
.
The goal is to find a policy
\pi^{\star}
such that

\pi^{\star}\in\arg\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{C}}J(\pi).

However, finding an exact
\pi^{\star}
is infeasible for large-scale problems.
Instead, we aim to derive an iterative policy improvement algorithm that given a current policy, improves upon it by approximately maximizing the increase in the reward, while not violating the constraints by too much and not being too different from the current policy.

Lastly, analogous to
\widebar{V}^{\pi}
,
\widebar{Q}^{\pi}
, and
\widebar{A}^{\pi}
, we define similar quantities for the cost functions
C_{i}(\cdot)
, and denote them by
\widebar{V}_{C_{i}}^{\pi}
,
\widebar{Q}_{C_{i}}^{\pi}
, and
\widebar{A}_{C_{i}}^{\pi}
.

1.2.4
Policy Improvement for discounted CMDPs

In many on-policy constrained RL problems, we improve policies iteratively by maximizing a predefined function within a local region of the current best policy as in
[
202
,
4
,
236
,
191
]
.

[
4
]
derived a policy improvement bound for the discounted CMDP setting as:

\displaystyle J_{\gamma}(\pi_{k+1})-J_{\gamma}(\pi_{k})\geq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d^{\pi_{k}}\\
a\sim\pi_{k+1}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[A_{\gamma}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a)-\frac{2\gamma\epsilon^{\pi_{k+1}}}{1-\gamma}D_{TV}(\pi_{k+1}||\pi_{k})[s]\right],

(1.3)

where
A_{\gamma}^{\pi_{k}}
is the discounted version of the advantage function,
\epsilon^{\pi_{k+1}}:=\max_{s}|{\mathbb{E}}_{a\sim\pi_{k+1}}[A_{\gamma}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a)]|
, and
D_{TV}(\pi_{k+1}||\pi_{k})[s]=(1/2)\sum_{a}\left|\pi_{k+1}(a|s)-\pi_{k}(a|s)\right|
is the total variational divergence between
\pi_{k+1}
and
\pi_{k}
at
s
. These results laid the foundations for on-policy constrained RL algorithms as in
[
229
,
215
]
.

However, Equation (
1.3
) does not generalize to the average setting (
\gamma\to 1
) (see Appendix
1.6.1
). In the next section, we will derive a policy improvement bound for the average case and present an algorithm based on trust region methods, which will generate almost-monotonically improving iterative policies. Proofs of theorems and lemmas, if not already given, are available in Appendix
1.6
.

1.3
ACPO: The Average-Constrained Policy Optimization Algorithm

In this section, we present the main results of our work. For conciseness, we denote by
d_{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|}
the column vector whose components are
d_{\pi}(s)
and
P_{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|\times|S|}
to be the state transition probability matrix under policy
\pi
.

1.3.1
Policy Improvement for the Average-CMDP

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be the policy obtained via some update rule from the current policy
\pi
. Analogous to the discounted setting of a CMDP, we would like to characterize the performance difference
J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)
by an expression which depends on
\pi
and some divergence metric between the two policies.

Lemma 1.3.1
.

[
249
]

Under the unichain assumption of the underlying Markov chain, for any stochastic policies
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
:

J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right].

(1.4)

Note that this difference depends on the stationary state distribution obtained from the
new
policy,
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
. This is computationally impractical as we do not have access to this
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
. Fortunately, by use of the following lemma we can show that if
d_{\pi}
and
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
are “close” with respect to some metric, we can approximate Eq. (
1.4
) using samples from
d_{\pi}
.

Lemma 1.3.2
.

Under the unichain assumption, for any stochastic policies
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
we have:

\begin{split}\left|J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\right|\leq 2\epsilon D_{\text{TV}}(d_{\pi^{\prime}}\parallel d_{\pi})\end{split}

(1.5)

, where
\epsilon=\max_{s}\big|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]\big|
.
See Appendix
1.6.4
for proof. Lemma
1.3.2
implies
J(\pi^{\prime})\approx J(\pi)+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]
when
d_{\pi}
and
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
are “close”. Now that we have established this approximation, we need to study the relation of how the actual change in policies affects their corresponding stationary state distributions. For this, we turn to standard analysis of the underlying Markov chain of the CMDP.

Under the ergodic assumption, we have that
P_{\pi}
is irreducible and hence its eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{\pi,i}\}_{i=1}^{|S|}
are such that
\lambda_{\pi,1}=1
and
\lambda_{\pi,i\neq 1}<1
. For our analysis, we define
\sigma^{\pi}=\max_{i\neq 1}\,(1-\lambda_{\pi,i})^{-1/2}
, and from
[
116
]
and
[
65
]
, we connect
\{\lambda_{\pi,i}\}_{i=1}^{|S|}
to the sensitivity of the stationary distributions to changes in the policy using the result below.

Lemma 1.3.3
.

Under the ergodic assumption, the divergence between the stationary distributions
d_{\pi}
and
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
is upper bounded as:

D_{\text{TV}}(d_{\pi^{\prime}}\parallel d_{\pi})\leq\sigma^{\star}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]],

(1.6)

, where
\sigma^{\star}=\max_{\pi}\sigma^{\pi}
. See Appendix
1.6.5
for proof. This bound is tighter and easier to compute than the one given by
[
247
]
, which replaces
\sigma^{\star}
by
\kappa^{\star}=\max_{\pi}\kappa^{\pi}
, where
\kappa^{\pi}
is known as
Kemeny’s constant
from
[
108
]
. It is interpreted as the expected number of steps to get to any goal state, where the expectation is taken with respect to the stationary-distribution of those states.

Combining the bounds in Lemma
1.3.2
and Lemma
1.3.3
gives us the following result:

Proposition 1.3.4
.

Under the ergodic assumption, the following bounds hold for any stochastic policies
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
:

L_{\pi}^{-}(\pi^{\prime})\leq J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)\leq L_{\pi}^{+}(\pi^{\prime})

(1.7)

where

\displaystyle L_{\pi}^{\pm}(\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\pm 2\nu\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]]

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nu

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\star}\max_{s}\big|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]\big|.

It is interesting to compare the inequalities of Equation (
1.7
) to Equation (
1.4
). The term
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]
in Prop.
1.3.4
is somewhat of a
surrogate
approximation to
J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)
, in the sense that it uses
d_{\pi}
instead of
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
. As discussed before, we do not have access to
d_{\pi^{\prime}}
since the trajectories of the new policy are not available unless the policy itself is updated. This surrogate is a first order approximation to
J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)
in the parameters of
\pi^{\prime}
in a neighborhood around
\pi
as in
[
105
]
. Hence, Eq. (
1.7
) can be viewed as bounding the worst-case approximation error.

Extending this discussion to the cost function of our CMDP, similar expressions follow immediately.

Corollary 1.3.5
.

For any policies
\pi^{\prime},\pi
, and any cost function
C_{i}
, the following bound holds:

M_{\pi}^{-}(\pi^{\prime})\leq J_{C_{i}}(\pi^{\prime})-J_{C_{i}}(\pi)\leq M_{\pi}^{+}(\pi^{\prime})

(1.8)

where

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{\pm}(\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}_{C_{i}}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\pm 2\nu_{C_{i}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]]

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nu_{C_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\star}\max_{s}\big|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}_{C_{i}}^{\pi}(s,a)]\big|.

Until now, we have been dealing with bounds given with regards to the TV divergence of the policies. However, in practice, bounds with respect to the KL divergence of policies is more commonly used as in
[
183
,
184
,
132
]
.
From Pinsker’s and Jensen’s inequalities, we have that

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi}][s]\big]/2}.

(1.9)

We can thus use Eq. (
1.9
) in the bounds of Proposition
1.3.4
and Corollary
1.3.5
to make policy improvement guarantees, i.e., if we find updates such that
\pi_{k+1}\in\argmax_{\pi}L_{\pi_{k}}^{-}(\pi)
, then we will have monotonically increasing policies as, at iteration
k
,
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}},a\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a)]=0
,
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\mathsf{KL}{\pi}{\pi_{k}}[s]]=0
for
\pi=\pi_{k}
, implying that
J(\pi_{k+1})-J(\pi_{k})\geq 0
. However, this sequence does not guarantee constraint satisfaction at each iteration, so we now turn to trust region methods to incorporate constraints, do policy improvement and provide safety guarantees.

1.3.2
Trust Region Based Approach

For large or continuous state and action CMDPs, solving for the exact optimal policy is impractical. However,
trust region
-based policy optimization algorithms have proven to be effective for solving such problems as in
[
183
,
184
,
185
,
2
]
.
For these approaches, we usually consider some parameterized policy class
\Pi_{\Theta}=\{\pi_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
for tractibility. In addition, for CMDPs, we also require the policy iterates to be feasible, so instead of optimizing just over
\Pi_{\Theta}
, we optimize over
\Pi_{\Theta}\cap\Pi_{C}
. However, it is much easier to solve the above problem if we introduce hard constraints, rather than limiting the set to
\Pi_{\Theta}\cap\Pi_{C}
. Therefore, we now introduce the ACPO algorithm, which is inspired by the trust region formulations above as the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\pi\in\Pi_{\Theta}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\\
a\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)]

(1.10)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{\theta_{k}})+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\\
a\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}_{C_{i}}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)]\leq l_{i},\;\;\;{\;\;\forall\;}i

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{\text{KL}}(\pi\parallel\pi_{\theta_{k}})\leq\delta

where
\overline{D}_{\text{KL}}(\pi\parallel\pi_{\theta_{k}}):=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\mathsf{KL}{\pi}{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}[s]]
,
\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)
is the average advantage function defined earlier, and
\delta>0
is a step size. We use this form of updates as it is an approximation to the lower bound given in Proposition
1.3.4
and the upper bound given in Corollary
1.3.5
.

In most cases, the trust region threshold for formulations like Eq. (
1.10
) are heuristically motivated. We now show that it is quantitatively motivated and comes with a worst case performance degradation and constraint violation. Proof is in Appendix
1.6.10
.

Theorem 1.3.6
.

Let
\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}
be the optimal solution to Eq. (
1.10
) for some
\pi_{\theta_{k}}\in\Pi_{\Theta}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle J(\pi_{\theta_{k+1}})-J(\pi_{\theta_{k}})\geq

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2(\delta+V_{max})}\nu^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}

(1.11)

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\;J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{\theta_{k+1}})\leq l_{i}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2(\delta+V_{max})}\nu^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}_{C_{i}}{\;\;\forall\;}i,

(1.12)

where
\nu^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}=\sigma^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}\max_{s}\big|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)]\big|
,
\nu^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}_{C_{i}}=\sigma^{\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}}\max_{i,s}\big|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi_{\theta_{k+1}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}_{C_{i}}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)]\big|
,
V_{max}=\max_{i}\beta_{i}^{2}
,
and

\beta_{i}=[J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{\theta_{k}})-l_{i}]_{+}
.

Remark 3.7.
Note that if the constraints are ignored (by setting
V_{max}=0
), then this bound is tighter than given in
[
247
]
for the unconstrained average-reward setting.

However, the update rule of Eq. (
1.10
) is difficult to implement in practice as it takes steps that are too small, which degrades convergence. In addition, it requires the exact knowledge of
\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)
which is computationally infeasible for large-scale problems. In the next section, we will introduce a specific sampling-based practical algorithm to alleviate these concerns.

1.4
Practical Implementation of ACPO

In this section, we introduce a practical version of the ACPO algorithm with a principle recovery method. With a small step size
\delta
, we can approximate the reward function and constraints with a first order expansion, and approximate the KL divergence constraint with a second order expansion. This gives us a new optimization problem which can be solved exactly using Lagrangian duality.

1.4.1
An Implementation of ACPO

Since we are working with a parameterized class, we shall now overload notation to use
\theta_{k}
as the policy at iteration
k
, i.e.,
\theta_{k}\equiv\pi_{\theta_{k}}
. In addition, we use
g
to denote the gradient of the advantage function objective,
a_{i}
to denote the gradient of the advantage function of the cost
C_{i}
,
H
as the Hessian of the KL-divergence. Formally,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{\theta}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\theta_{k}}\\
a\sim\theta\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\theta_{k}}(s,a)]\Bigr|_{\theta=\theta_{k}},

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{\theta}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\theta_{k}}\\
a\sim\theta\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\theta_{k}}_{C_{i}}(s,a)]\Bigr|_{\theta=\theta_{k}},

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{2}_{\theta}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\theta_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\theta}{\theta_{k}})[s]\big]\Bigr|_{\theta=\theta_{k}}.

In addition, let
c_{i}:=J_{C_{i}}(\theta_{k})-l_{i}
. The approximation to the problem in Eq. (
1.10
) is:

\displaystyle\max_{\theta}

\displaystyle g^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})

(1.13)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle c_{i}+a_{i}^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})\leq 0,{\;\;\forall\;}i

\displaystyle\text{and,}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{k})^{T}H(\theta-\theta_{k})\leq\delta.

This is a convex optimization problem in which strong duality holds, and hence it can be solved using a Lagrangian method. The update rule for the dual problem then takes the form

\theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{\star}}H^{-1}\left(g-A\mu^{\star}\right).

(1.14)

where
\lambda^{\star}
and
\mu^{\star}
are the Lagrange multipliers satisfying the dual

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\geq 0\\
\mu\succeq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{-1}{2\lambda}\left(g^{T}H^{-1}g-2r^{T}\mu+\mu^{T}S\mu\right)+\mu^{T}c-\frac{\lambda\delta}{2},

(1.15)

with
r:=g^{T}H^{-1}A
,
S:=A^{T}H^{-1}A
,
A:=[a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}]
, and
c:=[c_{1},\cdots,c_{m}]^{T}
.

1.4.2
Feasibility and Recovery

The approximation regime described in Eq. (
1.13
) requires
H
to be invertible. For large parametric policies,
H
is computed using the conjugate gradient method as in
[
183
]
. However, in practice, using this approximation along with the associated statistical sampling errors, there might be potential violations of the approximate constraints leading to infeasible policies.

To rectify this, for the case where we only have one constraint, one can recover a feasible policy by applying a recovery step inspired by the TRPO update on the cost surrogate as:

\displaystyle\theta_{k+1/2}=\theta_{k}-\sqrt{2\delta}\bigg[t\cdot\frac{H^{-1}a}{\sqrt{a^{T}H^{-1}a}}+(1-t)\cdot\frac{H^{-1}g}{\sqrt{g^{T}H^{-1}g}}\bigg]

(1.16)

where
t\in[0,1]
.
Contrasting with the policy recovery update of
[
4
]
which only uses the cost advantage function gradient
a
, we introduce the reward advantage function gradient
g
as well. This choice is to ensure recovery while simultaneously balancing the “regret” of not choosing the best (in terms of the objective value) policy
\pi_{k}
. In other words, we wish to find a policy
\pi_{k+1/2}
as close to
\pi_{k}
in terms of their objective function values. We follow up this step with a simple linesearch to find feasible
\pi_{k+1}
. Based on this, Algorithm
1
provides a basic outline of ACPO. For more details of the algorithm, see Appendix
1.6.3
.

Algorithm 1

Average-Constrained Policy Optimization (ACPO)

1:

Input:
Initial random policy
\pi_{0}\in\Pi_{\theta}

2:

for

k=0,1,2,...,K

do

3:

Sample a set of trajectories
\Omega
using
\pi_{k}=\pi_{\theta_{k}}

4:

Find estimates of
g,a,H,c
using
\Omega

5:

if
a feasible solution to Equation (
1.13
) exists
then

6:

Solve dual problem in Equation (
1.15
) for
\lambda^{\star}_{k},\mu^{\star}_{k}

7:

Find policy update
\pi_{k+1}
with Equation (
1.14
)

8:

else

9:

Find recovery policy
\pi_{k+1/2}
with Equation (
1.16
)

10:

Obtain
\pi_{k+1}
by linesearch till approximate constraint satisfaction of Equation (
1.13
)

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

Average Rewards:

Average Constraint values:

(a)
Ant Gather

(b)
Bottleneck

(c)
Grid

Figure 1.1:
The average reward and constraint cost function values vs iterations (in
10^{4}
) learning curves for some algorithm-task pairs. Solid lines in each figure are the empirical means, while the shaded area represents 1 standard deviation, all over 5 runs. The dashed line in constraint plots is the constraint threshold
l
. ATRPO and PPO are tested with constraints, which are included in their Lagrangian formulation. Additional results are available in Appendix
1.6.6
.

1.5
Empirical Results

We conducted a series of experiments to evaluate the relative performance of the ACPO algorithm and answer the following questions:
(i) Does ACPO learn a sequence of constraint satisfying policies while maximizing the average reward in the long run?
(ii) How does ACPO compare with the already existing constraint policy optimization algorithms which are applied with a large discount factor?
(iii) What are the factors that affect the performance of ACPO?

We work with the OpenAI Gym environments to train the various learning agent on the following tasks -
Gather
,
Circle
,
Grid
, and
Bottleneck
tasks (see Figure
1.3
in Appendix
1.6.6
for more details on the environments). For our experiments we only work with a single constraint with policy recovery using Eq. (
1.16
) (this is only a computational limitation; ACPO in principle can handle multiple constraints). We compare ACPO with the following baseline algorithms: CPO by
[
4
]
, ATRPO by
[
247
]
, PCPO by
[
236
]
(a close variant of CPO), BVF-PPO by
[
181
]
and PPO by
[
185
]
.

Although ATRPO and PPO originally do not incorporate constraints, for fair comparison, we introduce constraints using a Lagrangian. Also, CPO, PCPO and PPO are compared with
\gamma=0.999
. See Appendix
1.6.5
for more details.

1.5.1
Evaluation Details and Protocol

For the Gather and Circle tasks we test two distinct agents: a point-mass (
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{9},A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
), and an ant robot (
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{32},A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
). The agent in the Bottleneck task in
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{71},A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{16}
, and for the Grid task is
S\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{96},A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
. We use two hidden layer neural networks to represent Gaussian policies for the tasks. For Gather and Circle, size is (64,32) for both layers, and for Grid and Bottleneck the layer sizes are (16,16) and (50,25). We set the step size
\delta
to
10^{-4}
, and for each task, we conduct 5 runs to get the mean and standard deviation for reward objective and cost constraint values during training. We train CPO, PCPO, and PPO with the discounted objective, however, evaluation and comparison with BVF-PPO, ATRPO and ACPO is done using the average reward objective (this is a standard evaluation scheme as in
[
183
,
229
,
215
]
).

For each environment, we train an agent for
10^{5}
steps, and for every
10^{3}
steps, we instantiate 10 evaluation trajectories with the current (deterministic) policy. For each of these trajectories, we calculate the trajectory average reward for the next
10^{3}
steps and finally report the total average-reward as the mean of these 10 trajectories. Learning curves for the algorithms are compiled in Figure
1.1
(for Point-Circle, Point-Gather, and Ant-Circle see Appendix
1.6.6
).

Since there are two objectives (rewards in the objective and costs in the constraints), we show the plots which maximize the reward objective while satisfying the cost constraint. See Appendix
1.6.4
and
1.6.5
for more details.

1.5.2
Performance Analysis

From Figure
1.1
, we can see that ACPO is able to improve the reward objective while having approximate constraint satisfaction on all tasks. In particular, ACPO is the only algorithm that best learns almost-constraint-satisfying maximum average-reward policies across all tasks: in a simple Gather environment, ACPO is able to almost exactly track the cost constraint values to within the given threshold
l
; however, for the high dimensional Grid and Bottleneck environments we have more constraint violations due to complexity of the policy behavior. Regardless, in these environments, ACPO still outperforms all other baselines.

ACPO vs. CPO/PCPO.
For the Point-Gather environment (see Figure
1.4
), we see that initially ACPO and CPO/PCPO give relatively similar performance, but eventually ACPO improves over CPO and PCPO by 52.5% and 36.1% on average-rewards respectively. This superior performance does not come with more constraint violation. The Ant-Gather environment particularly brings out the effectiveness of ACPO where it shows 41.1% and 61.5% improvement over CPO and PCPO respectively, while satisfying the constraint. In the high dimensional Bottleneck and Grid environments, ACPO is particularly quick at optimizing for low constraint violations, while improving over PCPO and CPO in terms of average-reward.

ACPO vs Lagrangian ATRPO/PPO.
One could suppose to use the state of the art unconstrained policy optimization algorithms with a Lagrangian formulation to solve the average-rewards CMDP problem in consideration, but we see that such an approach, although principled in theory, does not give satisfactory empirical results. This can be particularly seen in the Ant-Circle, Ant-Gather, Bottleneck, and Grid environments, where Lagrangian ATRPO and PPO give the least rewards, while not even satisfying the constraints. If ATRPO and PPO were used with constraints ignored, one would see higher rewards but even worse constraint violations, which are not useful.

ACPO vs BVF-PPO.
BVF-PPO is a whole different formulation than the other baselines, as it translates the cumulative cost constraints into state-based constraints, which results in an almost-safe policy improvement method which maximizes returns at every step. However, we see that this approach fails to satisfy the constraints even in the moderately difficult Ant Gather environment, let alone the high dimensional Bottleneck and Grid environments.

1.5.3
Dependence of the Recovery Regime

(a)
Rewards

(b)
Costs

Figure 1.2:
Comparison of performance of ACPO with different values of the hyperparameter
t
in the Point-Circle environment. X-axis is iterations in
10^{4}
. See Appendix
1.6.6
for more details.

In Equation (
1.16
) we introduced a hyperparameter
t
, which provides for an intuitive trade-off as follows: either we purely decrease the constraint violations (
t=1
), or we decrease the average-reward (
t=0
), which consequently decreases the constraint violation. The latter formulation is principled in that if we decrease rewards, we are bound to decrease constraints violation due to the nature of the environments. Figure
1.2
shows the experiments we conducted with varying
t
. With
t=1
, we obtain the same recovery scheme as that of
[
4
]
. Our results show that this scheme does not lead to the best performance, and that
t=0.75
and
t=1
perform the best across all tasks. See Appendix
1.6.6
for a detailed study.

1.6
Appendix

1.6.1
Proofs

Lemma 1.6.1

(Trivialization of Discounted Criterion Bounds)
.

Consider the policy performance bound of
[
4
]
, which says that for any two stationary policies
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
:

J_{\gamma}(\pi^{\prime})-J_{\gamma}(\pi)\geq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left[\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi,\gamma}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{\gamma}^{\pi}(s,a)]-\frac{2\gamma\epsilon^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi,\gamma}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]\right]

(1.17)

where
\epsilon^{\gamma}=\max_{s}\left|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{\gamma}^{\pi}(s,a)]\right|
. Then, the right hand side times
1-\gamma
goes to negative infinity as
\gamma\to 1
.

Proof 1.6.2
.

Since
d_{\pi,\gamma}
approaches the stationary distribution
d_{\pi}
as
\gamma\to 1
, we can multiply the right hand side of (
1.17
) by
(1-\gamma)
and take the limit which gives us:

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 1}\left(\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi,\gamma}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{\gamma}^{\pi}(s,a)]\pm\frac{2\gamma\epsilon^{\gamma}}{1-\gamma}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi,\gamma}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]-2\epsilon\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]\big]\cdot\lim_{\gamma\to 1}\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\infty

Here
\epsilon=\max_{s}\left|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)]\right|
. The first equality is a standard result of
\lim_{\gamma\to 1}A_{\gamma}^{\pi}(s,a)=\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)
.

See
1.3.1

Proof 1.6.3
.

We directly expand the right-hand side using the definition of the advantage function and Bellman equation, which gives us:

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)-\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[r(s,a,s^{\prime})-J(\pi)+\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})-\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)+\underbrace{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})]-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s)]}_{A}

Analyzing
A
, since
d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s)
is a stationary distribution:

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\\
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s)\sum_{a}\pi^{\prime}(a|s)\sum_{s^{\prime}}P(s^{\prime}|s,a)\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s)\sum_{s^{\prime}}P_{\pi^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s)\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime}}d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})

Therefore,
A=\sum_{s^{\prime}}d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{V}^{\pi}(s)]=0
. Hence, proved.

See
1.3.2

Proof 1.6.4
.

\displaystyle\left|J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi^{\prime}}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\\
a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\right|

(from Lemma
1.3.1
)

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{s}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\left(d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s)-d_{\pi}(s)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}\left|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\left(d_{\pi^{\prime}}(s)-d_{\pi}(s)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s}\left|\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}a\sim\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{A}^{\pi}(s,a)\right]\right|\left\|d_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi}\right\|_{1}

(Holder’s inequality)

\displaystyle=2\epsilon D_{\text{TV}}(d_{\pi^{\prime}}\parallel d_{\pi})

See
1.3.3

Proof 1.6.5
.

This proof takes ideas from Markov chain perturbation theory in
[
48
,
97
,
249
]
. Firstly we state a standard result with
P_{\pi}^{\star}=\textbf{1}d_{\pi}^{T}

(d_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi})^{T}(I-P_{\pi^{\prime}}+P_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\star})=d_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}-d_{\pi}^{T}-d_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}+d_{\pi}^{T}P_{\pi^{\prime}}=d_{\pi}^{T}P_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi}^{T}=d_{\pi}^{T}(P_{\pi^{\prime}}-P_{\pi}).

Denoting the fundamental matrix of the Markov chain
Z^{\pi^{\prime}}=(I-P_{\pi^{\prime}}+P_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\star})^{-1}
and the mean first passage time matrix
M^{\pi^{\prime}}=(I-Z^{\pi^{\prime}}+EZ^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\text{dg}})D^{\pi^{\prime}}
, and right multiplying the above by
(Z^{\pi^{\prime}})^{-1}
we have,

\displaystyle d_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}-d_{\pi}^{T}=d_{\pi}^{T}(P_{\pi^{\prime}}-P_{\pi})(I-M^{\pi^{\prime}}(D^{\pi^{\prime}})^{-1})

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;d_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi}=(I-M^{\pi^{\prime}}(D^{\pi^{\prime}})^{-1})^{T}(P_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}-P_{\pi}^{T})d_{\pi}

(1.18)

\displaystyle\text{i.e.}\qquad\left\|d_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(I-M^{\pi^{\prime}}(D^{\pi^{\prime}})^{-1})^{T}(P_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}-P_{\pi}^{T})d_{\pi}\right\|_{1}

(submultiplicative property)

\displaystyle\left\|d_{\pi^{\prime}}-d_{\pi}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left\|(I-M^{\pi^{\prime}}(D^{\pi^{\prime}})^{-1})\right\|_{\infty}}_{T_{1}}\underbrace{\left\|(P_{\pi^{\prime}}^{T}-P_{\pi}^{T})d_{\pi}\right\|_{1}}_{T_{2}}

We know that
\kappa^{\pi}=\text{Tr}(Z^{\pi})
and from
[
98
]
, we can write
T_{1}
using the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{\pi,i}\}_{i=1}^{|S|}
of the underlying
P_{\pi}
as

T_{1}\leq\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i=2}^{|S|}\frac{1}{(1-\lambda_{\pi,i})^{1/2}}\leq\max_{i}(1-\lambda_{\pi,i})^{-1/2}=\sigma^{\pi}\leq\max_{\pi}\sigma^{\pi}=\sigma^{\star}.

For
T_{2}
, we refer to the result by
[
249
]
, and provide the proof for completeness below.

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}}\left|\sum_{s}\left(\sum_{a}P(s^{\prime}|s,a)\pi^{\prime}(a|s)-P(s^{\prime}|s,a)\pi(a|s)\right)d_{\pi}(s)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s^{\prime},s}\left|\sum_{a}P(s^{\prime}|s,a)(\pi^{\prime}(a|s)-\pi(a|s))\right|d_{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,s^{\prime},a}P(s^{\prime}|s,a)\left|\pi^{\prime}(a|s)-\pi(a|s)\right|d_{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s,a}\left|\pi^{\prime}(a|s)-\pi(a|s)\right|d_{\pi}(s)=2\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[D_{\text{TV}}(\pi^{\prime}\parallel\pi)[s]]

Combining these inequalities of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, we get the desired result.

1.6.2
Performance and Constraint Bounds of Trust Region Approach

Consider the trust region formulation in Equation (
1.10
). To prove the policy performance bound when the current policy is infeasible (i.e., constraint-violating), we prove the KL divergence between
\pi_{k}
and
\pi_{k+1}
for the KL divergence projection, along with other lemmas. We then prove our main theorem for the worst-case performance degradation.

Lemma 1.6.6
.

For a closed convex constraint set, if we have a constraint satisfying policy
\pi_{k}
and the KL divergence
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1/2}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]
of the ‘Improve’ step is upper bounded by step size
\delta
, then after KL divergence projection of the ‘Project’ step we have

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\delta.

Proof 1.6.7
.

We make use of the fact that Bregman divergence (hence, KL divergence) projection onto the constraint set (
\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,,d\in{\mathbb{N}}
) exists and is unique. Since
\pi_{k}
is safe, we have
\pi_{k}
in the constraint set, and
\pi_{k+1}
is the projection of
\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
onto the constraint set. Using the projection inequality, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k}}{\pi_{k+1}}[s]\big]+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}}[s]\big]\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k}}{\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}}[s]\big]

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k}}{\pi_{k+1}}[s]\big]\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k}}{\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}}[s]\big]\leq\delta.

(
\mathsf{KL}{\cdot}{\cdot}\geq 0
)

Since KL divergence is asymptotically symmetric when updating the policy within a local neighbourhood (
\delta<<1
), we have

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\delta.

Lemma 1.6.8
.

For a closed convex constraint set, if we have a constraint violating policy
\pi_{k}
and the KL divergence
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1/2}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]
of the first step is upper bounded by step size
\delta
, then after KL divergence projection of the second step we have

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\delta+V_{max},

where
V_{max}=\max_{i}\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}^{2},\,\beta_{i}=[J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{k})-l_{i}]_{+}
,
\alpha_{i}=\frac{1}{2a_{i}^{T}H^{-1}a_{i}},
with

a_{i}
as the gradient of the cost advantage function corresponding to constraint
C_{i}
, and
H
as the Hessian of the KL divergence constraint.
1
1

1

For any
x\in{\mathbb{R}},\,[x]_{+}:=\max(0,x)
.

Proof 1.6.9
.

Let the sublevel set of cost constraint function for the current infeasible policy
\pi_{k}
be given as:

L_{\pi_{k}}=\{\pi~|~J_{C_{i}}(\pi)+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\\
a\sim\pi\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi_{k}}_{C_{i}}(s,a)]\leq J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{k}){\;\;\forall\;}i\}.

This implies that the current policy
\pi_{k}
lies in
L_{\pi_{k}}
. The constraint set onto which
\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
is projected onto is given by:
\{\pi~|~J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{k})+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\\
a\sim\pi\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\widebar{A}^{\pi_{k}}_{C_{i}}(s,a)]\leq l_{i}{\;\;\forall\;}i\}.
Let
\pi_{k+1}^{L}
be the projection of
\pi_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
onto
L_{\pi_{k}}.

Note that the Bregman inequality of Lemma
1.6.6
holds for any convex set in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,,d\in{\mathbb{N}}
. This implies
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}^{L}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\delta
since
\pi_{k}
and
\pi_{k+1}^{L}
are both in
L_{\pi_{k}}
, which is also convex since the constraint functions are convex. Using the Three-point Lemma
2
2

2

For any
\phi
, the Bregman divergence identity:
D_{\phi}(x,y)+D_{\phi}(y,z)=D_{\phi}(x,z)+<\nabla\phi(z)-\nabla\phi(y),x-y>
, for polices
\pi_{k},\pi_{k+1},
and
\pi_{k+1}^{L}
, with
\varphi(\textbf{x}):=\sum_{i}x_{i}\log x_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta\geq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}^{L}}{\pi_{k}})[s]\big]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]

\displaystyle-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k+1}^{L}}[s]\big]

\displaystyle+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[(\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k})-\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k+1}^{L}))^{T}(\pi_{k+1}-\pi_{k+1}^{L})[s]\big]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]

\displaystyle\leq\delta+\underbrace{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k+1}^{L}}[s]\big]}_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[(\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k})-\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k+1}^{L}))^{T}(\pi_{k+1}-\pi_{k+1}^{L})[s]\big]}_{T_{2}}.

(1.19)

If the constraint violations of the current policy
\pi_{k}
are small, i.e.,
J_{C_{i}}(\pi_{k})-l_{i}=b_{i}
is small for all
i
, then
T_{1}
can be approximated by a second order expansion. We analyze
T_{1}
for any constraint
C_{i}
and then bound it over all the constraints. As before we overload the notation with
\pi_{k}=\pi_{\theta_{k}}=\theta_{k}
to write. For any constraint
C_{i}
, we can write
T^{i}_{1}
as the expected KL divergence if projection was onto the constraint set of
C_{i}
i.e.

\displaystyle T^{i}_{1}\approx\frac{1}{2}(\pi_{k+1}-\pi_{k+1}^{L})^{T}H(\pi_{k+1}-\pi_{k+1}^{L})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\beta_{i}}{a_{i}^{T}H^{-1}a_{i}}H^{-1}a_{i}\Big)^{T}H\Big(\frac{\beta_{i}}{a_{i}^{T}H^{-1}a_{i}}H^{-1}a_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{i}^{2}}{2a_{i}^{T}H^{-1}a_{i}}=\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}^{2},

where the second equality is a result of the trust region guarantee (see
[
183
]
for more details). Finally we invoke the projection result from
[
2
]
which uses Dykstra’s Alternating Projection algorithm from
[
203
]
to bound this projection, i.e.,
T_{1}\leq\max_{i}T^{i}_{1}\approx\max_{i}\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}^{2}
.

And since
\delta
is small, we have
\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k})-\nabla\varphi(\pi_{k+1}^{L})\approx 0
given
s
. Thus,
T_{2}\approx 0
. Combining all of the above, we have
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]\leq\delta+V_{max}.

See
1.3.6

Proof 1.6.10
.

Since
\overline{D}_{\text{KL}}(\pi_{\theta_{k}}\parallel\pi_{\theta_{k}})=0
,
\pi_{\theta_{k}}
is feasible. The objective value is 0 for
\pi_{\theta}=\pi_{\theta_{k}}
. The bound follows from Equation (
1.7
) and Equation (
1.9
) where the average KL i.e.
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{k}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\big[\mathsf{KL}{\pi_{k+1}}{\pi_{k}}[s]\big]
is bounded by
\delta+V_{max}
from Lemma
1.6.8
.

Similar to Corollary
1.3.5
, expressions for the auxiliary cost constraints also follow immediately as the second result.

Remark 1.6.11
.

Remark If we look at proof as given by
[
249
]
in Section 5 of their paper, with the distinction now that
\delta
is replaced by
\delta+V_{max}
, we have the same result. Our worse bound is due to the constrained nature of our setting, which is intuitive in the sense that for the sake of satisfying constraints, we undergo a worse worst-case performance degradation.

1.6.3

Approximate ACPO

Policy Recovery Routine

As described in Section
1.4.2
, we need a recovery routine in case the updated policy
\pi_{k+1/2}
is not approximate constraint satisfying. In this case, the optimization problem is inspired from a simple trust region approach by
[
183
]
. Since we only deal with one constraint in the practical implementation of ACPO, the recovery rule is obtained by solving the following problem:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\min_{\theta}&c+a^{T}\left(\theta-\theta_{k}\right)\\
\text{ s.t. }&\frac{1}{2}\left(\theta-\theta_{k}\right)^{T}H\left(\theta-\theta_{k}\right)\leq\delta.\end{array}

Let
x=\theta-\theta_{k}
, then the dual function
L(x,\lambda)
is given by:
L(x,\lambda)=c+a^{T}x+\lambda\left(\frac{1}{2}x^{T}Hx-\delta\right)
. Now,

\frac{L}{\partial x}=a+\lambda(Hx)=0\Longrightarrow x=-\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{-1}a.

x
obtained above should satisfy the trust-region constraint:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{-1}a\right)^{T}H\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}H^{-1}a\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot a^{T}H^{-1}a

\displaystyle\leq\delta

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\sqrt{\frac{a^{T}H^{-1}a}{2\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda.

Therefore, the update rule in case of infeasibility takes the form
\theta=\theta_{k}-\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}{a^{T}H^{-1}a}}H^{-1}a
. We augment this rule with the gradient of the reward advantage function as well, so the final recovery is

\theta_{k+1/2}=\theta_{k}-\sqrt{2\delta}\bigg[t\cdot\frac{H^{-1}a}{\sqrt{a^{T}H^{-1}a}}+(1-t)\cdot\frac{H^{-1}g}{\sqrt{g^{T}H^{-1}g}}\bigg]\quad;\quad t\in[0,1]

Line Search

Because of approximation error, the proposed update may not satisfy the constraints in Eq. (
1.10
). Constraint satisfaction is enforced via line search, so the final update is

\theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}+s^{j}\left(\theta_{k+1/2}-\theta_{k}\right),

where
s\in(0,1)
is the backtracking coefficient and
j\in\{0,...,L\}
is the smallest integer for which
\pi_{k+1}
satisfies the constraints in Equation
1.10
. Here,
L
is a finite backtracking budget; if no proposed policy satisfies the constraints after
L
backtracking steps, no update occurs.

1.6.4

Practical ACPO

As explained in Section
1.4
, we use the below problem formulation, which uses first-order Taylor approximation on the objective and second-order approximation on the KL constraint
3
3

3

The gradient and first-order Taylor approximation of
\overline{D}_{\text{KL}}(\pi_{\theta}\parallel\pi_{\theta_{k}})
at
\theta=\theta_{k}
is zero.
around
\theta_{k}
, given small
\delta
:

\displaystyle\max_{\theta}

\displaystyle g^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})

(1.20)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }

\displaystyle c_{i}+a_{i}^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})\leq 0,{\;\;\forall\;}i\qquad;\qquad\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{k})^{T}H(\theta-\theta_{k})\leq\delta.

where

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\\
a\sim\pi_{\theta_{k}}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)|_{\theta=\theta_{k}}\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)\right]\qquad

\displaystyle;\qquad c_{i}

\displaystyle:=J_{C_{i}}(\theta_{k})-l_{i}{\;\;\forall\;}i

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\\
a\sim\pi_{\theta_{k}}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)|_{\theta=\theta_{k}}\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}_{C_{i}}(s,a)\right]\qquad

\displaystyle;\qquad H

\displaystyle:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim d_{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}\\
a\sim\pi_{\theta_{k}}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)|_{\theta=\theta_{k}}\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)|_{\theta=\theta_{k}}^{T}\right]

Similar to the work of
[
4
]
,
g
,
a_{i}
, and
H
can be approximated using samples drawn from the policy
\pi_{\theta_{k}}
. The Hessian
H
is identical to the Hessian
H
used by
[
4
]
and
[
249
]
. However, the definitons
g
and
a_{i}
’s are different since they include the average reward advantage functions,
\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)=\widebar{Q}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)-\widebar{V}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s)
.

Since rewards and cost advantage functions can be approximated independently, we use the framework of
[
249
]
to do so. We describe the process of estimation of rewards advantage function, and the same procedure can be used for the cost advantage functions as well. Specifically, first approximate the average-reward bias
\widebar{V}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s)
and then use a one-step TD backup to estimate the action-bias function. Concretely, using the average reward Bellman equation gives

\widebar{A}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s,a)=r(s,a)-J(\pi_{\theta_{k}})+\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s,a)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\widebar{V}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s^{\prime})\right]-\widebar{V}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s)

(1.21)

This expression involves the average-reward bias
\widebar{V}^{\pi_{\theta_{k}}}(s)
, which we can approximated using the standard critic network
\widebar{V}_{\phi_{k}}(s)
. However, in practice we use the average-reward version of the Generalized Advantage Estimator (GAE) from
[
184
]
, similar to
[
249
]
. Hence, we refer the reader to that paper for detailed explanation, but provide an overview below for completeness.

Let
\widehat{J}_{\pi}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}r_{t}
denote the estimated average reward. The Monte Carlo target for the average reward value function is
\widebar{V}^{\text{target}}_{t}=\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{N}(r_{t}-\widehat{J}_{\pi})
and the bootstrapped target is
\widebar{V}^{\text{target}}_{t}=r_{t}-\widehat{J}_{\pi}+\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t+1})
.

The Monte Carlo and Bootstrap estimators for the average reward advantage function are:

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{\text{MC}}^{\pi}(s_{t},a_{t})=\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{N}(r_{t}-\widehat{J}_{\pi})-\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t})\qquad;\qquad\widehat{A}_{\text{BS}}^{\pi}(s_{t},a_{t})=r_{i,t}-\widehat{J}_{\pi}+\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t+1})-\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t})

We can similarly extend the GAE to the average reward setting:

\widehat{A}_{\text{GAE}}(s_{t},a_{t})=\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{N}\lambda^{t^{\prime}-t}\delta_{t^{\prime}}\qquad,\qquad\delta_{t^{\prime}}=r_{t^{\prime}}-\widehat{J}_{\pi}+\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t^{\prime}+1})-\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t^{\prime}}).

(1.22)

and set the target for the value function to
\widebar{V}^{\text{target}}_{t}=r_{t}-\widehat{J}_{\pi}+\widebar{V}^{\pi}_{\phi}(s_{t+1})+\sum_{t^{\prime}=t+1}^{N}\lambda^{t^{\prime}-t}\delta_{t^{\prime}}
.

1.6.5

Experimental Details

For detailed explanation of Point-Circle, Point-Gather, Ant-Circle, and Ant-Gather tasks, please refer to
[
4
]
. For detailed explanation of Bottleneck and Grid tasks, please refer to
[
211
]
. For the simulations in the Gather and Circle tasks, we use neural network baselines with the same architecture and activation functions as the policy networks. For the simulations in the Grid and Bottleneck tasks, we use linear baselines. For all experiments we use Gaussian neural policies whose outputs are the mean vectors and variances are separate parameters to be learned. Seeds used for generating evaluation trajectories are different from those used for training.

For comparison of different algorithms, we make use of CPO, PCPO, ATRPO, and PPO implementations taken from
rllab
and
safety-starter-agents
. Even the hyperparameters are selected so as to showcase the best performance of other algorithms for fair comparison. The choice of the hyperparameters given below is inspired by the original papers since we wanted to understand the performance of the average reward case.

We use settings which are common in all open-source implementations of the algorithms, such as normalizing the states by the running mean and standard deviation before being fed into the neural network and similarly normalizing the advantage values (for both rewards and constraints) by their batch means and standard deviations before being used for policy updates. For the Lagrangian formulation of ATRPO and PPO, note that the original papers do not provide any blueprint for formulating the Lagrangian, and even CPO and PCPO use
unconstrained

\ell\in[0,1]
that balances the rewards and constraints in the final objective function. More specifically, Equation (
1.13
) for a single constraint now becomes

\displaystyle\max_{\theta}

\displaystyle(1-\ell)g^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})-\ell\big[\big(c_{1}+a_{1}^{T}(\theta-\theta_{k})\big)+\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{k})^{T}H(\theta-\theta_{k})-\delta\big)\big].

(1.23)

Note

\ell
. Hence, the choice of the Lagrangian parameter
\ell
