More ablation studies continued from Section
3.6
.

3.7.1

Understanding Two Actions and Finite Deliberateness

In the building block towards Theorem
3.4.2
, we consider the case with
K=2
and hence
{\mathcal{A}}=\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
, which means
\overline{A}_{i}^{(0)}=a_{0}
and
\overline{A}_{i}^{(1)}=a_{1}
for all
i\in[N]
. Here, we focus on understanding how the comparison noises, due to finite deliberateness, affect the results. In other words, we see how the effect of deliberateness results in more than one sample being required to determine the optimal action with high probability.

Essentially, given an offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, we construct a
warm
-posterior of the likelihood that an action is optimal. Based on this posterior over the actions, we can construct an action subset
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
with
|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|=1
.

Constructing the Algorithm

In this part, we deal with the question that given an offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, how to develop an algorithm to constructing an action subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
with
|{\mathcal{U}}|=1
.

For this, we need to calculate the posterior distribution of an action being optimal given the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. If
P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})>P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})
, then
{\mathcal{U}}=\{a_{0}\}
, else
{\mathcal{U}}=\{a_{1}\}
. Let
p_{0}:=\exp\left(\beta\left\langle a_{0},\vartheta\right\rangle\right)
and
p_{1}:=\exp\left(\beta\left\langle a_{1},\vartheta\right\rangle\right)
. So,

\displaystyle P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,a_{0}=A^{\star})\cdot P(a_{0}=A^{\star})}{P({\mathcal{D}}_{0})}

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,a_{0}=A^{\star}\,;\,\vartheta)\,d\vartheta\cdot P(a_{0}=A^{\star})}{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\beta,\lambda)\,d\lambda d\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\,d\vartheta\cdot P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,;\,\theta_{0})}{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\beta,\lambda)\,d\lambda d\beta}

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mu_{0},\Sigma_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\,d\vartheta\cdot P\big(\langle a_{0},\theta_{0}\rangle\geq\langle a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle\big)}{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\beta,\lambda)\,d\lambda d\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\,d\vartheta}{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\beta,\lambda)\,d\lambda d\beta}\cdot P\big(\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle\geq 0\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\,d\vartheta}{\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\int P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\beta,\lambda)\,d\lambda d\beta}\cdot\bigg(1-\Phi\bigg(-\frac{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\Sigma_{0}(a_{0}-a_{1})}}\bigg)\bigg)

, where
\Phi
is the CDF of the standard normal distribution. Similar expression follows for
P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})=1-P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})
.

Limiting Behaviour of the Algorithm for Optimal Expert

Here, we see that under our specified algorithm, for any offline data size
N\geq 1
, as
\beta,\lambda\rightarrow\infty
,
{\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow\{A^{*}\}
almost surely. It is easy to see this. As
\lambda\to\infty
, we have
\vartheta\to\theta_{0}
. Then,

\lim_{\beta\to\infty,\vartheta\to\theta_{0}}\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}=\lim_{\beta\to\infty,\vartheta\to\theta_{0}}\bigg(\frac{1}{1+e^{-\beta\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\vartheta\rangle}}\bigg)^{N}.

Now observe that if
\beta,\lambda\to\infty
, if
a_{1}=A^{\star}
, then
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\vartheta\rangle\leq 0\;\Rightarrow\;\lim_{\beta,\lambda\to\infty}\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\to 0\;\Rightarrow\;P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})\to 0
. Same holds for when
\beta,\lambda\to\infty
and if
a_{0}
is optimal. Which means the specified decision rule above converges with
{\mathcal{U}}\to\{A^{\star}\}
almost surely.

Limiting Behaviour of the Algorithm for Large Datasets

Here, we show that under our specified algorithm, for any finite
\beta>0
, as
\lambda,N\rightarrow\infty
,
{\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow\{A^{*}\}
almost surely.

For this, we just calculate the ratio
\lim_{\lambda,N\to\infty}\frac{P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}{P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}
and check whether it tends to zero or infinity. Let
x:=\frac{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\Sigma_{0}(a_{0}-a_{1})}}
. So then,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda,N\to\infty}\frac{P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}{P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\vartheta\to\theta_{0},N\to\infty}\frac{\big(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\big)^{N}\cdot(1-\Phi(-x))}{\big(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\big)^{N}\cdot(1-\Phi(x))}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\vartheta\to\theta_{0},N\to\infty}\bigg(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{1}}\bigg)^{N}\cdot\frac{\Phi(x)}{1-\Phi(x)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}[\exp(\beta\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle)]^{N}\cdot\frac{\Phi(x)}{1-\Phi(x)}

Now, we can apply the same argument of, if
a_{0}=A^{\star}
, then
\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle\geq 0
to see that the above expression tends to positive infinity for any finite
\beta>0
. Hence, we can construct
{\mathcal{U}}=\{A^{\star}\}
almost surely.

Sample Complexity for Finite Deliberateness

In this part, we consider for any finite
\beta>0
, as
\lambda\to\infty
, and any given
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, under our specified algorithm, how large does
N
need to be to ensure
P({\mathcal{U}}=\{A^{*}\})\geq 1-\epsilon
?

Lemma 3.7.1
.

For an action set
{\mathcal{A}}=\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
and any finite
\beta\in(0,\infty)
, with
\lambda\to\infty
and for some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, the size of the offline dataset to ensure
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}=\{A^{\star}\}
and hence
(1-\epsilon)
-informative is:

N\geq\frac{\ln\bigg(\big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-1\big)\big(\frac{1}{\Phi(x)}-1\big)\bigg)}{\beta\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle}\,,

(3.11)

where
x:=\frac{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\Sigma_{0}(a_{0}-a_{1})}}
, and
\Phi(\cdot)
is the standard Normal CDF.

Proof 3.7.2
.

Assume
A^{\star}=a_{0}
. Then, we want
P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})>1-\epsilon
and
P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})<\epsilon
. Letting Let
x:=\frac{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{(a_{0}-a_{1})^{T}\Sigma_{0}(a_{0}-a_{1})}}
same as before and taking the ratio of these as
\lambda\to\infty
for a finite
\beta,N>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\frac{P(a_{0}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}{P(a_{1}=A^{\star}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\vartheta\to\theta_{0}}\frac{\big(\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\big)^{N}\cdot(1-\Phi(-x))}{\big(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{0}+p_{1}}\big)^{N}\cdot(1-\Phi(x))}

\displaystyle>\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon}

(3.12)

\displaystyle=[\exp(\beta\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle)]^{N}\cdot\frac{\Phi(x)}{1-\Phi(x)}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{\epsilon}-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;N>\frac{\ln\bigg(\big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-1\big)\big(\frac{1}{\Phi(x)}-1\big)\bigg)}{\beta\langle a_{0}-a_{1},\theta_{0}\rangle}.

Without loss of generality, similar argument holds for if
A^{\star}=a_{1}
.

3.7.2

Understanding Multiple Actions and Infinite Deliberateness

In this building block towards Theorem
3.4.2
, we focus on the case with
\lambda=\beta=\infty
. In other words, there are no comparison noises. Moreover, as before, the two actions
\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)}
and
\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)}
are i.i.d. sampled from a distribution
\mu
over
{\mathcal{A}}
. With this, we understand how this sampling distribution
\mu
affects the results.

For a finite dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
of size
N
, let
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be the set consisting of all unique actions occurring in
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Then, the informative set
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
can be constructed with two types of actions : (i) actions not appearing in
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
(ii) actions occurring in
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
that have not ‘lost’ the comparison with any another action. We begin by constructing an algorithm for this analysis.

Developing the Algorithm

For a finite dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
of size
N
, let
{\mathcal{U}}_{N}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be the set consisting of all unique actions occurring in
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Then,
{\mathcal{U}}
can be constructed with two types of actions : (i) actions not appearing in
{\mathcal{U}}_{N}
(ii) actions occurring in
{\mathcal{U}}_{N}
that have not ‘lost’ the comparison with any another action. For this, let
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
be the set of comparisons from
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
involving action
a_{i}
i.e.
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}=\big\{\big(\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},Y_{n}\big)\;;\;\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)}=a_{i}\;\text{or}\;\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)}=a_{i}\;,\;n\in[N]\big\}
. Hence, construct
{\mathcal{U}}=({\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N})\cup{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}
, where
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}:=\{a_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{N}\;;\;Y_{j}=a_{i}{\;\;\forall\;}\big(\overline{A}_{j}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{j}^{(1)},Y_{j}\big)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\}
.

Note here that the conditions mentioned above are tight conditions, which can be analyzed in the case of uniform action sampling distribution (
\mu=

Uniform
(\cdot)
). However, in the case of an arbitrary distribution such analysis is intractable. In this case, we then only consider the sufficient condition to obtain complete ordering of actions. The sufficient condition to determine the optimal action with high probability is to sample each pair of actions at least once i.e. sample each of
\binom{K}{2}
pairs once.

Finding the Optimal Action Given a Large Dataset

Here, we show that the construction procedure as described above in Part
3.7.2
yields in finding the optimal action given a large dataset. More formally, we show that if the action sampling distribution
\mu
is not degenerate i.e.
\lim_{N\to\infty}P(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{N})>0{\;\;\forall\;}a\in{\mathcal{A}}
, then as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
{\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow\{A^{*}\}
almost surely.

To see this, if
\mu
is not degenerate, then
\lim_{N\to\infty}P(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{N})>0{\;\;\forall\;}a\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Then,
\lim_{N\to\infty}({\mathcal{A}}\;\setminus\;{\mathcal{U}}_{N})=\emptyset
. In addition, as
N\to\infty
, for all possible pairs of actions
(a_{i},a_{j})
with
a_{i}\neq a_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, we will have
\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)}=a_{i}
and
\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)}=a_{j}
for some
\big(\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},Y_{n}\big)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Due to the construction of
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}
, we will also have
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}=\{A^{\star}\}
almost surely. This implies
\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathcal{U}}=\{A^{\star}\}
.

Now that we know that the construction procedure of
{\mathcal{U}}_{N}
is principled, we wish to generalize the result for finite size of the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
.

General Sample Complexity Analysis

In this section, we present results for the following question : In general, how large
N
need be to ensure that
P(|{\mathcal{U}}|=\mathcal{o}(K))
, or even
P(|{\mathcal{U}}|=1)
with high probability? We aim to derive this result for an arbitrary action sampling distribution
\mu
, however we begin by analyzing the case of uniform distribution i.e.
\mu\sim

Uniform
(\cdot)
.

General analysis of probability of picking all
n
items in
N
trials

.

Here, we describe the general theoretical framework to bound the probabilities of picking
all
of
n
given items in
N
independent trials. We first begin with a uniform distribution over each of these
n
items and later generalize to an arbitrary distribution
\mu
. Note that we derive a general result for
n
items, which in our case corresponds to actions (
n=K
) or action pairs (
n=\binom{K}{2}
).

•

Uniform distribution.
We have
n
items which are equally likely to be selected, so we can invoke the Stirling numbers of the second kind (or Stirling partition number) to get a bound on this probability. Stirling numbers of the second kind give the number of ways to partition a set of
u
objects into
v
non-empty subsets and is denoted by
S(u,v)
. For notation, we have
n
items to be selected,
N
as the number of trials.

Now, let
S_{i}
be all the outcomes in which an item
i
is
not
selected. For each
i
,
|S_{i}|=(n-1)^{N}
and there are
\binom{n}{1}
choices for
i
. For each
j\neq i
,
|S_{j}\cap S_{i}|=(n-2)^{N}
and there are
\binom{n}{2}
choices for
(i,j)
. Continuing in this manner to count the number of outcomes missing at least
1
number, we get

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}S_{i}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|S_{i}|-\sum_{j<i}|S_{j}\cap S_{i}|+\sum_{k<j<i}|S_{k}\cap S_{j}\cap S_{i}|-\;\dots

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{1}(n-1)^{N}-\binom{n}{2}(n-2)^{N}+\binom{n}{3}(n-3)^{N}-\;\dots

Since there are a total of
n^{N}
total outcomes, we get the number of desired outcomes in which
all
possible numbers are rolled, denoted by
\#_{\text{desired}}
as

\#_{\text{desired}}=n^{N}-\binom{n}{1}(n-1)^{N}+\binom{n}{2}(n-2)^{N}-\binom{n}{3}(n-3)^{N}+\;\dots.

Thus, the probability
p_{n,N}
of picking all
n
items in
N
trials is
\frac{\#_{\text{desired}}}{n^{N}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle p_{n,N}

\displaystyle=1-\binom{n}{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{N}+\binom{n}{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{n}\right)^{N}-\binom{n}{3}\left(1-\frac{3}{n}\right)^{N}+\dots

(3.13)

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow

\displaystyle p_{n,N}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\binom{n}{i}\left(1-\frac{i}{n}\right)^{N}

•

Arbitrary Distribution.
Assume now that the actions are sampled from an action sampling distribution
\mu
. Since we are forming action pairs for comparison, denote with
\ring{\mu}_{k}
, the probability of sampling action pair
k:=(i,j)\in[\binom{K}{2}]
and with
\sum_{k}\ring{\mu}_{k}=1
, with
(i,j)
representing the action pair
(a_{i},a_{j})
. Furthermore, this means that assume that
\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}\leq\ring{\mu}_{k}\leq\mu_{\text{max}}^{2}{\;\;\forall\;}k
for some arbitrary
0<\mu_{\text{min}}\leq\mu_{\text{max}}<1
.

For this problem, let
T_{i}
denote the random number of trials needed to sample item
i
for the first time. The total number of trials needed can be then denoted by the random variable
T=\max(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})
. Note that

T_{i}
is a geometric random variable with parameter
\ring{\mu}_{i}
because each new item obtained is of type
i
with probability
\ring{\mu}_{i}
, but now these variables are no more independent. Since the minimum of
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
is the number of trials needed to obtain either item
i
or item
j
, it follows that for
j\neq i
,
\min(N_{i},N_{j})
is a geometric random variable with parameter
\ring{\mu}_{i}+\ring{\mu}_{j}
and the same holds true for the minimum of any finite number of these random variables. Hence, we can write,

\begin{aligned} {\mathbb{E}}[T]&={\mathbb{E}}[\max_{i}T_{i}]\\
&=\sum_{i}{\mathbb{E}}[T_{i}]-\sum_{i<j}{\mathbb{E}}[\min(T_{i},T_{j})]+\sum_{i<j<k}{\mathbb{E}}[\min(T_{i},T_{j},T_{k})]-\;\dots\;+(-1)^{n+1}{\mathbb{E}}[\min(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})]\\
&=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{\ring{\mu}_{i}}-\sum_{i<j}\frac{1}{\ring{\mu}_{i}+\ring{\mu}_{j}}+\sum_{i<j<k}\frac{1}{\ring{\mu}_{i}+\ring{\mu}_{j}+\ring{\mu}_{k}}-\;\dots\;+(-1)^{n+1}\frac{1}{\ring{\mu}_{1}+\dots+\ring{\mu}_{n}}\end{aligned}

Recall that
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tx}dx=\frac{1}{t}
. We also know the identity

\displaystyle 1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-e^{-t_{i}x})=\sum_{i}e^{-t_{i}x}-\sum_{i<j}e^{-(t_{i}+t_{j})x}+\;\dots\;+(-1)^{n+1}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})x}

(3.14)

Using the above identity, and integrating it, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[T]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1-e^{-\ring{\mu}_{i}x}\big)\bigg)dx

(3.15)

Lemma 3.7.3
.

Let the action set be
{\mathcal{A}}=\{a_{0},\,\dots\,,a_{K}\}
, with a sampling distribution
\mu
such that
0<\mu_{\text{min}}\leq\mu_{k}\leq\mu_{\text{max}}<1{\;\;\forall\;}k\in[K]
. For the case of
\beta,\lambda\to\infty
, with some given
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, the minimum size of the offline dataset to ensure
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}=\{A^{\star}\}
and hence is
(1-\epsilon)
-informative is given by

\text{Uniform}\;\,\mu\;:\;N\geq\frac{K^{2}\ln K}{\epsilon}\qquad;\qquad\text{Arbitrary}\;\,\mu\;:\;N\gtrsim\frac{\ln K}{\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}\epsilon}

(3.16)

Proof 3.7.4
.

We first begin by proving the case of uniform action sampling distribution, and then extend the results to an arbitrary distribution.

Uniform Distribution

.

If
|{\mathcal{U}}|=1
, then
|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=1\land|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=0\;

OR

\;|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=0\land|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1
.

Case 1

.
|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=1
and
|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=0
.

For the former, we simply do not want to select the optimal action while making action pairs, and hence the probability is:

P(|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=1)\geq\bigg(1-\frac{2}{K}\bigg)^{N}

For the latter, we use Equation (
3.13
) with
n=\binom{K-1}{2}
to get

P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=0)\geq\sum_{i=0}^{\binom{K-1}{2}}(-1)^{i}\binom{\binom{K-1}{2}}{i}\left(1-\frac{i}{\binom{K-1}{2}}\right)^{N}.

Case 2

.
|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=0
and
|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1
.

In this case, if all pairs are sampled at least once, the event
\{|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1\}
is a sufficient condition for event
\{|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=0\}
to occur. Hence, we use Equation (
3.13
) with
n=\binom{K}{2}
to get the probability as:

P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1)\geq\sum_{i=0}^{\binom{K}{2}}(-1)^{i}\binom{\binom{K}{2}}{i}\left(1-\frac{i}{\binom{K}{2}}\right)^{N}.

Putting it all together, we need

1-\epsilon\leq P(|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=1)\cdot P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=0)+P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1)

However, the above form is intractable to solve for a closed form solution. Hence, we use the Stirling number approximation for factorials (i.e.
\ln(n!)\approx n\ln(n)-n
) and approximation of the Stirling number of second kind i.e.
S(u,v)\leq n\ln(n)-n\ln(\ln(n))+n\ln(k)
, where
S(u,v)=\sum_{i=0}^{v}\frac{(-1)^{v-i}i^{u}}{(v-i)!i!}
. In addition, we also Stirling’s approximation to the binomial as
\binom{a}{b}\approx\frac{a^{b}}{b!}
for
a>>b
. Using these, the expression simplifies to
N\geq\frac{K^{2}\ln K}{\epsilon}
.

Arbitrary Distribution

.

Similar to the case of uniform distribution, we still need

1-\epsilon\leq P(|{\mathcal{A}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{N}|=1)\cdot P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=0)+P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1)

However, a closed form solution for the above does not exist for the case of multiple actions. Instead, we aim to derive the result based on the sufficient condition for obtaining the optimal action : if all pairs of actions are sampled at least once, we know the optimal action. For this, we just need
P(|{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{N}}|=1)\geq 1-\epsilon
.

Recalling from the analysis given above in Section
3.7.2
,
T_{i}
denotes the random number of trials needed to sample item
i
for the first time. The total number of trials needed can be then denoted by the random variable
T=\max(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})
.

Now, since
T
is a random variable denoting the total number of trials needed to obtain all
n
items
at least
once, it can also be viewed as the
stopping time
for when the agent has collected all items. Hence, we are interested in the probability
P(T\leq N)
i.e. the probability that this stopping time
T
is less than the dataset size
N
. This is because the event
\{T\leq N\}
is the event that by time (or dataset size)
N
, the agent has sampled all
n
items.

We then also have
P(T\leq N)=1-P(T>N)
. Since
N
is non-negative, we can bound the
P(T>N)
using a concentration inequality as below using Equation (
3.15
).

\displaystyle P(T>N)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[T]}{N}=\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1-e^{-\ring{\mu}_{i}x}\big)\bigg)dx

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;P(T\leq N)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1-e^{-\ring{\mu}_{i}x}\big)\bigg)dx

(3.17)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{i}e^{-\ring{\mu}_{i}x}-\sum_{i<j}e^{-(\ring{\mu}_{i}+\ring{\mu}_{j})x}+\;\dots\;+(-1)^{n+1}e^{-(\ring{\mu}_{1}+\dots+\ring{\mu}_{n})x}\bigg)dx

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{N\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}}\bigg(\frac{\binom{n}{1}}{1}-\frac{\binom{n}{2}}{2}+\dots+(-1)^{n+1}\frac{\binom{n}{n}}{n}\bigg)

(3.18)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{H_{n}}{N\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}}

(3.19)

, where
H_{n}
is the Harmonic sum of the first
n
natural numbers. Now, we wish that
P(T\leq N)\geq 1-\epsilon
. Using the bound above, we find that we need

N\geq\frac{H_{n}}{\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}\epsilon}\gtrsim\frac{\ln K}{\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}\epsilon}.

3.7.3

Understanding Multiple Actions and Finite Deliberateness

We can break the expected number of samples needed to find an optimal action into parts and then use a generalized version of the Coupon Collection problem, solution of which is known
[
153
]
. The first deals with using Equation (
3.11
) to find the minimum samples needed to determine the more likely optimal action between two actions (one pair) with high probability of
(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2n})
, where
n
is total number of items. Here,
n
would be the number of pairs i.e.
n=\binom{K}{2}
. The second part deals with finding the bound on total number of samples needed to determine the more likely optimal action for
every
such pair.

Finding the better action in the
i^{\text{th}}
item (pair)

.

The expected number of samples needed to find the better action can be calculated using Equation (
3.11
). Call this number
k_{i}
. So,

k_{i}\geq\frac{\ln\bigg(\big(\frac{2\binom{K}{2}}{\epsilon}-1\big)\big(\frac{1}{\Phi(x_{i})}-1\big)\bigg)}{\beta\langle a_{i}^{(0)}-a_{i}^{(1)},\theta_{0}\rangle},

where
x_{i}:=\frac{(a_{i}^{(0)}-a_{i}^{(1)})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{\big(a_{i}^{(0)}-a_{i}^{(1)}\big)^{T}\Sigma_{0}\big(a_{i}^{(0)}-a_{i}^{(1)}\big)}}
,
\Phi(\cdot)
is the CDF of the standard Normal distribution, and
(a_{i}^{(0)},a_{i}^{(1)})
are the actions of the
i^{\text{th}}
pair.

See
3.4.2

Proof 3.7.5
.

Our sample complexity analysis to achieve no Bayesian regret can be broken down into three main building blocks:

•

Appendix
3.7.1
and Lemma
3.7.1
: there are only two actions (
|{\mathcal{A}}|=2
) but we have finite deliberateness (
\beta<\infty
).

•

Appendix
3.7.2
and Lemma
3.7.3
: there are many actions (
|{\mathcal{A}}|=K
) but we have very high deliberateness (
\beta\to\infty
).

•

Appendix
3.7.3
: there are many actions (
|{\mathcal{A}}|=K
) and finite deliberateness, where we combine the results from the first two cases. In this case, we can break the expected number of samples needed to find an optimal action into two parts. The first deals with using Lemma
3.7.1
to find the minimum samples needed to determine the more likely optimal action between two actions (one pair) with high probability of
(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2n})
, where
n
is total number of items. Here,
n
would be the number of pairs i.e.
n=\binom{K}{2}
. The second part deals with finding the bound on total number of samples needed to determine the more likely optimal action for
every
such pair.

With this in mind, we prove the result below.

[
153
]
gave a generalization of the coupon collector’s problem when
m
copies of each coupon need to be collected with total coupons being
n
. Let
T_{m}
be the first time
m
copies of
each
coupon are collected. We then know that
{\mathbb{E}}[T_{m}]=n\ln n+(m-1)n\ln(\ln n)
.

Using similar analysis as before for a general action pair sampling distribution
\mu
with
\mu_{\text{min}}\leq\mu_{i}\leq\mu_{\text{max}}
for some
\mu_{\text{min}},\mu_{\text{max}}\in[0,1)
for all items (pairs)
i\in[n]
, we can derive the general sampling result. Adapting it to our setting, we need
k_{i}
samples for
i^{\text{th}}
pair, and we have
n=\binom{K}{2}
pairs. Letting
T_{k_{\text{max}}}
denote the total number of samples needed to obtain
k_{\text{max}}
number of samples for each item (pair),

{\mathbb{E}}[T_{k_{\text{max}}}]\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}}\big[2\ln(n)+(k_{\text{max}}-1)\ln(\ln(n))\big]\quad;\quad n=\mbinom{K}{2}\,\,,\,\,k_{\text{max}}=\max_{i\in[n]}k_{i}

Denoting
T_{k_{\text{max}}}
as the random stopping time when at least
k_{\text{max}}
occurrences of all
n
items have been collected, we need
P(T_{k_{\text{max}}}>N)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, where
N
is the size of the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Hence, using Markov inequality, we can bound it as:

\displaystyle N\geq\frac{\ln K+(k_{\text{max}-1})\ln\ln K}{\mu_{\text{min}}^{2}\epsilon}

\displaystyle\quad\text{where},

(3.20)

\displaystyle k_{\text{max}}=\max_{i,j\in[K]}\frac{\ln\bigg(\big(\frac{2K^{2}}{\epsilon}-1\big)\big(\frac{1}{\Phi(x_{i,j})}-1\big)\bigg)}{\beta\langle a_{i}-a_{j},\theta_{0}\rangle}\;

\displaystyle,\;x_{i,j}=\frac{(a_{i}-a_{j})^{T}\mu_{0}}{\sqrt{\big(a_{i}-a_{j}\big)^{T}\Sigma_{0}\big(a_{i}-a_{j}\big)}}

3.7.4

Regret Analysis Continued

In this appendix section, we provide the building block proofs that allow us to construct a prior-dependent Bayesian regret bound on the
\mathsf{warmPref-PS}
algorithm. The heart of these proofs lies in constructing a
(1-\epsilon)
-informative set
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
from the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.7.6
.

{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
is
(1-f_{1})-
informative.

Proof 3.7.7
.

We construct
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
as a
set
of actions that have been preferred to
at least
once in the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and of actions that do not appear in the
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
contains at most
K
actions.

Now, we consider the formulation below. Recall that
\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)}
and
\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)}
are i.i.d. sampled from the action set and each datapoint in the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{i}
, conditioned on
\vartheta,\beta
, is independent of
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{j}
for
i\neq j
. Now,

\displaystyle P\left(A^{\star}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq P(A^{\star}\;\text{has lost all comparisons in}\;{\mathcal{D}}_{0})+P(A^{\star}\;\text{is not present in}\;{\mathcal{D}}_{0})

(3.21)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\exp\big(\beta\langle a_{n},\vartheta\rangle\big)}{\exp\big(\beta\langle a_{n},\vartheta\rangle\big)+\exp\big(\beta\langle A^{\star},\vartheta\rangle\big)}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\bigg(1-\frac{\exp\big(\beta\langle A^{\star},\vartheta\rangle\big)}{\exp\big(\beta\langle a_{n},\vartheta\rangle\big)+\exp\big(\beta\langle A^{\star},\vartheta\rangle\big)}\bigg)\bigg]+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\bigg(1-\underbrace{\frac{1}{1+\exp\big(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\vartheta\rangle\big)}}_{\clubsuit}\bigg)\bigg]+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}

, where
A^{\star}
is a function of
\theta
and thus a random variable as well. Looking closely at the term
\clubsuit
above, it can be written as
P(Y_{n}=A^{\star}\,|\,\vartheta)
. We now analyze this term.

\displaystyle P(Y_{n}=A^{\star}\,|\,\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\exp\big(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\vartheta\rangle\big)}

\displaystyle=\big(1+\exp\left(\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta-\vartheta\rangle-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right)\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq\big(1+\exp\left(\beta\|A^{\star}-a_{n}\|_{1}\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\infty}-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right)\big)^{-1}

(Hölder’s inequality)

\displaystyle\geq\big(1+\exp\left(\beta\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right)\big)^{-1}

(
\|A^{\star}-a_{n}\|_{1}\leq 1{\;\;\forall\;}a_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}
)

Since
\vartheta-\theta\sim N(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/\lambda^{2})
, using the Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz inequality bound
[
137
,
210
]
implies

P\left(\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}\geq t\right)\leq 2d^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)\,.

Set
t=\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}/\lambda
and define an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}:=\{\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}/\lambda\}
such that
P({\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{c})\leq 1/T
. We decompose Equation (
3.21
) using Union Bound as:

\begin{aligned} P\left(A^{\star}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right)&\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(1-P\left(Y_{n}=A^{\star}\;\big|\;\theta,\vartheta\right)\right){\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}}\right]+P({\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{c})+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{\beta\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}}{\lambda}\right)\underbrace{\exp\left(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right)}_{\blacktriangle}\right)^{-1}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\,.\end{aligned}

(3.22)

Now, we define another event
{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}:=\{\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\leq\Delta\}
. Based on
{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}
we analyze the
\blacktriangle
term as follows.

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big[\exp\left(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right){\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}\big]+{\mathbb{E}}\big[\exp\left(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\right){\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}^{c}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(0\right)P({\mathcal{E}}_{(n)})+\exp\left(-\beta\Delta\right)P({\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq P({\mathcal{E}}_{(n)})+(1-P({\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}))\exp\left(-\beta\Delta\right)

Plugging this back in Equation (
3.22
) we get,

\begin{aligned} P\left(A^{\star}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right)&\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{\beta\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}}{\lambda}\right)\left({\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}+(1-{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}})\exp\left(-\beta\Delta\right)\right)\right)^{-1}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\\
\end{aligned}

(3.23)

Note that the random variable
{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}
depends on the action sampling distribution
\mu
. Denote the probability of sampling this action
a_{n}
by
\mu_{n}
, and as before we have
\mu
supported by
[\mu_{\text{min}},\mu_{\text{max}}]
. We first analyze this for any arbitrary
n\in[N]
and study the the distribution of
{\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}
conditionaled on
A^{\star}
. Without loss of generality, we first condition on
A^{\star}=\ring{a}
for some
\ring{a}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. For that, let
\rho(\cdot)
be the univariate Gaussian distribution and
\theta_{a}=\langle a,\theta\rangle
for any action
a
.

\begin{aligned} P\left({\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}=1\,|\,A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)&=P\left({\mathbb{I}}\left(\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\leq\Delta\right)=1\;\big|\;A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)&\\
&=\frac{1}{P\left(A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)}P\left({\mathbb{I}}\left(\langle A^{\star}-a_{n},\theta\rangle\leq\Delta\right)=1\,,\,A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)&\\
&=\frac{1}{P\left(A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)}P\left({\mathbb{I}}\left(\theta_{a_{n}}\geq\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta\right)=1\,,\,\bigcap_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\{\theta_{\ring{a}}\geq\theta_{a}\}\right)&\\
&=\frac{1}{P\left(A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)}\bigintssss_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left[\int_{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}^{\infty}d\rho(\theta)\right]d\rho(\theta_{\ring{a}})&\\
&=\frac{1}{P\left(A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)}\bigintsss_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left[\int_{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)\right]d\rho(\theta_{\ring{a}})\,&(\text{since}\,\theta_{a}\leq\theta_{A^{\star}}{\;\;\forall\;}a)\end{aligned}

(3.24)

Noticing that the term inside the integral can be represented as a distribution, we first find a normalizing constant to represent the probabilities. So, define

\Phi(\theta_{\ring{a}})=\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{2}/2)\;dx\;\qquad;\qquad g(\theta_{\ring{a}})=\frac{1}{\Phi(\theta_{\ring{a}})}\int_{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)\;.

For fixed
\theta_{\ring{a}}
, let
X_{\theta_{\ring{a}}}\sim\text{Bernoulli}(1,g(\theta_{\ring{a}}))
. With Eq. (
3.24
) and letting
d\mu(\theta_{\ring{a}})=\frac{\Phi(\theta_{\ring{a}})}{P(A^{\star}=a_{\ring{a}})}d\rho(\theta_{\ring{a}})
we have,

P\left({\mathbb{I}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{(n)}}=1\,|\,A^{\star}=\ring{a}\right)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}P(X_{\theta_{\ring{a}}}=1)\frac{\Phi(\theta_{\ring{a}})}{P(A^{\star}=\ring{a})}d\rho(\theta_{\ring{a}})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}P(X_{\theta_{\ring{a}}}=1)\;d\mu(\theta_{\ring{a}})\;.

(3.25)

Plugging this back in Equation (
3.23
) and upper bounding the probabilities we get,

\begin{aligned} P\left(A^{\star}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right)&\leq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\bigintssss_{{\mathbb{R}}}P(X_{\theta_{a}}=0)d\mu(\theta_{a})P\left(A^{\star}=a\right)\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}-\Delta\right)\right)\right)^{-1}\right)^{N}\cdot\mu_{\text{max}}^{N}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\\
&\leq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\bigintssss_{{\mathbb{R}}}P(X_{\theta_{a}}=0)\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}-\Delta\right)\right)\right)^{-1}\right)^{N}\cdot\mu_{\text{max}}^{N}\;d\mu(\theta_{a})P\left(A^{\star}=a\right)\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\\
&\leq\bigintssss_{{\mathbb{R}}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\ring{a}\,\in\,{\mathcal{A}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}-(1-X_{\theta_{\ring{a}}})\Delta\right)\right)\right)^{-1}\right)^{N}\right]\cdot\mu_{\text{max}}^{N}d\mu(\theta_{\ring{a}})+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\,,\end{aligned}

(3.26)

where
\mu_{\text{max}}
is used to obtain the exponent
N
by accounting for the sampling distribution
\mu
, and last step follows from the uniformity of each action being optimal. Finally, we need to find the supremum of
g(\theta_{\ring{a}})
and hence Equation (
3.26
). Recall that,

g(\theta_{\ring{a}})=\frac{1}{\Phi(\theta_{\ring{a}})}\int_{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)=\frac{\int_{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)}{\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)}=\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)-\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}d\rho(\theta)}{\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)}=1-h_{\Delta}(\ring{a})\;

, where
h_{\Delta}(\ring{a}):=\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta}d\rho(\theta)}{\int_{-\infty}^{\theta_{\ring{a}}}d\rho(\theta)}
. Setting
\nabla_{\ring{a}}h_{\Delta}(\ring{a})=0
and analyzing
\nabla_{\ring{a}}^{2}h_{\Delta}(\ring{a})>0
, we find that

g(\theta_{\ring{a}})\leq 1-\Delta\exp\left(-\frac{(2\theta_{\ring{a}}-\Delta)\Delta}{2}\right)\leq\min(1,\Delta)\;.

(3.27)

Finally we, decompose Equation (
3.26
) based on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}:=\{X_{\theta_{\ring{a}}}=0\}
, and upper bound the probability to simplify. Setting
\Delta=\ln(T\beta)/\beta
, we conclude with the following bound:

\begin{aligned} P\left(A^{\star}\notin{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right)&\leq\left(1-\left(1+\exp\left(\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}-(K-1)\min(1,\ln(T\beta)/\beta)\right)\right)\right)^{-1}\right)^{N}+\frac{1}{T}+(1-\mu_{\text{min}})^{2N}\end{aligned}

(3.28)

Lemma 3.7.8
.

{\mathbb{E}}[|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|]\leq f_{2}
.

Proof 3.7.9
.

Recall that
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
is a
set
of actions that have been preferred to
at least
once in the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and of actions that do not appear in the
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. We first see that
{\mathbb{E}}[|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}k\cdot P\left(|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|=k\right)
. Define an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{a}=\{\langle A^{\star}-a,\theta\rangle\leq\Delta\}
and analyze as follows,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathbb{I}}(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}})\right]=\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbb{I}}(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a})+{\mathbb{I}}(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{c})\right]

(3.29)

\displaystyle\leq K\min(1,\Delta^{2}/2)+\frac{1}{T}+{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathbb{I}}\left(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\right){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{c})\right]\,,

where the second step follows from the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{a}
and analysis done before : break down the indicator variable conditioning on arbitrary
A^{\star}=\ring{a}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, and use Poisson approximation to bound the probability. Now, analyze term in expectation above.

\begin{aligned} {\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathbb{I}}(a\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{c})\right]&={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}P\left(Y_{n}=a,\langle A^{\star}-a,\theta-\vartheta\rangle+\langle A^{\star}-a,\vartheta\rangle\geq\Delta\;\big|\;\theta,\vartheta\right){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{c})\right]\\
&\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}P\left(Y_{n}=a\,,\,\langle A^{\star}-a,\vartheta\rangle\geq\Delta-\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}/\lambda\;\big|\;\vartheta\right){\mathbb{I}}({\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{c})\right]\\
&\leq N{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}\;;\;\langle A^{\star}-a,\vartheta\rangle\geq\Delta-\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}/\lambda}\left(1+\exp\left(\beta\langle b-a,\vartheta\rangle\right)\right)^{-N}\right]\\
&\leq N{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}\;;\;\langle A^{\star}-a,\vartheta\rangle\geq\Delta-\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}/\lambda}\left(1+\exp\left(-\beta\langle A^{\star}-a,\vartheta\rangle\right)\right)^{-N}\right]\\
&\leq\frac{N(K-1)}{T\beta}\left(1+\exp\left(-\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}+(K-1)\min(1,\Delta)\right)\right)\right)^{-N}\\
&\leq\frac{NK}{T\beta}\left(1+\exp\left(-\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}+(K-1)\min(1,\Delta)\right)\right)\right)^{-N}\end{aligned}

(3.30)

Putting all of this together, we obtain the bound below.

\begin{aligned} {\mathbb{E}}[|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|]&\leq K\min(1,\Delta^{2}/2)+\frac{NK}{T\beta}\left(1+\exp\left(-\beta\left(\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}+(K-1)\min(1,\Delta)\right)\right)\right)^{-N}+\frac{1}{T}\;.\end{aligned}

(3.31)

Of course,
|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|
cannot exceed
K
, so we have with the choice of
\Delta=\ln(T\beta)/\beta
,

\begin{aligned} {\mathbb{E}}[|{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}|]&\leq\min\left(K\min\left(1,\frac{\ln^{2}(T\beta)}{2\beta^{2}}\right)+\frac{NK}{T\beta}\left(1+\exp\left(-\beta\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}T)}+(K-1)\min(1,\ln(T\beta)/\beta)\right)\right)^{-N}+\frac{1}{T},K\right).\end{aligned}

(3.32)

See
3.4.5

Proof 3.7.10
.

Combining Lemma
3.7.6
and Lemma
3.7.8
we have the desired result.

3.7.5

Constructing Surrogate Loss Function

This section contains proofs of construction of the surrogate loss function as described in Section
3.5
.

Lemma 3.7.11
.

At time
t
, the MAP estimate of
(\theta,\vartheta)
can be constructed by solving the following equivalent optimization problem:

\displaystyle(\theta_{opt},\vartheta_{opt})

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\;P(\theta,\vartheta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{t-1})

(3.33)

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmin}\;{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta)\;,

\displaystyle\text{where},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\big(R_{s}-\langle A_{s},\theta\rangle\big)^{2},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta):=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(Y_{n})},\vartheta\rangle+\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta):=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0}).

Proof 3.7.12
.

We first analyze the posterior distribution of
\vartheta,\theta
given the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, optimize it by treating these random variables as parameters.

\displaystyle\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\;P(\theta,\vartheta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{t-1})

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\;P({\mathcal{D}}_{t-1}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta)\cdot P(\theta,\vartheta)

(3.34)

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\;\ln P({\mathcal{D}}_{t-1}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta)+\ln P(\theta,\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\underbrace{\ln P({\mathcal{H}}_{t-1}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0},\theta,\vartheta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}+\underbrace{\ln P({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}+\underbrace{\ln P(\theta,\vartheta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{3}}

Then,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\underbrace{\ln P(A_{s}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{s-1},\theta,\vartheta)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\textbf{indep. of $\theta,\vartheta\;\Rightarrow\;\textbf{constant}$}}}+\ln P(R_{s}\,|\,A_{s},\theta,\vartheta)

(3.35)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\text{constant}}\;-\;\frac{t-1}{2}\ln\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\sigma^{2}}\bigg)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\big(R_{s}-\langle A_{s},\theta\rangle\big)^{2}.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\ln\bigg(\left(\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},Y_{n}\right)\,\big|\,\theta,\vartheta\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\ln\bigg(Y_{n}\,\big|\,\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},\theta,\vartheta\bigg)+\underbrace{\ln P\bigg(\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)}\;\big|\;\theta,\vartheta\bigg)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\textbf{indep. of $\theta,\vartheta$ \, ; \, depends on $\mu\;\Rightarrow\;$ constant}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(Y_{n})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\text{\small constant}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\ln P(\vartheta\,|\,\theta)+\ln P(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\ln\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\lambda^{2}}\bigg)-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\big(|2\pi\Sigma_{0}|\big)-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0}).

Hence, final surrogate loss function is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\theta,\vartheta)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta),\qquad\text{where}

(3.36)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\big(R_{s}-\langle A_{s},\theta\rangle\big)^{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(Y_{n})},\vartheta\rangle+\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{A}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0}).

Finally the problem in Equation (
3.34
) becomes equivalent as follows:

(\theta_{opt},\vartheta_{opt})=\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmax}\;P(\theta,\vartheta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{t})\equiv\underset{\theta,\vartheta}{\argmin}\;{\mathcal{L}}(\theta,\vartheta)

(3.37)

3.7.6

warmPref-PS with Online Feedback (warmTSOF)

Here, we present an extension to
\mathsf{warmPref-PS}
, where the agent has the option to ask for feedback during the online phase.

Problem Formulation

.

Algorithm 6

warm Thompson Sampling with Preference Feedback (
warmTSOF
)

1:

Input:
Horizon
T
, offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, set of arms
{\mathcal{A}}
, knowledgeability
\lambda
, deliberateness
\beta
, feedback cost
c
.

2:

for

t=1,2,\dots,T

do

3:

Sample a set of perturbations
{\mathcal{P}}_{t}=\{\zeta_{s},\omega_{n},\theta^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime}\}
.

4:

Solve Equation (
3.9
) using this set
{\mathcal{P}}_{t}
to find
(\widehat{\theta}_{t},\widehat{\vartheta}_{t})
.

5:

Let
A_{t}^{1}\,,A_{t}^{2}
be s.t.
\langle A_{t}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{t}\rangle\geq\langle A_{t}^{2},\widehat{\theta}_{t}\rangle\geq\langle A,\widehat{\theta}_{t}\rangle{\;\;\forall\;}A\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{A_{t}^{1},A_{t}^{2}\}
.

6:

Compute
\epsilon_{t}=\texttt{get\_epsilon}(c,{\mathcal{D}}_{t},t,\lambda,\beta)
.

7:

if

\big|\langle A_{t}^{1},\widehat{\theta}_{t}\rangle-\langle A_{t}^{2},\widehat{\theta}_{t}\rangle\big|<\epsilon_{t}

then

8:

Ask for feedback on
(A_{t}^{1},A_{t}^{2})
and receive
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
.

9:

Update
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\cup\{A_{t}^{1},A_{t}^{2},Y_{t}\}
.

10:

Update posterior using Equation (
3.9
) to get new
(\tilde{\theta_{t}},\tilde{\vartheta_{t}})
.

11:

Set
A_{t}=\argmax_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\langle a,\tilde{\theta_{t}}\rangle
and
c_{t}=c
.

12:

else

13:

Set
A_{t}=A_{t}^{1}
and
c_{t}=0
.

14:

end

if

15:

Take action
A_{t}
to receive reward
R_{t}-c_{t}
.

16:

Set
{\mathcal{D}}_{t+1}={\mathcal{D}}_{t}\cup\{A_{t},R_{t}\}
.

17:

end

for

(a)

c=0

(b)

c=0.1

Figure 3.4
:
Performance of
warmTSOF
with varying cost of feedback
c
.

We present the warm Posterior Sampling with Online Feedback (
warmTSOF
) algorithm wherein the learning agent can ask for preference feedback between two actions at some cost. We present some preliminary empirical results and defer the theoretical analysis to future work. This has applications in active learning
[
170
,
136
]
and crowd-sourcing data from experts for large language models
[
143
,
113
]
.

Consider, now in addition to Algorithm
5
(
warmTS
), the agent at any time, has the option to ask for
online
preference feedback between two actions. For simplicity, we assume the rater for this feedback is the same rater who generated the offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
.

Let the cost incurred for this feedback on actions
A_{t}^{1}
and
A_{t}^{2}
be
c_{t}=c\in{\mathbb{R}}
if agent asks for feedback, else
c_{t}=0
. So, this feedback takes the form
\{A_{t}^{1},A_{t}^{2},Y_{t}\}
, and
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
and the reward the agent receives then becomes
R_{t}-c_{t}
.

The agent should incorporate the expected current rewards for all actions, cost of feedback, and expert competency into the decision making process. The core idea is to only initiate feedback retrieval process if top-two expected rewards of all actions are ‘close’. This idea finds its in beginnings in the Top-Two Thompson Sampling procedure
[
173
]
. See
warmTSOF
(Algorithm
6
) for exact details. The
get_epsilon(
\cdot
)
function will be decided through analysis.

Performance

. See Figure
3.4
for performance comparison. Experiments are run with size of offline dataset
N=20
, deliberateness
\beta=10
, and knowledgeability
\lambda=10
. In addition, we let number of arms
k=10
, dimension of environment
d=4
, and horizon
T=300
, all averaged over 100 runs (random seeds). For baselines, we consider the traditional and warm Thompson Sampling (
TS
and
warmTS
).

3.7.7

Evaluation using
DPO
and
IPO

DPO
[
165
]
is an alternative approach to the RL paradigm, which avoids the training of a reward model altogether. The loss that DPO optimizes to obtain the optimal policy, given an empirical dataset
{\mathcal{D}}=\{y_{w},y_{l}\}
of the winning (preferred)
y_{w}
and losing (not preferred)
y_{l}
outputs (arms in our bandit setting), as a function of the reference policy
\pi_{\text{ref}}
and regularization strength
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\text{DPO}}=\argmin_{\pi}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{(y_{w},y_{l})\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\left[-\log\sigma\left(\tau\log\left(\frac{\pi(y_{w})}{\pi(y_{l})}\right)-\tau\log\left(\frac{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_{w})}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_{l})}\right)\right)\right]

, where
\sigma(\cdot)
denotes the sigmoid function.

IPO is an instance of the
\Psi\text{PO}
algorithm
[
78
]
. The loss function that IPO optimizes is given by,

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\text{IPO}}=\argmin_{\pi}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{(y_{w},y_{l})\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\left[h_{\pi}(y_{w},y_{l})-\frac{1}{2\tau}\right]^{2}\;\text{where}\,,\;h_{\pi}(y,y^{\prime}):=\log\left(\frac{\pi(y)\pi_{\text{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi(y^{\prime})\pi_{\text{ref}}(y)}\right)\,.

Note here that DPO and IPO are purely offline learning algorithms that only work with preference datasets. DPO and IPO cannot be trivially extended to our problem setting i.e. fixed offline preference dataset with active online numerical reward learning. Comparing DPO and IPO based solely on offline datasets is not fair. Hence, for a more fair comparison, we consider an offline-online variant of DPO with
\epsilon
-greedy online exploration. The pseudo code is as follows:

•

Input:
offline preference data
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
,
\epsilon
,
\min_{a}r^{\star}(a)
, DPO parameters

•

Offline learning:
use DPO to learn a policy
\pi
, use
\pi
to infer a reward model
r
such that
\min_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}r(a)=\min_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}r^{\star}(a)
, where
r^{\star}(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is true reward model.

•

Online learning:
at each time
t=1,\ldots,T

–

with probability
\epsilon
, choose
A_{t}
uniformly randomly; otherwise, choose
A_{t}=\argmax_{a}r(a)
.

–

observe reward from the environment, which is
r^{\star}(A_{t})
plus noise.

–

update the reward model
r
based on the received reward.

For training, mini‑batches are drawn uniformly with replacement from
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and optimized using
DPO
and
IPO
for 20k steps. Policy is encoded simply as
\pi_{\psi}(a_{i})=\text{softmax}(\bm{\psi})_{i}
for an action
a_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
using a vector
\bm{\psi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
, and is optimized for 20k steps using Adam
[
110
]
with a learning rate of 0.015 and mini-batch size 12. Reference policy is
\pi_{\text{ref}}
is chosen to be uniform over the action space, regularization is set at 0.1, and
\epsilon=0.16
for best results.
