4.7

Appendix

Network Architecture for DPO
[
165
]
and IPO
[
78
]

. We use a lightweight shared‐trunk MLP with two hidden layers of 64 ReLU units each (Xavier initialization
[
79
]
), followed by a policy action head, outputting action logits for
MountainCar

[
146
]
and
RiverSwim

[
196
]
environments. We train with Adam (learning rate
3\times 10^{-4}

[
110
]
), and apply dropout (0.1) on the trunk to regularize. This architecture balances expressivity and sample efficiency on these control tasks.

DPO
[
165
]
is an alternative approach to the RL paradigm, which avoids the training of a reward model altogether. The loss that DPO optimizes to obtain the optimal policy, given an empirical dataset
{\mathcal{D}}=\{y_{w},y_{l}\}
of the winning (preferred)
y_{w}
and losing (not preferred)
y_{l}
trajectories, as a function of the reference policy
\pi_{\text{ref}}
and regularization strength
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\text{DPO}}=\argmin_{\pi}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{(y_{w},y_{l})\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\left[-\log\sigma\left(\tau\log\left(\frac{\pi(y_{w})}{\pi(y_{l})}\right)-\tau\log\left(\frac{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_{w})}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_{l})}\right)\right)\right]

, where
\sigma(\cdot)
denotes the sigmoid function.

IPO is an instance of the
\Psi\text{PO}
algorithm
[
78
]
. The loss function that IPO optimizes is given by,

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\text{IPO}}=\argmin_{\pi}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{(y_{w},y_{l})\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\left[h_{\pi}(y_{w},y_{l})-\frac{1}{2\tau}\right]^{2}\;\text{where}\,,\;h_{\pi}(y,y^{\prime}):=\log\left(\frac{\pi(y)\pi_{\text{ref}}(y^{\prime})}{\pi(y^{\prime})\pi_{\text{ref}}(y)}\right)\,.

4.7.1

Prior-dependent analysis

Lemma 4.7.1

(Monotone Contraction)
.

\mathsf{PSPL}
is monotone with respect to optimality of candidate policies
\pi_{k}^{(0)}
and
\pi_{k}^{(1)}
at any episode
k\in[K]
i.e.
\Pr(\pi_{k}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})\leq\Pr(\pi_{1}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})
for
i\in\{0,1\}

{\;\;\forall\;}k\geq 1
.

Proof 4.7.2
.

Define
f(x)=x(1-x)
.
f
is a concave function. We have for any
i\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\Pr(\pi_{k}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\Pr\left(\pi_{k}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star}\;\big|\;{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\right)\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\Pr(\pi_{k}^{(i)}=\pi,\pi^{\star}\neq\pi\;\big|\;{\mathcal{D}}_{k})\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}f\left(\Pr(\pi^{\star}=\pi\;\big|\;{\mathcal{D}}_{k})\right)\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[f\left(\Pr\left(\pi^{\star}=\pi\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\right)\right)\right]\right]\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}f\left(\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Pr\left(\pi^{\star}=\pi\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\right)\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}f\left(\Pr\left(\pi^{\star}=\pi\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\right)\right)\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi}\Pr(\pi_{1}^{(i)}=\pi,\pi^{\star}\neq\pi\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr(\pi_{1}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\Pr(\pi_{k}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})\leq\Pr(\pi_{1}^{(i)}\neq\pi^{\star})

Lemma 4.7.3
.

For any policy
\pi
, let an event
E_{1}:=\left\{k,s,a:\,n_{k}(s,a)<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<k}w_{j}(s,a)-H\ln\left(\frac{SAH}{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\right\}
for some
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, where
w_{h,j}(s,a)
is the probability of visiting the
(s,a)
pair at time
h
of episode
j
under the chosen policy, and
w_{j}(s,a)=\sum_{h}w_{h,j}(s,a)
is the sum of the probabilities of visiting the
(s,a)
pair in episode
j
. Then,
\Pr(E_{1})\leq\delta^{\prime}SAH=\delta
, where we set
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{SAH}
.

Proof 4.7.4
.

Consider a fixed
s\in{\mathcal{S}},a\in{\mathcal{A}},h\in[H]
, and denote the state and action visited in the
k^{\text{th}}
episode at step
h
as
s_{k,h}
and
a_{k,h}
respectively. We define
\mathcal{F}_{k}
to be the sigma-field induced by the first
k-1
episodes. Let
X_{k}
be the indicator whether
s,a
was observed in episode
k
at time
h
. The probability
\Pr(s=s_{k,h},a=a_{k,h}\,|\,s_{k,1},\pi_{k}^{(i)})
(for
i\in\{0,1\}
) of whether
X_{k}=1
is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-measurable and hence we can apply Lemma F.4 from
[
61
]
with
W=\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta^{\prime}}
and obtain that
\Pr(E_{1})\leq SAH\delta^{\prime}
after summing over all statements for
h\in[H]
and applying the union bound over
s,a,h
.

Lemma 4.7.5
.

For all estimates
\widehat{\theta}_{k}^{(i)},\widehat{\eta}_{k}^{(i)}
(
i\in\{0,1\}
) of
\;\theta,\eta\;
in any fixed episode
k\in[K]
, and for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
we have,

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left|{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta,\eta}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)-{\mathbb{P}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{k}^{(i)},\widehat{\eta}_{k}^{(i)}}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{4S\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta}\right)}{\sum_{j<k}w_{j}(s,a)-2H\ln\left(\frac{SAH}{\delta}\right)}}

Proof 4.7.6
.

[
223
]
gives the following high probability bound on the one norm of the maximum likelihood estimate using posterior sampling. In particular, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it holds that:

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left|{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta,\eta}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)-{\mathbb{P}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{k}^{(i)},\widehat{\eta}_{k}^{(i)}}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\right|\leq\sqrt{2S\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta}\right)/\,n_{k}(s,a)}\quad.

Then, using Lemma
4.7.3
, the proof is complete.

Lemma 4.7.7
.

Under Algorithm
7
for large enough K, every reachable state-action pair is visited infinitely-often.

Proof 4.7.8
.

The proof proceeds by assuming that there exists a state-action pair that is visited only finitely-many times. This assumption will lead to a contradiction
2
2

2

Note that in finite-horizon MDPs, the concept of visiting a state finitely-many times is not the same as that of a transient state in an infinite Markov chain, because: 1) due to a finite horizon, the state is resampled from the initial state distribution
\rho
every
H
timesteps, and 2) the policy—which determines which state-action pairs can be reached in an episode—is also resampled every
H
timesteps.
: once this state-action pair is no longer visited, the reward model posterior is no longer updated with respect to it. Then, Algorithm
7
is guaranteed to eventually sample a high enough reward for this state-action that the resultant policy will prioritize visiting it.

First we note that Algorithm
7
is guaranteed to reach at least one state-action pair infinitely often: given our problem’s finite state and action spaces, at least one state-action pair must be visited infinitely-often during execution of Algorithm
7
. If all state-actions are not visited infinitely-often, there must exist a state-action pair
(s,a)
such that
s
is visited infinitely-often, while
(s,a)
is not. Otherwise, if all actions are selected infinitely-often in all infinitely-visited states, the finitely-visited states are unreachable (in which case these states are irrelevant to the learning process and simple regret minimization, and can be ignored). Without loss of generality, we label this state-action pair
(s,a)
as
\widetilde{s}_{1}
. To reach a contradiction, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{s}_{1}
is visited infinitely-often.

Let
\bm{r}_{1}
be the reward vector with a reward of
1
in state-action pair
\widetilde{s}_{1}
and rewards of zero elsewhere. Let
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
be the policy that maximizes the expected number of visits to
\widetilde{s}_{1}
under dynamics
\widetilde{\eta}
and reward vector
\bm{r}_{1}
:

\begin{aligned} \pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})=\text{argmax}_{\pi}V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1},\pi),\quad\text{where},\quad V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r},\pi)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}\sim\rho\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{H}\overline{r}(s_{t},\pi(s_{t},t))\,\Big|\,s_{t+1}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\widetilde{\eta}},\bm{\overline{r}}=\bm{r}\right].\end{aligned}

where
V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1},\pi)
is the expected total reward of a length-
H
trajectory under
\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}
, and
\pi
, or equivalently (by definition of
\bm{r}_{1}
), the expected number of visits to state-action
\widetilde{s}_{1}
.

We next show that there exists a
\kappa>0
such that
\Pr(\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}))>\kappa
for all possible values of
\widetilde{\eta}
. That is, for any sampled parameters
\widetilde{\eta}
, the probability of selecting policy
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
is uniformly lower-bounded, implying that Algorithm
7
must eventually select
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
.

Let
\widetilde{r}_{j}
be the sampled reward associated with state-action pair
\widetilde{s}_{j}
in a particular Algorithm
7
episode, for each state-action
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, with
d=SA
. We show that conditioned on
\widetilde{\eta}
, there exists
v>0
such that if
\widetilde{r}_{1}
exceeds
\text{max}\{v\widetilde{r}_{2},v\widetilde{r}_{3},\ldots,v\widetilde{r}_{d}\}
, then policy iteration returns the policy
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
, which is the policy maximizing the expected amount of time spent in
\widetilde{s}_{1}
. This can be seen by setting
v:=\frac{H}{\kappa_{1}}
, where
\kappa_{1}
is the expected number of visits to
\widetilde{s}_{1}
under
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
. Under this definition of
v
, the event
\left\{\widetilde{r}_{1}\geq\text{max}\{v\widetilde{r}_{2},v\widetilde{r}_{3},\ldots,v\widetilde{r}_{d}\}\right\}
is equivalent to
\{\widetilde{r}_{1}\kappa_{1}\geq h\,\text{max}\{\widetilde{r}_{2},\widetilde{r}_{3},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{d}\}\}
; the latter inequality implies that given
\widetilde{\eta}
and
\bm{\widetilde{r}}
, the expected reward accumulated solely in state-action
\widetilde{s}_{1}
exceeds the reward gained by repeatedly (during all
H
time-steps) visiting the state-action pair in the set
\{\widetilde{s}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{s}_{d}\}
having the highest sampled reward. Clearly, in this situation, policy iteration results in the policy
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
.

Next we show that
v=\frac{h}{\kappa_{1}}
is continuous in the sampled dynamics
\widetilde{\eta}
by showing that
\kappa_{1}
is continuous in
\widetilde{\eta}
. Recall that
\kappa_{1}
is defined as expected number of visits to
\widetilde{s}_{1}
under
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
. This is equivalent to the expected reward for following
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
under dynamics
\widetilde{\eta}
and rewards
\bm{r}_{1}
:

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1},\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}))=\max_{\pi}V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1},\pi).

(4.10)

The value of any policy
\pi
is continuous in the transition dynamics parameters, and so
V(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1},\pi)
is continuous in
\widetilde{\eta}
. The maximum in (
4.10
) is taken over the finite set of deterministic policies; because a maximum over a finite number of continuous functions is also continuous,
\kappa_{1}
is continuous in
\widetilde{\eta}
.

Next, recall that a continuous function on a compact set achieves its maximum and minimum values on that set. The set of all possible dynamics parameters
\widetilde{\eta}
is such that for each state-action pair
j
,
\sum_{k=1}^{S}p_{jk}=1
and
p_{jk}\geq 0\,\forall\,k
; the set of all possible vectors
\widetilde{\eta}
is clearly closed and bounded, and hence compact. Therefore,
v
achieves its maximum and minimum values on this set, and so for any
\widetilde{\eta}
,
v\in[v_{\text{min}},v_{\text{max}}]
, where
v_{\text{min}}>0
(
v
is nonnegative by definition, and
v=0
is impossible, as it would imply that
\widetilde{s}_{1}
is unreachable).

Then,
\Pr(\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}))
can then be expressed in terms of
v
and the parameters of the reward posterior. Firstly,

\displaystyle\Pr(\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}))\geq\Pr(\widetilde{r}_{1}>\text{max}\{v\widetilde{r}_{2},\ldots,v\widetilde{r}_{d}\})\geq\prod_{j=2}^{d}\Pr(\widetilde{r}_{1}>v\widetilde{r}_{j})=\prod_{j=2}^{d}[1-\Pr(\widetilde{r}_{1}-v\widetilde{r}_{j}\leq 0)]

The posterior updates for the reward model are given by Equation (
4.4
), which is intractable to compute in closed form. Since we have
\theta_{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mu_{0},\Sigma_{0})
, we can use the result of Lemma 3 in Appendix of
[
154
]
. The remaining proof thereby follows, and as a result, there exists some
\kappa>0
such that
\Pr(\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1}))\geq\kappa>0
.

In consequence, Algorithm
7
is guaranteed to infinitely-often sample pairs
(\widetilde{\eta},\pi)
such that
\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
. As a result, Algorithm
7
infinitely-often samples policies that prioritize reaching
\widetilde{s}_{1}
as quickly as possible. Such a policy always takes action
a
in state
s
. Furthermore, because
s
is visited infinitely-often, either a)
p_{0}(s)>0
or b) the infinitely-visited state-action pairs include a path with a nonzero probability of reaching
s
. In case a), since the initial state distribution is fixed, the MDP will infinitely-often begin in state
s
under the policy
\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
, and so
\widetilde{s}_{1}
will be visited infinitely-often. In case b), due to Lemma 1 in Appendix of
[
154
]
, the transition dynamics parameters for state-actions along the path to
s
converge to their true values (intuitively, the algorithm knows how to reach
s
). In episodes with the policy
\pi=\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
, Algorithm
7
is thus guaranteed to reach
\widetilde{s}_{1}
infinitely-often. Since Algorithm
7
selects
\pi_{pi}(\widetilde{\eta},\bm{r}_{1})
infinitely-often, it must reach
\widetilde{s}_{1}
infinitely-often. This presents a contradiction, and so every state-action pair must be visited infinitely-often as the number of episodes tend to infinity.

Lemma 4.7.9
.

For any confidence
\delta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
, let
\delta_{2}\in(c,1)
with
c\in(0,1)
, be the probability that any optimal policy estimate
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
constructed from the offline preference dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
is
\varepsilon
-optimal with probability at least
(1-\delta_{2})
i.e.
\Pr\left(\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim\rho\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[V_{\theta,\eta,0}^{\pi^{\star}}(s)-V_{\theta,\eta,0}^{\widehat{\pi}^{\star}}(s)\right]>\varepsilon\right)<\delta_{2}
. Then, the simple Bayesian regret of the learner
\Upsilon
is upper bounded with probability of at least
1-3\delta_{1}
by,

\displaystyle\mathscr{SR}_{K}^{\Upsilon}(\pi^{\star}_{K+1},\pi^{\star})\leq\sqrt{\frac{10\delta_{2}S^{2}AH^{3}\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)+3\delta_{2}SAH^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2K\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}}

(4.11)

Proof 4.7.10
.

Let
\Theta:=(\theta,\eta)
denote the unknown true parameters of the MDP
{\mathcal{M}}
, and let
\widehat{\Theta}_{k}^{(i)}:=\left(\widehat{\theta}_{k}^{(i)},\widehat{\eta}_{k}^{(i)}\right)
denote the sampled reward and transition parameters at episode
k
, which are used to compute policy
\pi_{k}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Let
J_{\pi}^{\widetilde{\Theta}}:=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\Theta},\tau\sim\pi\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[r(\tau)]
denote the expected total reward for a trajectory sampled from policy
\pi
under some environment
\widetilde{\Theta}:=\left(\widetilde{\theta},\widetilde{\eta}\right)
. Then define for each
i\in\{0,1\}
,

Z_{k}(i):=J_{\pi^{\star}}^{\Theta}-J_{{\pi}_{k}^{(i)}}^{\Theta}-\varepsilon\quad;\quad\tilde{Z}_{k}(i):=J_{{\pi}_{k}^{(i)}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{k}^{(i)}}-J_{{\pi}_{k}^{(i)}}^{\Theta}-\varepsilon\quad;\quad I_{k}(i):={\mathbf{I}}\left\{J^{\Theta}_{\pi_{k}^{(i)}}\neq J^{\Theta}_{\pi^{\star}}-\varepsilon\right\}

First, note that
Z_{k}(i)=Z_{k}(i)I_{k}(i)
with probability 1. Then compute,

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[Z_{k}(i)-\tilde{Z}_{k}(i)I_{k}(i)]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(Z_{k}(i)-\tilde{Z}_{k}(i)\right)I_{k}(i)\right]=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\left(J_{\pi^{\star}}^{\Theta}-J_{{\pi}_{k}^{(i)}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{k}^{(i)}}\right)I_{k}(i)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[J_{\pi^{\star}}^{\Theta}{\mathbf{I}}\left\{J^{\Theta}_{\pi_{k}^{(i)}}\neq J^{\Theta}_{\pi^{\star}}-\varepsilon\right\}\right]-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[J_{{\pi}_{k}^{(i)}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{k}^{(i)}}{\mathbf{I}}\left\{J^{\Theta}_{\pi_{k}^{(i)}}\neq J^{\Theta}_{\pi^{\star}}-\varepsilon\right\}\right]=0,

where the last equality is true since
\Theta
and
\widehat{\Theta}_{k}^{(i)}
are independently identically distributed given
\mathcal{D}_{k}
. Therefore, we can write the simple Bayesian regret upper bounded by
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)I_{K+1}(0)]
. By Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)I_{K+1}(0)]\leq\sqrt{\left({\mathbb{E}}[I_{K+1}(0)^{2}]\right)\left({\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)^{2}]\right)}

Since
\pi^{\star}:=\argmax_{\pi}J^{\Theta}_{\pi}
, using Lemma
4.7.1
in conjunction with Appendix B of
[
87
]
, the first part can be bounded by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[I_{K+1}(0)^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(J^{\Theta}_{\pi_{K+1}^{(0)}}<J^{\Theta}_{\pi^{\star}}-\varepsilon\right)\leq\max_{i\in\{0,1\}}\Pr\left(J^{\Theta}_{\pi_{1}^{(i)}}<J^{\Theta}_{\pi^{\star}}-\varepsilon\right)\leq\delta_{2}.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\pi_{h}}^{\Theta}
be the Bellman operator at time
h
defined by
\mathcal{T}_{\pi_{h}}^{\Theta}V_{h+1}(s):=r_{\theta}(s_{h},a_{h})+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}(s^{\prime}){\mathbb{P}}_{\eta}(s^{\prime}|s_{h},\pi(s_{h}))
and
\mathcal{T}_{\pi_{H}}^{\Theta}V_{H+1}(s)=0
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
. Using Equation (6) of
[
155
]
(also see Lemma A.14 of
[
245
]
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\Theta,\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left[\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})-\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\Theta}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})\right]\right]

Recall that the instantaneous reward satisfies
r_{\theta}(\cdot,\cdot)\in[0,1]
. So we have with
a_{K+1,h}:=\pi_{K+1}(s_{K+1,h})
,

\begin{aligned} \left|\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})-\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\Theta}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})\right|\leq H\left\|{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\widehat{\eta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(\cdot\,|\,s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})-{\mathbb{P}}_{\eta}(\cdot\,|\,s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})\right\|_{1}^{1}\end{aligned}

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq H{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left[\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})-\mathcal{T}_{{\pi}_{h}^{K+1}}^{\Theta}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\Theta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(s_{K+1,h})\right]^{2}\right]

(Cauchy-Schwartz)

\displaystyle\leq H^{3}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left\|{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\widehat{\eta}_{K+1}^{(0)}}(\cdot\,|\,s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})-{\mathbb{P}}_{\eta}(\cdot\,|\,s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})\right\|_{1}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 5H^{3}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{4S\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)}{\sum_{j<K+1}w_{j}(s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})-2H\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}\right]

, where the last line holds with probability
(1-2\delta_{1})
from Lemma
4.7.5
and Section H.3 in
[
240
]
. Now it remains to bound
\sum_{j<K+1}w_{j}(s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})
, which is essentially the sum of probabilities of visiting the pair
(s_{K+1,h},a_{K+1,h})
before the
(K+1)^{\text{th}}
episode. We know from Appendix G of
[
240
]
(also see Appendix B of
[
104
]
) that with probability at least
(1-\delta^{\prime})
, for any state-action
(s,a)
in any episode
e
, we have
\frac{1}{4}w_{e}(s,a)\geq H\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta^{\prime}}+H\;.
Using this, we have with probability at least
(1-3\delta_{1})
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}_{K+1}(0)^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 5H^{3}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{4SH\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}}{4HK\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-2H\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{20S^{2}AH^{4}\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)+5SAH^{3}\varepsilon^{2}}{4HK\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-2H\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10S^{2}AH^{3}\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)+3SAH^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2K\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}\;.

Putting it all together, we have with probability at least
(1-3\delta_{1})
,

\mathscr{SR}_{K}^{\Upsilon}(\pi^{\star}_{K+1},\pi^{\star})\leq\sqrt{\frac{10\delta_{2}S^{2}AH^{3}\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)+3\delta_{2}SAH^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{2K\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta}}}

Theorem 4.7.11
.

For any confidence
\delta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
and offline preference dataset size
N>2
, the simple Bayesian regret of the learner
\Upsilon
is upper bounded with probability of at least
1-3\delta_{1}
by,

\displaystyle\mathscr{SR}_{K}^{\Upsilon}(\pi^{\star}_{K+1},\pi^{\star})\leq\sqrt{\frac{20\delta_{2}S^{2}AH^{3}\ln\left(\frac{2KSA}{\delta_{1}}\right)}{2K\left(1+\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}\right)-\ln\frac{SAH}{\delta_{1}}}}\;\;,\text{where}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\delta_{2}=2\exp\left(-N\left(1+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{N}{4}(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})^{3}\right)

Proof 4.7.12
.

Define an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{n}=\left\{\overline{Y}_{n}\neq\argmax_{i\in\{0,1\}}g_{\beta,\vartheta}\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(i)}\right)\right\}
, i.e. at the
n
-th index of the offline preference dataset, the rater preferred the suboptimal trajectory (wrt trajectory score
g(\cdot)
). Given the optimal trajectory parity at index
n
as
\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star}=\argmax_{i\in\{0,1\}}g_{\beta,\vartheta}(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(i)})
, we have,

\begin{aligned} \Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{n}\,|\,\beta,\vartheta)&=1-\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{c}\,|\,\beta,\vartheta)\\
&=1-\frac{g_{\beta,\vartheta}\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star}}\right)}{g_{\beta,\vartheta}\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star}}\right)+g_{\beta,\vartheta}\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)}\\
&=1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\beta\left\langle\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right),-\vartheta\right\rangle\right)}\\
&=1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\beta\left\langle\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right),\theta-\vartheta\right\rangle-\beta\left\langle\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right),\theta\right\rangle\right)}\\
&\leq 1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\beta\left\|\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)\right\|_{1}\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\infty}-\beta\left\langle\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right),\theta\right\rangle\right)}\\
&\leq 1-\frac{1}{1+\underbrace{\exp\left(\beta B\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}-\beta\left\langle\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right)-\phi\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}\right),\theta\right\rangle\right)}_{\clubsuit}}\end{aligned}

, where the last two lines use Hölder’s inequality, and bounded trajectory map assumption respectively. Since
\vartheta-\theta\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/\lambda^{2})
, using the Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz inequality bound
[
137
,
210
]
implies

\Pr\left(\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}\geq t\right)\leq 2d^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}\right)\,.

Set
t=\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}N)}/\lambda
and define an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\vartheta}:=\{\|\vartheta-\theta\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}N)}/\lambda\}
such that
P({\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\vartheta}^{c})\leq 1/N
. We apply Union Bound on the
\clubsuit
term, and decompose the entire right hand side term as:

\begin{aligned} \Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{n}\,|\,\beta,\vartheta)&\leq\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\beta B\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}N)}/\lambda+\beta\Delta_{\min}\right)}+\frac{1}{N}\\
&\leq\exp\left(-\beta B\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}N)}/\lambda-\beta\Delta_{\min}\right)+\frac{1}{N}:=\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\;.\end{aligned}

(4.13)

Now we need to provide conditions on the rater’s competence, in terms of
(\lambda,\beta)
to be a valid expert i.e. for
\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\in(0,1)
. Let
k_{1}=\beta B,k_{2}=\frac{2\ln\left(2d^{1/2}\right)}{\lambda^{2}},k_{3}=2/\lambda^{2}
, and
k_{4}=\beta\Delta_{\min}
. We then have,

\displaystyle\exp\left(-k_{1}\sqrt{k_{2}+k_{3}\ln N}-k_{4}\right)+\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle<1

\displaystyle-k_{1}\sqrt{k_{2}+k_{3}\ln N}-k_{4}<\ln N

\displaystyle\left(\ln N\right)^{2}+(2k_{4}-k_{1}^{2}k_{3})\ln N+(k_{4}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}k_{2})>0

The above is a quadratic inequality that holds for all
N>1
if
\beta
is large enough i.e.

\text{If}\;\beta>\frac{2\ln\left(2d^{1/2}\right)}{\left|B\lambda^{2}-2\Delta_{\min}\right|},\text{then,}\;\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\in(0,1)\,.

Now, since we have Lemma
4.7.7
, the remaining argument shows to find a separation of probability between two types of states and time index pairs under the rater’s preference, parameterized by
\beta
and
\lambda
, and the offline dataset, characterized by its size
N
: the ones that are probable under the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
, and the ones that are not. We have two cases:

Case I.

p^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}(s)>0

In this case, we want the rater to prefer trajectories that are most likely to occur under the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
. Given
p^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}(s)>0
, we now lower bound the probability of the state
s
having
c_{h}(s)\equiv\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}c_{h}(s,a)>0
i.e.

\displaystyle\Pr(c_{h}(s)>0)

\displaystyle=\Pr\left(\sum_{a}w_{h}(s,a)>\sum_{a}l_{h}(s,a)\right)=1-\Pr\left(\sum_{a}w_{h}(s,a)\leq\sum_{a}l_{h}(s,a)\right)

(4.14)

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{t=0}^{H}\Pr\left(\sum_{a}w_{h}(s,a)\leq t\right)\cdot\Pr\left(\sum_{a}l_{h}(s,a)=t\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\sum_{t=1}^{H+1}\Pr\left(\sum_{a}w_{h}(s,a)<t\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}/(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})

, where the last step uses Equation (
4.13
).

Case II.

p^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}(s)=0

In this case, we wish to upper bound the probability of the rater preferring trajectories (and hence, states) which are unlikely to occur under the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
. If for some state
s
we have
p^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}(s)=0
but also this state
s\in\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1-\overline{Y}_{n}^{\star})}
, this means the rater preferred the suboptimal trajectory. Similar to the proof above we conclude that,

\Pr(c_{h}(s)>0)=\Pr\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N}\{{\mathcal{E}}_{n}\}\,|\,\beta,\vartheta\right)\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Pr_{\theta}({\mathcal{E}}_{n}\,|\,\beta,\vartheta)\leq\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}/(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})

(4.15)

The above argument shows that there’s a probability gap between parity of states and time index pairs under the rater’s preference model: the ones that are probable under the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
and the ones that are not i.e. we have shown that the states which are
more
likely to be visited under
\pi^{\star}
have a lower bound on the probability of being part of preferred trajectories, and also the states which are
less
likely to be visited by
\pi^{\star}
have an upper bound on the probability of being part of preferred trajectories by the rater. Using this decomposition, we will show that when the rater tends to an expert (
\beta\to\infty,\lambda\to\infty
) and size
N
of the offline preference dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
is large, we can distinguish the two types of state and time index pairs through their net counts in
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. This will allow us to construct an
\varepsilon
-optimal estimate of
\pi^{\star}
with probability at least
(1-\delta_{2})
. Noticing the structure of Equation (
4.6
), we see that the upper bound is minimized when
\varepsilon\to 0
, i.e. we construct an
optimal
estimate of the optimal policy from the offline preference dataset. We now upper bound the probability that the estimated optimal policy
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
is
not
the optimal policy.

Let
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
be the optimal estimator of
\pi^{\star}
constructed with probability at least
(1-\delta_{2})
. Based on the separability of states and time index pairs, we have four possible events for each
(s,h)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times[H]
pair. For
\delta=(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})/2
, we have,

1.

E_{1}:=\{p_{h}^{\pi^{\star}}(s)>0
and
c_{h}(s)<\delta N\}
;

2.

E_{2}:=\{p_{h}^{\pi^{\star}}(s)>0
and
c_{h}(s)\geq\delta N
, but
\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)=a_{h}^{\star}(s)\neq\argmax_{a}c_{h}(s,a)=\widehat{\pi}_{h}^{\star}(s)\}
.

3.

E_{3}:=\{p_{h}^{\pi^{\star}}(s)=0
and
c_{h}(s)\geq\delta N\}
;

4.

E_{4}:=\{p_{h}^{\pi^{\star}}(s)=0
and
c_{h}(s)<\delta N\}
;

Denoting the the event to occur with high probability as
{\mathcal{T}}
i.e.
{\mathcal{T}}:=\{r_{\theta}(s_{h},\pi_{h}^{\star}(s_{h}))-r_{\theta}(s_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{\star}_{h}(s_{h}))\leq 0\}
.
If we can show that
\Pr({\mathcal{T}}\,|\,E_{i})>1-\delta_{2}
for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, then union bound implies that
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
is not optimal with probability at most
\delta_{2}
.

1.

Under the event
E_{1}
.
Let
b\sim\mathrm{Bin}(T,q)
denote a binomial random variable with parameters
T\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
q\in[0,1]
. Notice that the each
c_{h}(s)
is the difference of two binomial random variables
b_{1}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(N,1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})
and
b_{2}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(N,\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})
. This implies that
c_{h}(s)+N\sim\mathrm{Bin}(2N,1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})
. We then have,

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{T}}^{c}\,|\,E_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\Pr(c_{h}(s)<\delta N)\leq\Pr\left(\mathrm{Bin}(2N,1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})<(1+\delta)N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-4N\left(\delta+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(-N\left(1+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)

2.

Under the event
E_{2}
.
Given Equation (
4.14
), we have that,

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{T}}^{c}\,|\,E_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(\argmax_{a\in U^{W}_{h}(s)}Q^{\pi^{\star}}_{\theta,\eta,h}(s,a)\neq\argmax_{a\in U^{W}_{h}(s)}Q^{\pi^{\star}}_{\theta,\eta,h}(s,a)\,|\,E_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(c_{h}\left(s,\argmax_{a\in U^{W}_{h}(s)}Q^{\pi^{\star}}_{\theta,\eta,h}(s,a)\right)\leq c_{h}(s)/2\,|\,E_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(\mathrm{Bin}(c_{h}(s),1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})\geq c_{h}(s)/2\,|\,E_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\exp\left(-2c_{h}(s)(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}-c_{h}(s)/2)^{2}\right)\right]\rvert_{c_{h}(s)=\delta N}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{N}{4}(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})^{3}\right)

3.

Under the event
E_{3}
.
Similar to event
E_{1}
, we have,

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{T}}^{c}\,|\,E_{3})

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(\mathrm{Bin}(2N,1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})>(1+\delta)N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-4N\left(\delta+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(-N\left(1+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)

4.

Under the event
E_{4}
.
Under this event, notice that conditioned on
\theta
, we have

\displaystyle r_{\theta}(s_{h},\pi_{h}^{\star}(s_{h}))-r_{\theta}(s_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{\star}_{h}(s_{h}))

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[r_{\theta}(s_{h},\pi_{h}^{\star}(s_{h}))-r_{\theta}(s_{h},\widehat{\pi}^{\star}_{h}(s))\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathring{a}\sim{\mathcal{A}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[r_{\theta}(s_{h},\mathring{a})-r_{\theta}(s_{h},\mathring{a})\right]\right]=0

This means that
\Pr({\mathcal{T}}\,|\,E_{4})
is a non-failure event that occurs with probability 1 i.e.
\Pr({\mathcal{T}}^{c}\,|\,E_{4})=0
.

Combining all of the above, we have for
N>2
,

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left(2\exp\left(-N\left(1+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{N}{4}(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})^{3}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\delta_{2}

Using Lemma
4.4.1
, the proof is complete.

4.7.2

Results for Bandits

For the bandit setting we let the action set be
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with number of arms be
A=|{\mathcal{A}}|
, online episodes (rounds) be
K
, and offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}=\left\{\left(\overline{a}^{(0)}_{n},\overline{a}^{(1)}_{n},Y_{n}\right)\right\}_{n=1}^{N}
of size
N
, where
\overline{a}^{(0)}_{n},\overline{a}^{(1)}_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Also let
\mu_{\text{min}}(\cdot)\in(0,1)
be the minimum action sampling distribution during construction of
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
.

Letting
\Delta_{\text{min}}=\min_{n\in[N]}\left|r_{\theta}(\overline{a}^{(0)}_{n})-r_{\theta}(\overline{a}^{(1)}_{n})\right|
, where
\theta
is the underlying reward model of the environment with
r_{\theta}(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and
\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}
to be error upper bound of the rater’s preference (similar to Lemma
4.4.3
), we have the following result for simple regret of the learner
\Upsilon
, where simple regret is defined as
\mathscr{SR}_{K}^{\Upsilon}(\pi_{K+1}^{\star},\pi^{\star})=r_{\theta}(a^{\star})-r_{\theta}(a^{\star}_{K+1})
, where
\pi^{\star}
is the optimal policy that picks the optimal action
a^{\star}=\argmax_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}r_{\theta}(a)
, and
\pi_{K+1}^{\star}
is the policy of the learner after
K
online rounds. We shall use
A^{\star}
and
a^{\star}
interchangebly to refer to the optimal action.

Analogous to the winning and undecided subsets of Section
3.4
, we construct an
information
subset of
{\mathcal{A}}
, call it
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
such that
\Pr(a^{\star}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}})\geq 1-\epsilon
, where
\epsilon\in(0,1)
is the
error
probability. As an algorithmic choice, we let
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
consist of actions that have been preferred to
at least
once in the offline preference dataset and of actions that do not appear in
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. Given this construction, we have the following result.

Theorem 4.7.13
.

For any confidence
\delta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
and offline preference dataset size
N>2
, the simple Bayesian regret of the learner
\Upsilon
is upper bounded with probability of at least
1-3\delta_{1}
by,

\displaystyle\mathscr{SR}_{K}^{\Upsilon}(\pi^{\star}_{K+1},\pi^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{20\delta_{2}A\ln\left(\frac{2KA}{\delta_{1}}\right)}{2K\left(1+\ln\frac{A}{\delta_{1}}\right)-\ln\frac{A}{\delta_{1}}}}\quad\text{with},

(4.16)

\displaystyle\;\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\beta B\sqrt{2\ln(2d^{1/2}N)}/\lambda-\beta\Delta_{\min}\right)+\frac{1}{N}\;\;\text{, and}

\displaystyle\;\delta_{2}

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-N\left(1+\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N}\right)^{2}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{N}{4}(1-\gamma_{\beta,\lambda,N})^{3}\right).

For a fixed
N>2
, and large
A
, the simple regret bound is
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\sqrt{AK^{-1}}\right)
. Note that this bound converges to zero exponentially fast as
N\to\infty
and as the rater tends to an expert (large
\beta,\lambda
). In addition, as the number of online episodes
K
gets large,
\mathsf{PSPL}
is able to identify the best policy (arm) with probability at least
(1-3\delta_{1})
.

4.7.3

Constructing Surrogate Loss Function

Lemma 4.7.14
.

At episode
k
, the MAP estimate of
(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)
can be constructed by solving the following equivalent optimization problem:

\displaystyle(\theta_{opt},\vartheta_{opt},\eta_{opt})

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\;\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{k})

(4.17)

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmin}\;{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)\;,\text{where},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\vphantom{\int_{1}^{2}}\left[\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(Y_{t})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\ln\Pr_{\eta}\left({s}_{t,h+1}^{(j)}\,|\,{s}_{t,h}^{(j)},{a}_{t,h}^{(j)}\right)\vphantom{\int_{1}^{2}}\right],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left[\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)\right],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}-SA\sum_{i=1}^{S}(\bm{\alpha}_{0,i}-1)\ln\eta_{i}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0}).

Proof 4.7.15
.

We first analyze the posterior distribution of
\vartheta,\theta,\eta
given the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
at the beginning of episode
k
, and then optimize it by treating these random variables as parameters.

\displaystyle\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\;\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\;\Pr({\mathcal{D}}_{k}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta,\eta)\cdot\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

(4.18)

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\;\ln\Pr({\mathcal{D}}_{k}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta,\eta)+\ln\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\underbrace{\ln\Pr({\mathcal{H}}_{k}\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{0},\theta,\vartheta,\eta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}+\underbrace{\ln\Pr({\mathcal{D}}_{0}\,|\,\theta,\vartheta,\eta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}+\underbrace{\ln\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{3}}

Then,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\ln\Pr\bigg(\left({\tau}_{t}^{(0)},{\tau}_{t}^{(1)},Y_{t}\right)\,\big|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{t},\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)

(4.19)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\ln\Pr\bigg(Y_{t}\,\big|\,{\tau}_{t}^{(0)},{\tau}_{t}^{(1)},\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)+\ln\Pr\bigg({\tau}_{t}^{(0)},{\tau}_{t}^{(1)}\;\big|\;{\mathcal{D}}_{t},\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\left[\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(Y_{t})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\ln\Pr_{\eta}\left({s}_{t,h+1}^{(j)}\,|\,{s}_{t,h}^{(j)},{a}_{t,h}^{(j)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\ln\Pr\bigg(\left(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)},\overline{Y}_{n}\right)\,\big|\,\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\ln\Pr\bigg(\overline{Y}_{n}\,\big|\,\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)},\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)+\underbrace{\ln\Pr\bigg(\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)}\;\big|\;\theta,\vartheta,\eta\bigg)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\textbf{indep. of $\theta,\vartheta,\eta\;\Rightarrow\;$ constant}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\text{constant}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\ln\Pr(\vartheta\,|\,\theta)+\ln\Pr(\theta)+\ln\Pr(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\ln\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\lambda^{2}}\bigg)-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\big(|2\pi\Sigma_{0}|\big)-\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0})+SA\sum_{i=1}^{S}(\bm{\alpha}_{0,i}-1)\ln\eta_{i}.

Hence, final surrogate loss function is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)+{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta),\qquad\text{where}

(4.20)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\left[\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(Y_{t})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle{\tau}_{t}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\ln\Pr_{\eta}\left({s}_{t,h+1}^{(j)}\,|\,{s}_{t,h}^{(j)},{a}_{t,h}^{(j)}\right)\right]\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left[\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(\overline{Y}_{n})},\vartheta\rangle-\ln\bigg(e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)},\vartheta\rangle}+e^{\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)},\vartheta\rangle}\bigg)\right]\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{3}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\left\|\theta-\vartheta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\mu_{0})^{T}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\theta-\mu_{0})-SA\sum_{i=1}^{S}(\bm{\alpha}_{0,i}-1)\ln\eta_{i}.

Finally the problem becomes equivalent as follows:

(\theta_{opt},\vartheta_{opt},\eta_{opt})=\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmax}\;\Pr(\theta,\vartheta,\eta\,|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{k})\equiv\underset{\theta,\vartheta,\eta}{\argmin}\;{\mathcal{L}}(\theta,\vartheta,\eta)

(4.21)

4.7.4

Practical PSPL (cont.)

Estimating Rater Competence in Practice

There are two main methods of estimating rater competence in practice:

1.

Based on maximum likelihood estimation (MLE). Similar idea has been proposed to estimate the expertise level in imitation learning
[
26
,
24
]
.

2.

The second method is to simply look at the entropy of the empirical distribution of the action in the offline dataset. Suppose the empirical distribution of
\zeta
. Then we use
c/\mathcal{H}(\zeta)
as an estimation for
\beta
, where
c>0
is a hyperparameter. The intuition is that for smaller
\beta
, the net state-action pair visit counts tend to be more uniform and thus the entropy will be larger. This is an unsupervised approach and agnostic to specific offline data generation processes. The knowledgeability
\lambda
is not quite ‘estimable’ because for a single environment, even though we know the true environment
\theta
and the expert’s knowledge
\vartheta
, we only have one pair of observations generated with the same
\vartheta
. Thus, the variance of the estimation for
\lambda
could be infinite. However, exact estimation of
\lambda
is not necessary as we show that the algorithm is robust to misspecified
\lambda
through experiments in Section
2.4
.

Ablation Studies

The Bootstrapped
\mathsf{PSPL}
algorithm in Section
4.5
requires a knowledge of rater’s parameters
\lambda,\beta
. We study the sensitivity of the algorithm’s performance to mis-specification of these parameters.

(i)
Different Preference Generation Expert Policy.
Though the learning agent assumes Equation (
4.2
) as the expert’s generative model, we consider it to use a deterministic greedy policy. Trajectories
\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(0)}
and
\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(1)}
are sampled, and then choose
\overline{Y}_{n}=\argmax_{i\in\{0,1\}}\beta\langle\overline{\tau}_{n}^{(i)},\vartheta\rangle
, where
\vartheta\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\theta,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/\lambda^{2})
. In Figure
4.5
, we see that even when the learning agent’s assumption of the rater policy is flawed,
\mathsf{PSPL}
significantly outperforms the baselines.

(ii)
Misspecified Competence parameters.
First, we generate offline data with true
\lambda=10^{3}
but
\mathsf{PSPL}
uses a misspecified
\lambda
. Second, we generate offline data with true
\beta=10
but
\mathsf{PSPL}
uses a misspecified
\beta
. Figure
4.5
shows that although the performance of
\mathsf{PSPL}
decreases as the degree of flawness increases, it still outperforms the baselines.

RiverSwim

RiverSwim

Figure 4.5
:
Sensitivity to flawed expert policy with
\lambda=\{10,10^{3}\}
, and misspecified
competence.

Experiments on Image Generation Tasks (cont.)

We instantiate our framework on the Pick-a-Pic dataset of human preferences for text–to–image generation
[
111
]
. Overall, the dataset contains over 500,000 examples and 35,000 distinct prompts. Each example contains a prompt, sequence generations of two images, and a label for which image is preferred. We let each generation be a trajectory, so the dataset contains trajectory preferences
\mathcal{D}_{0}={(\tau_{i}^{+},\tau_{i}^{-},y_{i})}_{i=1}^{N}
with
y_{i}=1
iff
\tau_{i}^{+}\succ\tau_{i}^{-}
. Each trajectory
\tau=(p,z_{0:T})
is the entire latent denoising chain of length
T
for prompt
p
sampled from some prompt distribution.

Following
[
33
,
253
]
, text–to–image generation is a finite–horizon MDP
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P)
:
s_{t}=(p,z_{t}),a_{t}=\epsilon_{t},z_{t+1}=f_{\rho}(z_{t},\epsilon_{t}),
where
z_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the latent,
a_{t}
is the noise
\epsilon_{t}
predicted by the policy
\pi_{\theta}(a_{t}\mid s_{t})
, and
f_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is the deterministic DDPM transition with frozen scheduler
\rho
. Please see
[
33
]
for a comprehensive and detailed discussion on modeling diffusion as a MDP; we follow the same approach. The episode horizon is
H=T=50
. For each trajectory, we adopt the additive embedding as discussed in the paper:
\phi(\tau)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\phi(s_{t},a_{t})
with
\phi(s_{t},a_{t})=\bigl[\text{CLIP}(p,\text{Dec}(z_{T})),\lVert\epsilon_{t}\rVert_{2},t/T\bigr],
where
\text{Dec}(\cdot)
is the standard VAE decoder shipped with Stable‐Diffusion
[
172
]
. As in the paper, the model assumes that rater of competence
\lambda,\beta
follows the Bradley-Terry model i.e.
\Pr(Y=1\mid\tau^{+},\tau^{-},\vartheta,\beta)=\sigma\bigl(\beta\langle\vartheta,\phi(\tau^{+})-\phi(\tau^{-})\rangle\bigr)
, where
\sigma(\cdot)
is the sigmoid link function.

To model the reward parameters, we let the uninformed prior be
\nu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{0},\Sigma_{0})
,
\mu_{0}=0
, and diagonal
\Sigma_{0}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{\text{clip}}^{2},\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2},\sigma_{\text{time}}^{2})
, where
\sigma_{\text{clip}}^{2},\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}
are empirical variances from
10
k random chains and
\sigma_{\text{time}}^{2}=1/12
. Regarding modeling of transitions, the physical scheduler
f_{\rho}
is known; uncertainty remains only in the
\ell_{2}
-norm of
\epsilon_{t}
. We discretize this norm into
C{=}10
bins as
b_{i}
for
i\in[C]
and model
P(b_{i}\mid s_{t})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}),\;\;\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{C}.
The resulting Dirichlet counts are updated from both
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and online episodes.

Since we do not know the optimal generation sequence, minimizing simple regret is equivalent to maximizing expected reward in the final online episode. To evaluate this final generation, we conduct
L=10
rollouts and compute the average reward with a weighted ensemble of automatic quality metrics, similar to
[
53
,
232
]
i.e.
r_{\theta}(\cdot)=0.7*\text{ImageReward--v2}+0.3*\text{Aesthetic--LAION}
where ImageReward–v2 is a reward model from
[
232
]
, and Aesthetic–LAION is a reward model hosted on HuggingFace
[
72
]
. For evaluation, we sweep
N_{\text{off}}=50
k preference triplets for the prior, reserve
N_{\text{val}}=15
k examples for evaluation, and create an exploration pool of
10
k unseen examples. For implementation, we use a 2 layer MLP, 512 GELU units, and outputs
(\mu_{\theta})_{t}
and
\log((\sigma_{\theta})_{t})
, where
(\mu_{\theta})_{t}
is the predicted noise vector the agent believes will best denoise
z_{t}
and
(\sigma_{\theta})_{t}
controls exploratory perturbations around that prediction, enabling posterior sampling for PSPL. This is analogous to solving the unconstrained optimization Problem (
4.9
) in the function approximation setting.

For the online phase, we use the above reward model
r_{\theta}(\cdot)
for the BT preference model (see Equation (
4.2
)), with an expert rater (i.e.
\lambda,\beta\to\infty
), similar to
[
111
]
. Since, we show that PSPL is robust to mis-specifications in rater competence (please see Appendix
4.7.4
), we use an expert rater for ease of comparison.

Training details.
Following
[
253
]
, we first cluster the prompts in the dataset to obtain a mapping from
\text{cluster}_{j}\to({\mathcal{D}}_{0})_{j}
, where
j\in[J]
is the cluster index out of J clusters, and
({\mathcal{D}}_{0})_{j}
is the dataset of trajectories and corresponding preference labels for prompts in prompt
\text{cluster}_{j}
i.e.
({\mathcal{D}}_{0})_{j}={(\tau_{j,i}^{+},\tau_{j,i}^{-},y_{j,i})}_{i=1}^{N_{j}}
, where
\tau_{j,\cdot}^{+}
and
\tau_{j,\cdot}^{-}
are the
winning
and
losing
trajectory generations given a prompt from
\text{cluster}_{j}
for all
j\in[J]
. For tractability, we compress the training images to be 128
\times
128 pixels, and optimize for
K=100k
episodes for each cluster
j\in[J]
. Future direction of this work will consider incorporating prompt information as a prior to the MLP, resulting in prompt conditioned inference. However, that is beyond the current scope of the paper. Finally, all experiments are run on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5080, GPU 16GB, and Memory DDR5 64 GB. Training times for all algorithms are given in Table
4.1
and comprehensive validation results are shown in Figure
4.6
.

Table 4.1
:
Average training times of baselines over 5 independent runs.

Training Time (h)

\widehat{r}_{\theta}(\cdot)
over 5 independent runs. Images are enlarged for clarity.

4.8

Auxiliary Definitions and Lemmas

Lemma 4.8.1
.

Let
X
be the sum of
L
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with mean
p\in(0,1)
. Let
q\in(0,1)
, then

\displaystyle\Pr(X\leq qL)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-2L(q-p)^{2}\right),\qquad\text{if }q<p,

\displaystyle\Pr(X\geq qL)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-2L(q-p)^{2}\right),\qquad\text{if }q>p.

Proof 4.8.2
.

Both inequalities can be obtained by applying Hoeffding’s Inequality (see
4.8.10
).

Definition 4.8.3
.

\alpha
-dependence in
[
174
]
. For
\alpha>0
and function class
\mathcal{Z}
whose elements are with domain
\mathcal{X}
, an element
x\in\mathcal{X}
is
\alpha
-dependent on the set
\mathcal{X}_{n}:=\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\right\}\subset\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\mathcal{Z}
, if any pair of functions
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
with
\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(z\left(x_{i}\right)-z^{\prime}\left(x_{i}\right)\right)^{2}}\leqslant

\alpha
satisfies
z(x)-z^{\prime}(x)\leqslant\alpha
. Otherwise,
x
is
\alpha
-independent on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
if it does not satisfy the condition.

Definition 4.8.4
.

Eluder dimension in
[
174
]
. For
\alpha>0
and function class
\mathcal{Z}
whose elements are with domain
\mathcal{X}
, the Eluder dimension
\operatorname{dim}_{E}(\mathcal{Z},\alpha)
, is defined as the length of the longest possible sequence of elements in
\mathcal{X}
such that for some
\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant\alpha
, every element is
\alpha^{\prime}
independent of its predecessors.

Definition 4.8.5
.

Covering number.
Given two functions
l
and
u
, the bracket
[l,u]
is the set of all functions
f
satisfying
l\leq f\leq u
. An
\alpha
-bracket is a bracket
[l,u]
with
\|u-l\|<

\alpha
. The covering number
N_{[\cdot]}(\mathcal{F},\alpha,\|\cdot\|)
is the minimum number of
\alpha
-brackets needed to cover
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 4.8.6
.

(Linear Preference Models Eluder dimension and Covering number).
For the case of
d
-dimensional generalized trajectory linear feature models
r_{\xi}\left(\xi_{H}\right):=\left\langle\phi\left(\xi_{H}\right),\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\rangle
, where
\phi:
Traj
\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{dim_{\mathbb{T}}}
is a known
dim_{\mathbb{T}}
dimension feature map satisfying
\left\|\psi\left(\xi_{H}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq B
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an unknown parameter with
\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{w}
. Then the
\alpha
-Eluder dimension of
r_{\xi}(\xi_{H})
is at most
\mathcal{O}(dim_{\mathbb{T}}\log(B\kappa_{w}/\alpha))
. The
\alpha
- covering number is upper bounded by
\left(\frac{1+2B\kappa_{w}}{\alpha}\right)^{dim_{\mathbb{T}}}
.

Let
\left(X_{p},Y_{p}\right)_{p=1,2,\ldots}
be a sequence of random elements,
X_{p}\in X
for some measurable set
X
and
Y_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a subset of the set of real-valued measurable functions with domain
X
. Let
\mathbb{F}=\left(\mathbb{F}_{p}\right)_{p=0,1,\cdots}
be a filtration such that for all
p\geq 1,\left(X_{1},Y_{1},\cdots,X_{p-1},Y_{p-1},X_{p}\right)
is
\mathbb{F}_{p-1}
measurable and such that there exists some function
f_{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{p}\mid\mathbb{F}_{p-1}\right]=f_{*}\left(X_{p}\right)
holds for all

. The (nonlinear) least square predictor given

\widehat{f}_{t}=\operatorname{argmin}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{p=1}^{t}\left(f\left(X_{p}\right)-Y_{p}\right)^{2}
. We say that

(4.22)

Theorem 4.8.10
.

Hoeffding’s inequality
[
91
]
.
Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random variables that are sub-Gaussian with parameter
\sigma
. Define
S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
. Then, for any
t>0
, Hoeffding’s inequality provides an upper bound on the tail probabilities of
S_{n}
, which is given by:

\Pr\left(|S_{n}-\mathbb{E}[S_{n}]|\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n\sigma^{2}}\right).

This result emphasizes the robustness of the sum
S_{n}
against deviations from its expected value, particularly useful in applications requiring high confidence in estimations from independent sub-Gaussian observations.
