5.4.3

Secondary Evaluation

We now take a deeper dive into
MOPO
’s performance and discuss its dependence on various factors.

Verbosity-constrained tasks.
Consider tasks in which the lengths of LLM responses need to be contained in the range
[l_{\text{low}},l_{\text{high}}]
to control verbosity (while maintaining helpfulness); for example, in summarization tasks
[
133
]
. In this case, the natural choice for reward functions is to directly constrain the rewards
r_{1}(y|\cdot)=|y|\geq l_{\text{low}}
and
r_{2}(y|\cdot)=-|y|\geq-l_{\text{high}}
. We illustrate the distributions of the generated response lengths (in tokens) by Zephyr-7b-beta aligned using
MOPO
, and report the corresponding helpfulness scores in Figure
5.7
. We observe that the mean response lengths are in the required range in each case, satisfying the imposed constraints while improving helpfulness.

Effect of regularization.

MOPO
employs a
lagged
reference policy to impose a dynamic KL-regularization. This moving baseline stabilizes gradient estimates and prevents overfitting, allowing maximization of multiple reward functions and extension towards the Pareto frontier. In contrast, DPO variants use a static reference and tend to overfit irrespective of regularization
[
78
,
235
]
.
MOPO
’s adaptive reference updates yield more robust multi-objective alignment as seen in Figure
5.8
.

Sensitivity analysis.
As discussed before, the updates in
MOPO
are governed by optimization hyperparameters such as the constraint relaxation factor
\beta
and the lag interval
t_{0}
. In practice, robustness to these hyperparameters is desirable, as their tuning can significantly affect performance. Figure
5.8
shows that
MOPO
remains stable and effective even under suboptimal choices of
\beta
and
t_{0}
.

5.5

Conclusion

In this chapter, we introduced
MOPO
, an offline, multi-objective constrained optimization algorithm that learns from preference data and maximizes a primary objective while enforcing tunable lower bound constraints on secondary objectives. On synthetic benchmarks
MOPO
accurately recovers the true Pareto front. Experiments on real-world datasets show that
MOPO
matches or surpasses baselines, and ablation studies prove robustness to hyperparameters. An important future direction is to develop a rigorous theoretical analysis of
MOPO
to provably advance the field of RLHF.

5.6

Appendix

Definition 5.6.1
.

For any two policies
P,Q\in\Delta_{{\mathcal{Y}}}^{{\mathcal{X}}}
such that
Supp
(P)
=
Supp
(
Q
), their
\mathsf{KL}
divergence is defined as:

\mathsf{KL}\left(P\,||\,Q\right)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\nu\\
y\sim P(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\log\left(\frac{P(y\,|\,x)}{Q(y\,|\,x)}\right)\right]\;.

5.6.1

A Detailed Motivating Example

In this example, we empirically demonstrate the necessity of
principled
multi-objective optimization methods that account for multi-dimensional preferences. We benchmark various approaches for multi-objective alignment and show that existing state-of-the-art techniques consistently fail to reach the Pareto front. To ensure clarity, we conduct experiments on synthetic datasets where the true Pareto front is known, allowing for precise evaluation of alignment quality.

Now define the input space
{\mathcal{X}}:={\mathcal{U}}[0,1]
and output space
{\mathcal{Y}}:={\mathcal{U}}[0,1]
. For input
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and outputs
y,y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}
, the Bradley-Terry preference model
\mathsf{BT}(r(\cdot))

[
38
]
w.r.t. to a reward model
r(\cdot)
provides preference
z
as,

\Pr(z=y\,\,|\,\,x,y,y^{\prime})=r(x,y)/\left(r(x,y)+r(x,y^{\prime})\right)

(5.11)

Reward models.

For
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
, we consider two pairs of reward functions
r:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
:

\displaystyle r_{1}^{A}(x,y)=e^{x}+\sqrt{y}-y\quad\text{and}\quad r_{2}^{A}(x,y)=-\sin(x)-y^{2}.

\displaystyle r_{1}^{B}(x,y)=(x+y)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad r_{2}^{B}(x,y)=\log(\frac{1+x}{1+y}).

(5.12)

Dataset construction.

For arbitrary
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, generate
x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\sim{\mathcal{U}}[0,1]
for
i\in[N]
. Let
z_{i}^{(1)}\sim\mathsf{BT}(r_{1})
and
z_{i}^{(2)}\sim\mathsf{BT}(r_{2})
with
z_{i}^{(1)},z_{i}^{(2)}\in\{y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\}
. We now construct four datasets: (i)
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
=
\{(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},z_{i}^{(1)})\}_{i=1}^{N}
incorporating preferences w.r.t. reward model
r_{1}(\cdot)
only, (ii)
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
=
\{(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},z_{i}^{(2)})\}_{i=1}^{N}
incorporating preferences w.r.t. reward model
r_{2}(\cdot)
only, (iii)
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
=
\{(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},z):z=z_{i}^{(1)}=z_{i}^{(2)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
incorporating preferences only if they are consistent with reward models
r_{1}(\cdot)
and
r_{2}(\cdot)
, and (iv)
{\mathcal{D}}_{C}
=
\{(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},z_{i}^{(C)}):z_{i}^{(C)}\sim\mathsf{BT}(wr_{1}+(1-w)r_{2})\}_{i=1}^{N}
for some
w\in[0,1]
, which incorporates preferences based on some convex weighting of both reward models.

Another approach of solving the multi-objective preference problem is a constrained optimization approach
COP
, where we can solve for the optimal policy as
\pi_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.2871,0.4949}{\small COP}}}}(x)=\argmax_{y}\;r_{1}(x,y)
,
\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;r_{2}(x,y)\geq b
for some
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Now, given the four datasets and the constrained optimization approach, we wish to compare learning the optimal policy as described by a Pareto frontier in the
(r_{1},r_{2})
space. We train a neural network policy with DPO
[
164
]
for each of the four datasets, and solve a constrained optimization problem for the
COP
approach. See Figure
5.9
(a) for empirical results under reward model sets
A
(left) and
B
(right).

While it is somewhat trivial to see why learning from
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
and

{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
alone yields suboptimal rewards, the case for the jointly preferred dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
and a convex weighted reward model dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{C}
is not obvious. The issue with
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
is that it only contains samples where
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
agree, effectively discarding all points that exhibit a meaningful trade-off between the two objectives. This results in a sparse and biased preference signal that does not span the entire Pareto front. In contrast,
{\mathcal{D}}_{C}
encodes preferences with respect to a
single
scalarized reward model, which inherently biases learning toward one specific convex combination of the objectives. While more sophisticated approaches have been proposed for learning from multi-dimensional preferences - such as RiC
[
235
]
, MODPO
[
256
]
, Rewarded Soups
[
167
]
, and SIPO
[
120
]
- they remain fundamentally limited in their expressivity. Ultimately, each method relies on learning with respect to a
single
scalarized reward signal of the form
\widetilde{r}=f(r_{1},r_{2})
, where the function
f
varies across methods. As a result, these approaches do not recover the full structure of the underlying preference landscape and cannot characterize the Pareto front in the multi-objective setting.

In contrast, constrained optimization (
COP
) over
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
yields solutions that lie close to the true Pareto frontier. This highlights the need for optimization methods that explicitly account for trade-offs across objectives, rather than collapsing them into a single reward signal, in order to fully leverage multi-dimensional preference data.
MOPO
follows this principle by directly optimizing within the multi-objective space, and empirically achieves solutions that approach the Pareto front as in Figure
5.9
(b).

We further empirically validate the correctness of
MOPO
and consider whether it is able to generalize and regularize effectively w.r.t. the reference policy. See Figure
5.10
for comparison of the policy learned through
MOPO
under various regularization values. We observe that even with an uninformed
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
,
MOPO
is able to push toward the Pareto frontier and is limited only by the strength of regularization.

Remark 5.6.2
.

Note that reward models are only used for evaluation, and are not assumptions or requirements to finetune policies using
MOPO
.
MOPO
learns strictly from preference data, without: (i) assuming the existence of a mapping from preferences to pointwise rewards, and (ii) learning this mapping (reward model) from preference data.

(a)

COP
comparison with traditional approaches

(b)

MOPO
approximates the Pareto front

Figure 5.9
:
Illustration of how a
COP
approach, and hence
MOPO
, achieves Pareto-optimal alignment in comparison with DPO on
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
, and
{\mathcal{D}}_{C}
under two sets of reward models.

\tau=0.1

\tau=0.5

\tau=1

Figure 5.10
:
Comparison of the
\mathsf{KL}
-regularized policy learned using
MOPO
with the reference policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
and the Pareto frontier, visualized in the reward space for two reward model pairs,
A
(top) and
B
(bottom).

5.6.2

Sub-optimality of baselines.

To begin, we list some common divergence metrics that have been used in literature to characterize two probability distributions. Then, we follow with some definitions before listing the main proofs for the sub-optimality of baselines.

Divergence measures and closed-form policies

We acknowledge that commonly used
f
-divergence measures have been introduced in
[
218
,
187
]
and show them here for completeness:

Divergence measure

f(x)

\nabla f(x)

barrier function

Reverse KL-divergence

x\log x

\log x+1

✔

Forward KL-divergence

-\log x

-1/x

✔

JSD

x\log x-(x+1)\log\frac{x+1}{2}

\log\frac{2x}{1+x}

✔

\alpha
-divergence

\frac{x^{1-\alpha}-(1-\alpha)x-\alpha}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}

(1-x^{-\alpha})/\alpha

✔

Jeffery divergence

x\log x-\log x

\log x-\frac{1}{x}+1

✔

Total Variation

|x-1|/2

\operatorname{sgn}(x-1)/2

✗

Chi-squared

(x-1)^{2}

2(x-1)

✗

Here we show the optimal sampling policies for multi-objective w.r.t. these divergence measures:

Divergence measure

Optimal policy

Reverse KL-divergence

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{K}\pi_{i}(y|x)^{w_{i}}\right)\cdot\exp(-Z(x))

Forward KL-divergence

\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)\cdot\left(Z(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{w_{i}\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)}{\pi_{i}(y|x)}\right)^{-1}

JSD

\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)\cdot\left(-1+\exp(Z(x))\prod_{i=1}^{K}\left(\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)}{\pi_{i}(y|x)}+1\right)^{w_{i}}\right)^{-1}

\alpha
-divergence

\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)\cdot\left(\alpha Z(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\left(\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y|x)}{\pi_{i}(y|x)}\right)^{\alpha}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}

Definitions

We first begin with some definitions.

Definition 5.6.3

(
f
-divergence
[
15
,
54
,
55
]
)
.

For probability measures
P
and
Q
, let
\mu
be a dominating measure of
P
and
Q
(i.e.
P,Q\ll\mu
), and let
p,q
be the Radon-Nikodym derivative
[
69
]

\frac{dP}{d\mu}
,
\frac{dQ}{d\mu}
respectively. For simplicity, here we assume
q>0
almost surely. Then
f
-divergence from
P
to
Q
is defined as

\displaystyle I_{f}(p\|q):=\int qf\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)d\mu\;,

where
f
is convex on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, satisfying
f(1)=0
. Most useful divergence measures are included in
f
-divergences, and the commonly used ones and corresponding
f
are introduced in Appendix
5.6.2
.

Definition 5.6.4

(Barrier function
[
151
]
)
.

Given conditions satisfied in Definition
5.6.3
, if additionally
0\notin\operatorname{dom}(\nabla f)
, then
f
is a barrier function. If a barrier function
f
is continuously differentiable and strongly convex on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then
f
is a strongly convex and smooth barrier function (abbreviated as strong-barrier function).

Definition 5.6.5

(Expected calibration error
[
84
,
218
]
)
.

Denote the ground truth distribution as
\mathbb{P}
, context as
X
and response as
Y
. The expected calibration error of a stochastic policy
\pi
is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{ECE}(\pi):=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\mathcal{X}\\
y\sim\pi(\cdot|x)\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathbb{P}(Y=y|X=x)-\pi(y|x)\big|\;.

Hypothesis 1

(Reducible reward misspecification
[
227
,
167
,
100
]
)
.

Let
\theta_{k}
be the parameter of the optimal policy for objective value
J_{k}
,
\forall k\in[K]
, and
\theta^{\star}_{w}
be the parameter of the optimal policy for the interpolated objective
\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}\cdot J_{k}\;
, then this hypothesis claims that

\displaystyle\theta^{*}_{w}\in\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{k}\cdot\theta_{k},\lambda\in\Delta^{K-1}\right\}\;,\;\forall w\in\Delta^{K-1}\;.

Extending the results of
[
218
]
to the multi-objective setting, we prove the necessity of
f
being barrier functions to find an optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
for multi-objective alignment. We refer the reader to
[
187
]
for a complete discussion.

Theorem 5.6.6
.

If
f
is not a barrier function, then for
\forall C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 4}
,
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, any multi-objective decoding or merging algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{S}^{K+1}\times\Delta^{K-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
, there exists a reference policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
, policies
\{\mathcal{\pi}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
and
\pi^{\prime}
, reward functions
\{\mathcal{R}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
, preference weightings
w\in\Delta^{K-1}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, s.t.
\pi_{i}
is the optimal policy for
\mathcal{R}_{i}
w.r.t.
\tau\cdot I_{f}(\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
,
\forall i\in[K]
, but

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]\leq\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-C\;,\mbox{and }

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-\tau I_{f}(\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})\leq\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-\tau I_{f}(\pi^{\prime}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})-C\;,

where
\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}(y):=\mathcal{A}\big(\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots,\pi_{K},w\big)(y)\;
.

Remark 5.6.7

(Motivating example)
.

Here we provide a motivating example where
f\equiv 0
: let
K=4
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{1})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{2})=1
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{2})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{1})=-1
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{3+k})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{3+k})=0
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{4-k})=\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{4-k})=1/2
, where
k\in\{0,1\}
. Then the optimal policy for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is
\pi_{1}(y_{i}):=\delta_{1i}
, for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is
\pi_{2}(y_{i}):=\delta_{2i}
, and for
\mathcal{R}_{1}/2+\mathcal{R}_{2}/2
is
\pi^{\star}(y_{i}):=\delta_{4-k,i}
. Thus
\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}
cannot fit
\pi^{\star}
both for
k=0,1
.

Proof 5.6.8
.

Since
f
is not a barrier function,
0\in\text{dom}(\nabla f)
. Now we can define
p:=\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\max}\nabla f(x)
,
q:=\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\min}\nabla f(x)
,
r:=\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\max}f(x)-\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\min}f(x)
,
s:=\frac{N-2}{N-3}\cdot C
. Let
w=(0.5,0.5,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{N-2})
, and we pick
k=\underset{j\in\{3,4,\ldots,N\}}{\argmin}\;\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}(y_{j})
. Let
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y_{i})=\frac{1}{N}
,
\pi_{1}(y_{i})=\delta_{1i}
,
\pi_{2}(y_{i})=\delta_{2i}
,
\pi_{j}(y_{i})=\frac{1}{N}
and
\pi^{\prime}(y_{i})=\delta_{ik}
,
\forall i\in[N],\;j\in\{3,4,\ldots,K\}
. And set
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{i})=\begin{cases}2p+2r+2s&i=1\\
4q-2p-2r-2s&i=2\\
p+q+r+s&i=k\\
2q&\text{o/w}\end{cases}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{i})=\begin{cases}4q-2p-2r-2s&i=1\\
2p+2r+2s&i=2\\
p+q+r+s&i=k\\
2q&\text{o/w}\end{cases}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{j}\equiv 0
,
\forall j\in\{3,4,\ldots,K\}
.

Let
\tau=1
, then the optimization objective for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
w.r.t.
I_{f}
is
J_{1}(\pi):=\underset{y\sim\pi}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{R}_{1}(y)\right]-I_{f}(\pi\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
, and the Lagrangian dual is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\pi):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{i})\cdot\pi(y_{i})+\frac{1}{N}f\left(N\cdot\pi(y_{i})\right)\right)+\lambda\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\pi(y_{i})-1\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}\pi(y_{i})\;.

As the objective is convex and the constraints are affine, we can directly apply the
Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions

[
151
]
:

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{L}_{1}(\pi_{1}^{\star})

\displaystyle=0\;,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{i})

\displaystyle=1\;,

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{i})

\displaystyle\geq 0\;,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}^{\star}

\displaystyle\geq 0\;,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}^{\star}\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{i})

\displaystyle=0\;.

(5.14)

Equation (
5.13
) implies

\displaystyle-\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{i})+\nabla f(N\cdot\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{i}))+\lambda^{\star}-\mu_{i}^{\star}=0\;.

If
\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{1})>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{\star}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{1})-\nabla f(N\cdot\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{1}))

\displaystyle\geq p+2r+2s\;,

and then for
\forall j\neq 1\;
,

\displaystyle\mu_{j}^{\star}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{j})+\nabla f(N\cdot\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{j}))+\lambda^{\star}

\displaystyle\geq-p-q-r-s+q+p+2r+2s

\displaystyle=r+s

\displaystyle>0\;.

Combining it with Equation (
5.14
) yields
\pi_{1}^{\star}(y_{j})=0
for
\forall j\neq 1
, which is exactly
\pi_{1}
. Note that we have

\displaystyle J(\pi_{1})\geq 2p+2r+2s-\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\max}f(x)\;.

For any
\pi^{\prime}
with
\pi^{\prime}(y_{1})=0
, we have

\displaystyle J(\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq p+q+r+s-\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\min}f(x)

\displaystyle=p+q+2r+s-\underset{x\in[0,N]}{\max}f(x)

\displaystyle<J(\pi_{1})\;.

Thus
\pi_{1}
is the optimal policy for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
w.r.t.
I_{f}(\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
. Similarly,
\pi_{2}
is the optimal policy for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
w.r.t.
I_{f}(\cdot|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
. By convexity of
f
, the minimum of
I_{f}(\pi\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
is obtained when
\pi=\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
, and thus
\pi_{j}
is the optimal policy for
\mathcal{R}_{j}
w.r.t.
I_{f}(\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
, for
\forall j\in\{3,4,\ldots,K\}
. Therefore, all conditions are well satisfied by this construction. Note that

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]

\displaystyle=p+q+r+s\;.

(5.15)

While by the selection of
k
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]\leq\frac{(N-3)\cdot 2q+p+q+r+s}{N-2}\;.

(5.16)

Comparing Equation (
5.15
) with Equation (
5.16
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-\frac{N-3}{N-2}s

\displaystyle=\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-C\;.

Note that
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
is a uniform distribution and both
\pi_{\mathcal{A},w},\pi^{\prime}
are one-point distributions, thus
I_{f}(\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})=I_{f}(\pi^{\prime}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
. We have

\displaystyle\underset{y\sim\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-I_{f}(\pi_{\mathcal{A},w}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})\leq\underset{y\sim\pi^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(y)\right]-I_{f}(\pi^{\prime}\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})-C\;.

Baselines are not Pareto-optimal

Given the necessity of
f
being a barrier-function, we now show how parameter-merging paradigm algorithms (
[
85
,
235
,
100
,
256
]
) fail to achieve Pareto-optimality. The optimality of parameter-merging paradigm primarily relies on reduced reward mis-specification hypothesis (see Hypothesis
1
). The following theorem demonstrates that this hypothesis does not hold for almost all
f
-divergence regularized policies.

Theorem 5.6.9
.

For any
f
-divergence satisfying one of the following conditions:
(i)
f
is not a barrier function; (ii)
I_{f}
is Reverse KL-divergence; (iii)
f
is a strong-barrier function, with finite roots of

\displaystyle 2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{1-2x}}{2\sqrt{1-2x}+\sqrt{x}}\right)-2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{x}}{2\sqrt{1-2x}+\sqrt{x}}\right)-\nabla f(3-6x)+\nabla f(3x)\;,

\exists N,K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, a neural network
nn=\operatorname{softmax}(h_{\theta}(z_{0}))
where
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
h_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a continuous mapping, preference weightings
w\in\Delta^{K-1}
, reference policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
, and the objectives
J_{1},J_{2},\ldots,J_{K}
representing reward functions
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{K}
w.r.t.
\tau\cdot I_{f}(\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})
, s.t. Hypothesis
1
does not hold.

Proof 5.6.10
.

(i) If
f
is not a barrier function, Hypothesis
1
does not hold immediately from Theorem
5.6.6
.

(ii) If
I_{f}
is Reverse KL-divergence, we let
N=3
,
K=3
, and
h_{\theta}(z_{0})=W_{\theta}^{(2)}\sigma\left(W_{\theta}^{(1)}z_{0}\right)
, where
\sigma
is
\text{ReLU}(\cdot)
. We set
\mathcal{R}_{i}(y_{j})=\delta_{ij}
,
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y_{i})=1/3
for
\forall i,j\in[3]
,
z_{0}=1
and
\tau=1
. Then the optimal policies are
W_{\theta_{1}}^{(1)}=e_{1}
,

W_{\theta_{1}}^{(2)}=\left(\begin{matrix}100\\
000\\
000\end{matrix}\right)
for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
w.r.t.
\mathsf{KL}{\cdot}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}}
,
W_{\theta_{2}}^{(1)}=e_{2}
,

W_{\theta_{2}}^{(2)}=\left(\begin{matrix}000\\
010\\
000\end{matrix}\right)
for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
w.r.t.
\mathsf{KL}{\cdot}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}}
, and
W_{\theta_{3}}^{(1)}=e_{3}
,
W_{\theta_{3}}^{(2)}=\left(\begin{matrix}000\\
000\\
001\end{matrix}\right)
for
\mathcal{R}_{3}
w.r.t.
\mathsf{KL}{\cdot}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}}
. Thus we have
h_{\sum_{j=1}^{3}\lambda_{j}\theta_{j}}(z_{0})=\left(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2}\right)^{\top}
. Given
w=(0,1/3,2/3)
, the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
should output
\pi^{\star}(y_{1})=\frac{1}{1+\exp(1/3)+\exp(2/3)}
,
\pi^{\star}(y_{2})=\frac{\exp(1/3)}{1+\exp(1/3)+\exp(2/3)}
and
\pi^{\star}(y_{3})=\frac{\exp(2/3)}{1+\exp(1/3)+\exp(2/3)}
. Note that

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}+\sqrt{t+1/3}+\sqrt{t+2/3}>1\;,\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\;,

thus there is no solution
\lambda\in\Delta^{2},t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
for
\left(\lambda_{1}^{2},\lambda_{2}^{2},\lambda_{3}^{2}\right)^{\top}=\left(t,t+\frac{1}{3},t+\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\top}
, i.e. there is no
\lambda
s.t.
\operatorname{softmax}\left(h_{\sum_{j=1}^{3}\lambda_{j}\theta_{j}}(z_{0})\right)=\big(\pi^{\star}(y_{1}),\pi^{\star}(y_{2}),\pi^{\star}(y_{3})\big)
, i.e. Hypothesis
1
does not hold.

(iii) If
f
is a strong-barrier function, with finite roots of

\displaystyle 2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{1-2x}}{2\sqrt{1-2x}+\sqrt{x}}\right)-2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{x}}{2\sqrt{1-2x}+\sqrt{x}}\right)-\nabla f(3-6x)+\nabla f(3x)\;,

we let
N=3
,
K=2
,
h_{\theta}(z_{0})=W_{\theta}(z_{0})
,
z_{0}=1
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}(y_{i})=\delta_{1i}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}(y_{i})=\delta_{2i}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y_{i})=1/3
, for
\forall i\in[3]
. The optimal policy for
J_{1}
is
\pi_{\theta_{1}}(y_{i})=\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\delta_{1i}-Z\right)
, and the optimal policy for
J_{2}
is
\pi_{\theta_{2}}(y_{i})=\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\delta_{2i}-Z\right)
, where
Z
is the normalization factor. And these policies can be learned by setting
W_{\theta_{i}}=\big(\log\pi_{\theta_{i}}(y_{1}),\log\pi_{\theta_{i}}(y_{2}),\log\pi_{\theta_{i}}(y_{3})\big)^{\top}
.

We set
a:=\pi_{\theta_{1}}(y_{1})=\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}(\frac{1}{\tau}-Z)
,
b:=\pi_{\theta_{1}}(y_{2})=\pi_{\theta_{1}}(y_{3})=\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}(-Z)
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\nabla f(3a)-\nabla f(3b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\;,

(5.17)

\displaystyle a+2b

\displaystyle=1\;.

(5.18)

The optimal policy for
w_{1}\cdot J_{1}+w_{2}\cdot J_{2}
(see
[
187
]
for proof) is

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{w}(y_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(-Z_{w}^{\star}+\frac{w_{1}}{\tau}\delta_{1i}+\frac{w_{2}}{\tau}\delta_{2i}\right)\;,

(5.19)

where
Z_{w}^{\star}
is the normalization factor. By linearly merging the weights of
\pi_{\theta_{1}}
and
\pi_{\theta_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\lambda_{1}\theta_{1}+\lambda_{2}\theta_{2}}(y_{i})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{softmax}\left(\lambda_{1}W_{\theta_{1}}(z_{0})+\lambda_{2}W_{\theta_{2}}(z_{0})\right)(y_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\lambda}}\left((\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\delta_{1i}-Z\right)\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\left((\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\delta_{2i}-Z\right)\right)^{\lambda_{2}}\;,

(5.20)

where
Z_{\lambda}
is the normalization factor.

With symmetry, Equation (
5.19
), (
5.20
) and Hypothesis
1
indicate that
\pi_{\frac{1}{2}\theta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\theta_{2}}=\pi^{\star}_{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}
, thus

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(-Z_{(0.5,0.5)}^{\star}+\frac{1}{2\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{a}}{2\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}}\;,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(\nabla f)^{(-1)}\left(-Z_{(0.5,0.5)}^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{b}}{2\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}}\;,

and combining them with Equation (
5.17
) yields

\displaystyle 2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{a}}{2\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}}\right)-2\nabla f\left(\frac{3\sqrt{b}}{2\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla f(3a)-\nabla f(3b)\;.

(5.21)

Given the condition, the solution set
(a,b)
to Equation (
5.18
), (
5.21
) is finite, thus there exists
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
s.t. Equation (
5.17
) does not hold, implying that Hypothesis
1
does not hold.

5.6.3

Constraint Threshold Initialization

We begin this section by describing an equivalence between the preference-learning based optimization problem and a reward-learning based optimization problem. Note that
MOPO
does not assume this equivalence, and directly works with preference data, and this equivalence is established for analysis only. Following this equivalence discussion, we specify a provable method for setting constraint thresholds
\bm{b}
such that the optimal solution learned by
MOPO
is a Pareto-optimal solution.

For all preference instances in the preference dataset, there exists an underlying, unknown reward model based on which preferences are provided. For all contexts-output pairs
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
, let the reward model for the
k^{th}
objective be
r_{k}(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
k\in[K]
.

Preference–reward link.

For every objective
k\in[K]
there exists a
strictly increasing
function
\phi_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a strictly increasing transfer function
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,1)
such that for all contexts
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and actions
y,y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}
we have,

p_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\;=\;\sigma\bigl(\phi_{k}\bigl(r_{k}(x,y)-r_{k}(x,y^{\prime})\bigr)\bigr).

(5.22)

Now, with respect to the
k^{th}
objective, let the expected reward
R_{k}(\pi)
and preference-based objective value
F_{k}(\pi)
for a policy be given by,

\displaystyle R_{k}(\pi)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu,\;y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}[r_{k}(x,y)]\;\text{for}\,\;k\in[K],

\displaystyle F_{k}(\pi)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu,\;y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x),\;y^{\prime}\sim\mu(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[p_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\bigr],\quad k\in[K-1],

\displaystyle F_{K}(\pi)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu,\;y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x),\;y^{\prime}\sim\mu(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[p(y\succ y^{\prime}\mid x)\bigr]-\tau\,\mathsf{KL}\bigl(\pi\;\|\;\pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\bigr).

Lemma 5.6.11

(Order preservation)
.

Under Equation (
5.22
) and some
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the mapping
H_{k}(u):=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathrm{Unif}[-u,u]}\bigl[\sigma\circ\phi_{k}(z)\bigr]
is strictly increasing. Moreover, for every policy
\pi

F_{k}(\pi)\;=\;H_{k}\bigl(R_{k}(\pi)-R_{k}(\mu)\bigr),\qquad k\in[K-1],

so that for any
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
, we have

R_{k}(\pi)\geq R_{k}(\pi^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow F_{k}(\pi)\geq F_{k}(\pi^{\prime}).

Proof 5.6.12
.

Fix
k
. By Equation (
5.22
),
\sigma\circ\phi_{k}
is strictly increasing, hence so is its odd
extension
z\mapsto\sigma\circ\phi_{k}(z)
. For
U:=R_{k}(\pi)-R_{k}(\mu)
let

z:=r_{k}(x,y)-r_{k}(x,y^{\prime})
. Because
(y,y^{\prime})\sim(\pi,\mu)
are independent,
z
is symmetrically distributed around
U
and
z\sim\mathrm{Unif}[U-\delta,U+\delta]
for some
\delta>0
that does not depend on
U
. Taking expectation yields
F_{k}(\pi)=H_{k}(U)
, and strict monotonicity of
H_{k}
follows from strict monotonicity of
\sigma\circ\phi_{k}
.

Note there that we introduced
H_{k}(u)=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathrm{Unif}[-u,u]}\bigl[\sigma\circ\phi_{k}(z)\bigr]
. The uniform law is chosen purely for notational brevity;
the proof requires only that the base distribution be symmetric and shifted by the reward gap

u=R_{k}(\pi)-R_{k}(\mu)
. Consequently, one may replace
\mathrm{Unif}[-u,u]
by any symmetric density
\rho_{u}(z)=\rho(z-u)
, and define
H_{k}(u)=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\rho_{u}}\bigl[\sigma\circ\phi_{k}(z)\bigr]
. Strict monotonicity of
\sigma\circ\phi_{k}
then guarantees that the Lemma
5.6.11
holds. Now, let
\bm{b}\in[0,1]^{K-1}
be the probability thresholds in
COP
, let
\mathbf{F}_{1:K-1}(\pi)=\left(F_{1}(\pi),\dots,F_{K-1}(\pi)\right)
and
\mathbf{R}_{1:K-1}(\pi)=\left(R_{1}(\pi),\dots,R_{K-1}(\pi)\right)
, and define component-wise

c_{k}:=H_{k}^{-1}(b_{k})+R_{k}(\mu),\quad k\in[K-1],\quad\bm{c}:=(c_{1},\dots,c_{K-1}).

By Lemma
5.6.11
,F_{k}(\pi)\geq b_{k}\;\Leftrightarrow\;R_{k}(\pi)\geq c_{k}.
Hence the preference‐space constrained problem

\max_{\pi}\,F_{K}(\pi)\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\mathbf{F}_{1:K-1}(\pi)\geq\bm{b}

is
equivalent
to the reward‐space problem

\max_{\pi}\,\bigl\{R_{K}(\pi)-\tau\,\mathrm{KL}(\pi\;\|\;\pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\bigr\}\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\mathbf{R}_{1:K-1}(\pi)\geq\bm{c}.

Given this equivalence, we now describe the procedure of setting appropriate constraint thresholds
\bm{c}
for the
COP-R
problem, following which constraint thresholds
\bm{b}
for the original
COP
can be obtained element-wise via
c_{k}:=H_{k}^{-1}(b_{k})+R_{k}(\mu)
. This setting of constraint thresholds ensures that the optimal solution of the
COP
problem (Problem (
5.1
)) is also a Pareto-optimal solution. We begin with a definition, following which we state the main result for
\bm{c}_{k}
, which holds for all
k\in[K-1]
.

Definition 5.6.13

(Insertion Index)
.

Let
\mathsf{P}_{k}\;=\;\bigl(\mathsf{P}_{k}(0),\,\mathsf{P}_{k}(1),\,\dots,\,\mathsf{P}_{k}(M-1)\bigr)
be an ascending (sorted) list of the
k
-th objective values from the Pareto front consisting of
M
points. For any new value
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the
insertion index

j
is the smallest integer
0\leq j<M
satisfying
\mathsf{P}_{k}(j)\;\geq\;\alpha\quad(\text{if such a }j\text{ exists})
, and set
j=M
if no such index exists.

Proposition 5.6.14
.

For the initial point
\pi_{0}
of Problem (
5.1
), let the insertion index of
\alpha_{k}:=R_{k}(\pi_{0})
in
\mathsf{P}_{k}
be
j_{k}
. If
\bm{c}_{k}\geq\mathsf{P}_{k}(\max(0,j_{k}-1)){\;\;\forall\;}k\in[K-1]
, then the optimal solution of Problem
COP-R
, if it exists, is a Pareto-optimal solution.

Proof 5.6.15
.

We prove by contradiction. First, define a solution element by the tuple
(\pi,\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\pi})
, which refers to a policy
\pi
along with its corresponding reward vector
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\pi}=\left(R_{1}(\pi),\dots,R_{K}(\pi)\right)
. Now suppose that the optimal solution
P^{{}^{\prime}}=(\pi^{\prime},\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\pi^{\prime}})
of Problem
COP-R
is not a Pareto-optimal solution. By the definition of Pareto-optimal solution, there exists a solution
\widehat{P}=(\widehat{\pi},\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}})
in
\Pi_{{P}}
that dominates
P^{\prime}
, i.e.,
R_{k}(\pi^{\prime})\leq R_{k}(\widehat{\pi}){\;\;\forall\;}k\in[K]
. Given
P_{0}=(\pi_{0},\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\pi_{0}})
, we have
R_{K}(\widehat{\pi})\geq R_{K}(\pi^{\prime})\geq R_{K}(\pi_{0})
by definition. Since both
P_{0}
and
\widehat{P}
do not dominate each other, since
R_{K}(\widehat{\pi})\geq R_{K}(\pi_{0})
, there exists
k\in[K-1]
such that
R_{k}(\pi_{0})\geq R_{k}(\widehat{\pi})
.

Now consider the values of
\bm{c}_{k}
and
R_{k}(\widehat{\pi})
for some objective
k
. Note that
\bm{c}_{k}\geq\mathsf{P}_{k}(\max(0,j_{k}-1))
. If
R_{k}(\pi_{0})\geq R_{k}(\widehat{\pi})>\bm{c}_{k}
, then
R_{k}(\pi_{0})\geq R_{k}(\widehat{\pi})>\mathsf{P}_{k}(\max(0,j_{k}-1))
, which is conflicting with the condition that
\mathsf{P}_{k}(\max(0,j_{k}-1))
is the
(\max(0,j_{k}-1))^{th}
objective value in
\mathsf{P}_{k}
. If
R_{k}(\widehat{\pi})\leq\bm{c}_{k}
, it conflicts with the condition that
\widehat{P}
dominates
P^{\prime}
. Therefore, such a
\widehat{P}
does not exist, and hence,
P^{\prime}
is a Pareto-optimal solution.

Figure 5.11
:
Visualization of criteria for specifying constraint values.
The expected return surface of
\pi_{1}
(
\pi_{2}
), call it
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
(
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
), in objective 1(2) is the
\max(0,j_{k}-1)^{th}
value in list
\mathsf{P}_{1}
(
\mathsf{P}_{2}
) respectively. Therefore, specifying constraints values
\bm{b}_{1}\geq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
\bm{b}_{2}\geq{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
is sufficient for the optimal solution of Equation (
5.1
) to be a Pareto-optimal solution.

Proposition
5.6.14
formalizes the criteria for specifying appropriate constraint values and provides the condition for which the optimal solution of Problem (
5.1
) is a Pareto optimal solution. See Figure
5.11
for the visualization. Proposition
5.6.14
gives a
sufficient
condition under which the solution of Problem (
5.2
) is Pareto optimal. However, in practice this condition is (i) overly conservative and may exclude many feasible Pareto points, and (ii) computationally expensive as it requires re‑evaluating
all
policies for non‑dominated sorting at every optimization step. Please see Section
5.3.1
for an empirically validated practical constraint specification procedure.

5.6.4

Log-barrier Function Optimization

For some
\sigma,s>0
, consider the following log barrier function,

{\mathcal{B}}_{\sigma,s}(z)=\begin{cases}-\sigma\log(-z)\,,&z\leq-s\\
\frac{\sigma}{s}z+(1-\log(s))\sigma\,,&z>-s\end{cases}\;\;,\;\;\text{with}\;\;\partial_{z}{\mathcal{B}}_{\sigma,s}(z)=\frac{\sigma}{\max(-z,s)}\,.

For all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}
, this is a convex, continuous, and differentiable function. Importantly, for
s=\sigma^{2}
, this barrier function converges to the characteristic function
\chi\{z\leq 0\}
as
\sigma\rightarrow 0
, i.e., it takes the value 0 when
z\leq 0
and
\infty
otherwise; the condition
s=\sigma^{2}
is sufficient, but not necessary for constraint satisfaction
[
109
]
. This convergence to the characteristic function is visually depicted in Figure
5.12
, showing the change in the log barrier function as we gradually decrease
\sigma
.

Figure 5.12
:

The relaxed logarithmic barrier.

We depict the convergence of the relaxed logarithmic barrier
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,\sigma^{2}}(z)
to the characteristic function
\chi\{z\leq 0\}
as
\sigma\rightarrow 0
.
We gradually decrease
\sigma
from 1 to 0.01.
Consequently,
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,\sigma^{2}}(z)
gets closer to 0 for
z\leq 0
and increases to
\infty
otherwise.

In Section
5.3
, we had defined

{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{LB}}(\rho,\bm{\sigma})={\mathcal{F}}(\rho)-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}{\mathcal{B}}_{\bm{\sigma}_{k},\bm{\sigma}_{k}^{2}}(\bm{b}_{k}-\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}_{k}(\rho))\;.

(5.23)

Since the log barrier converges to the characteristic function as
\sigma\rightarrow 0
, we want to find the maximizer of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{LB}}(\rho,\bm{\sigma})
for small
\bm{\sigma}
. However, doing so directly leads to instabilities as the objective function is ill-conditioned. Instead, it is common practice to follow an iterative procedure: one finds the maximizer for a fixed
\bm{\sigma}
, reduces
\bm{\sigma}
, and repeats
[
57
]
. Specifically, the procedure is instantiated with initial values
\rho_{0}
,
\bm{\sigma}_{0}
, and
0<\bm{\gamma}_{k}<1
for
\bm{\gamma}=\{\bm{\gamma}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1}
. On the
t
-th iteration,
\bm{\sigma}_{k}^{(t)}\leftarrow\bm{\gamma}_{k}\bm{\sigma}_{k}^{(t-1)}
is reduced and
\rho^{(t)}\leftarrow\argmax_{\rho}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{LB}}(\rho,\bm{\sigma})
(with initialization
\rho^{(t-1)}
). In doing so, the constraints are gradually enforced, nudging the LLM to satisfy them over the optimization procedure while avoiding instabilities. As
\{\bm{\sigma}^{(t)}\}\searrow 0
, the weights
\{\rho^{(t)}\}
converge to the maximizer of the constrained problem.

It is impossible to maximize
{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{LB}}(\rho,\bm{\sigma})
exactly in many practical applications. Instead, at each iteration, one can take a single optimization step toward the solution.
Doing so is amenable to stochastic gradient methods and mitigates computational overhead: the optimization proceeds as normal while the value of
\bm{\sigma}
is reduced over the course of the procedure. One can guarantee the convergence of this procedure to the optimal solution in some settings; for example,
[
57
]
prove convergence when dealing with box constraints.
However, convergence in a scenario like ours is not guaranteed. Nevertheless, we will experimentally demonstrate its use for our constrained problems.

We employ stochastic gradient methods and derive the gradient of our objective function directly:

\partial_{\rho}{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{LB}}(\rho,\bm{\sigma})=\partial_{\rho}{\mathcal{F}}(\rho)\ +\ \sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\frac{\bm{\sigma}_{k}\partial_{\rho}\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}_{k}(\rho)}{\max\left(\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}_{k}(\rho)-\bm{b}_{k},\bm{\sigma}_{k}^{2}\right)}.

(5.24)

This follows immediately from Equation (
5.23
). See
[
184
]
for a more detailed review.

Connection to Lagrange multipliers.
The log barrier and the Lagrangian are intrinsically connected; this becomes evident when comparing Equation (
5.24
) with the (gradient of the) Lagrangian in Equation (
5.3.1
). In particular, we define the multipliers:

\bm{\lambda}_{k}\ =\ \frac{\bm{\sigma}_{k}}{\max\left(\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}_{k}(\rho)-\bm{b}_{k},\bm{\sigma}_{k}^{2}\right)}\;.

They can be interpreted as Lagrange multipliers: for active constraints,
\bm{\lambda}_{k}=1/\bm{\sigma}_{k}
is non-zero; for inactive constraints,
\bm{\lambda}_{k}=\bm{\sigma}_{k}/\left(\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}_{k}(\rho)-\bm{b}_{k}\right)
vanishes to
0
as
\bm{\sigma}_{k}\rightarrow 0
. Hence, the KKT complementary slackness condition is satisfied by design.

5.6.5

Main Text Proofs

See
5.3.1

Proof 5.6.16
.

Given the Lagrangian
{\mathcal{L}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})
, the dual formulation is given by

\max_{\rho}\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq 0}{\mathcal{L}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})\equiv\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq 0}\max_{\rho}{\mathcal{L}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})\,.

By the strong duality, it is sufficient to consider KKT conditions for
(\rho^{\star},\bm{\lambda}^{\star})
.

(i) Primal feasibility i.e.
{\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho^{\star}(y)\,\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\geq\bm{b}
.

(ii) Dual feasibility i.e.
\bm{\lambda}^{\star}\geq\bm{0}
.

(iii) Complementary slackness i.e.
(\bm{\lambda}^{\star})^{T}\left(\bm{b}-{\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho^{\star}(y)\,\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\right)=\bm{0}
.

(iv) Stationarity i.e.
\nabla_{\rho}{\mathcal{L}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})=0
i.e.

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[p(y\succ y^{\prime})]-\tau\left(\ln(\rho(y))+1\right)+\bm{\lambda}^{T}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]=0\,.

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\rho^{\star}(y)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p(y\succ y^{\prime})+\bm{\lambda}^{T}\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})\right]-1\right)

Now we show that conditions (i)-(iii) hold for the above
\rho^{\star}(y)
. For condition (i), by initialization of
\bm{b}
using Proposition
5.6.14
, we have

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho^{\star}(y)\,\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\geq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho_{0}(y)\,\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\geq\bm{b}\;,

where
\rho_{0}(y)=\pi_{0}(y)/\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)
,
\pi_{0}
is the initialization point of solving Problem (
5.2
), and the second inequality follows by construction. Condition (ii) also holds by construction of
\bm{\lambda}
. Now, condition (iii) holds by definition if the constraint is active i.e.
{\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho^{\star}(y)\,\bm{q}(y\succ y^{\prime})]=\bm{b}
, and if it is inactive, then dual feasibility also ensures that complimentary slackness holds.
As a consequence, all KKT conditions are always satisfied with the above
\rho^{\star}(y)
, which concludes the proof.

See
5.3.3

Proof 5.6.17
.

For the given constrained optimization problem:

\min_{\pi_{k}}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\;\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\;\mathsf{KL}(\pi_{k}\;||\;\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})\leq\epsilon\quad\text{and}\,,\quad\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}(y)=1\;,

we consider its Lagrangian to find its dual problem. By noticing that,
\pi_{k}(y)\rho(y)=\pi_{k}(y)\frac{\pi(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\approx\pi(y)
, the Lagrangian for some arbitrary multipliers
\chi_{k}\geq 0
and
\zeta_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is given by,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]+\chi_{k}\left(\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\pi_{k}(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\right)\right]-\epsilon\right)+\zeta_{k}\left(\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}(y)-1\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]+\chi_{k}\left(\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}(y)\ln\left(\frac{\pi_{k}(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\right)-\epsilon\right)+\zeta_{k}\left(\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}(y)-1\right)\,,

where
\chi_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is the Lagrange multiplier for
\mathsf{KL}
constraint, and
\zeta_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is the Lagrange multiplier for the normalization constraint that ensures
\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}(y)=1
. Hence, the corresponding optimization problem due to strong duality is:

\min_{\pi_{k}}\max_{\chi_{k}\geq 0,\zeta_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k})\equiv\max_{\chi_{k}\geq 0,\zeta_{k}}\min_{\pi_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k}).

Now, we can compute the non-parametric closed form solution for each sample
y
for
the inner minimization problem. Due to the convexity of
\mathsf{KL}
-divergence, it is sufficient to consider
\nabla_{\pi_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k})=0
. For each
y
we then have,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\pi_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k})=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]+\chi_{k}^{\star}\left(\ln\left(\frac{\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\right)+1\right)+\zeta_{k}=0

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)\propto\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)\exp\left((\chi_{k}^{\star})^{-1}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]\right)\,

with some normalization constant
Z_{k}
that ensures that
\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)=1
, which is described with respect to
\zeta_{k}
. Then, by plugging the above stationarity condition into the Lagrangian, we have the dual unconstrained optimization problem as,

\displaystyle\max_{\chi_{k}\geq 0,\zeta_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}(\pi_{k}^{\star},\chi_{k},\zeta_{k})

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}^{\star}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]+\chi_{k}\left(\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}^{\star}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\right)\right]-\epsilon\right)+\zeta_{k}\left(\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)-1\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{k}^{\star}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})]+\chi_{k}\ln\left(\frac{\pi_{k}^{\star}(y)}{\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}(y)}\right)\right]-\chi_{k}\epsilon

\displaystyle=-\chi_{k}\ln\left({\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}\left[\exp\left(\chi_{k}^{-1}\rho(y)\bm{q}_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})\right)\right]\right)-\chi_{k}\epsilon\,.

This concludes the proof.

5.6.6

Empirical Optimization Problem

We now formulate the problem given a fixed offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
of the form
{\mathcal{D}}:=\left\{\left(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
, where context
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}
are two generations from
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}
and
\mu
respectively, and
{\mathbb{I}}(\cdot,\cdot)\in\{0,1\}^{K}
is preference indicator vector over
K
objectives, i.e.,
{\mathbb{I}}_{k}(y,y^{\prime})=1
if
y\succ_{k}y^{\prime}
, and
0
otherwise for
k\in[K]
. With a slight abuse in notation, we let
{\mathbb{I}}_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)\triangleq{\mathbb{I}}_{K}(y,y^{\prime})
and
{\mathbb{I}}_{\bm{q}}(\cdot,\cdot)\triangleq\left({\mathbb{I}}_{1}(y,y^{\prime}),\dots,{\mathbb{I}}_{K-1}(y,y^{\prime})\right)^{T}
. Then, the empirical optimization problem is given by:

\max_{\rho}\;\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho(y_{i})\,{\mathbb{I}}_{p}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})-\tau\rho(y_{i})\,\ln(\rho(y_{i}))}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}(\rho)}\hskip 9.24994pt\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\hskip 9.24994pt\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho(y_{i})\,{\mathbb{I}}_{\bm{q}}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})}_{\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}}(\rho)}\geq\bm{b}.

(5.25)

Proof 5.6.18
.

We need to show that we can build an unbiased estimate of the optimization function from empirical observations. To this end, consider the sampled
COP
as:

\max_{\rho}\;\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\,I_{p}(y,y^{\prime})]-\tau\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\,\ln(\rho(y))\quad\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\quad\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\rho(y)\,\bm{I}_{\bm{q}}(y,y^{\prime})]\geq\bm{b}

(5.26)

, where
\bm{I}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a Bernoulli random preference vector over
K
objectives i.e.
\bm{I}_{k}(y,y^{\prime})
is a random variable sampled from a Bernoulli distribution with mean
p_{k}(y\succ y^{\prime})
such that it is
1
if
y\succ_{k}y^{\prime}\,
, and 0 otherwise, where
\succ_{k}
denotes preference with respect to the
k^{th}
objective for
k\in[K]
. Following the notation discussed before, we let
I_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
to be the preference with respect to the
K^{th}
objective, and let
\bm{I}_{\bm{q}}(\cdot,\cdot)
to be the preference vector for the remaining
(K-1)
objectives i.e.
I_{p}(y,y^{\prime})=\bm{I}_{K}(y,y^{\prime})
and
(\bm{I}_{\bm{q}})_{k}=\bm{I}_{k}(y,y^{\prime})
for
k\in[K-1]
. Note that Problem (
5.2
) and Problem (
5.26
) are equivalent since
{\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho(y)\,I_{p}(y,y^{\prime})]={\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho(y)\,I_{p}(y,y^{\prime})\,|\,y,y^{\prime}]={\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim\pi_{\mathsf{ref}},y^{\prime}\sim\mu}[\rho(y)\,p(y\succ y^{\prime})]
. Similar argument follows for
\bm{I}_{\bm{q}}
.

Note that each data point
\left(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\right)
contributes two terms to the empirical problem above: one with
(x,y,y^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y,y^{\prime}))=(x_{i},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}))
and another with
(x,y,y^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y,y^{\prime}))
=
(x_{i},y_{i}^{\prime},

y_{i},{\mathbb{I}}(y_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}))
. This symmetry is important to exploit since it reduces gradient variance and improves stability during optimization. For clarity, we omit the symmetric term in notation as incorporating it is trivial – simply augment the current dataset by swapping
y_{i}
and
y_{i}^{\prime}
and bit flipping
{\mathbb{I}}(\cdot,\cdot)
element-wise.

Now, as before, for some
\bm{\lambda}:=\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1}\geq\bm{0}
, we have the Lagrangian as
\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})=\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}(\rho)-\bm{\lambda}^{T}\left(\bm{b}-\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}}(\rho)\right)
, and the dual as,

\begin{aligned} \widehat{\texttt{Dual${}^{\star}$}}&\triangleq\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq\bm{0}}\max_{\rho}\;\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}(\rho,\bm{\lambda})=\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq\bm{0}}\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}(\rho^{\star}_{\bm{\lambda}},\bm{\lambda})=\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq 0}\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}(\rho_{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})-\bm{\lambda}^{T}(\bm{b}-\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}}(\rho_{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star}))\\
\text{where},&\;\,\rho^{\star}_{\bm{\lambda}}(y)=\exp\left((\tau N)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}[{\mathbb{I}}_{p}(y,y^{\prime})+\bm{\lambda}^{T}{\mathbb{I}}_{\bm{q}}(y,y^{\prime})]-1\right).\end{aligned}

(5.27)

Following our earlier discussion on lower bounding the preference probabilities for
[K-1]
constraints, we wish to constrain
\texttt{LowerBound}(\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{G}}}}(\rho))
to be greater than
\bm{b}
. Then, following Equation (
5.9
), the empirical lower bound is obtained by solving below for
M
batches of
{\mathcal{D}}
, where each batch
m
of size
N_{M}=\lfloor N/M\rfloor
is of the form
\left(x_{m,j},y_{m,j},y_{m,j}^{\prime},{\mathbb{I}}(y_{m,j},y_{m,j}^{\prime})\right)_{j=1}^{N_{M}}
.

\begin{aligned} \min_{\bm{\chi}\geq\bm{0}}\widehat{J}(\bm{\chi}\,;\rho):=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left[\chi_{k}\ln\left(\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{M}}\exp\left(\chi_{k}^{-1}[\rho(y_{m,j})({\mathbb{I}}_{\bm{q}})_{k}(y_{m,j},y_{m,j}^{\prime})]\right)\right)+\chi_{k}\epsilon\right]\right].\end{aligned}

(5.28)

This transforms the empirical dual Problem (
5.27
) into the below optimizations for
\bm{\lambda}
and policy
\pi_{\psi}
:

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{\lambda}\geq\bm{0}}\widehat{J}(\bm{\lambda}\,;\bm{\chi}):=\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}(\rho)-\bm{\lambda}^{T}(\bm{b}-\widehat{J}(\bm{\chi}\,;\rho))\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and},\;\min_{\psi}\widehat{J}_{\rho}(\pi_{\psi}):=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star}(y_{i})\log(\pi_{\psi}(y_{i}))\,,

(5.29)

where
\rho_{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star}(\cdot)
is computed using Equation (
5.27
).

5.6.7

Implementation Details

Background

SFT.
Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with labeled demonstrations is widely adopted to fine-tune LLMs
[
244
,
161
]
. Given prompt-response pairs
\{(x,y)\}
sampled form the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, the SFT loss function is defined as:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SFT}}=-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\left[\sum_{i}\log\pi_{\rm sft}(y_{i}|x,y_{<i})\right],

(5.30)

where
\pi_{\mathrm{sft}}
refers to the LLM policy and
y_{<i}
indicates all tokens before the
i
-th token in response
y
.

RLHF.
RLHF typically involves two steps
[
157
,
230
]
: reward modeling, and RL training. In reward modeling, a reward model
r_{\phi}
is trained to minimize the loss function

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)=-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y_{w},y_{l})\sim{\mathcal{D}}}[\log(\sigma(r_{\phi}(x,y_{w})-r_{\phi}(x,y_{l})))]

, where
\sigma(z)
is the sigmoid function,
y_{w}
and
y_{l}
refer to preferred and dispreferred responses, respectively. Generally, RL training uses the PPO algorithm
[
185
]
with an additional KL penalty relative to the SFT policy:

\arg\max_{\pi_{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{D}},y\sim\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}\left[r_{\phi}(x,y)-\tau\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(y|x)}{{\pi_{\rm sft}(y|x)}}\right],

where
\tau>0
is the KL penalty coefficient.

Training details.

We summarize the key implementation details of text generation tasks in Table
5.3
. This table also provides links to the open-sourced datasets and reward models utilized in our study. Implementation is primarily based on trl
[
214
]
. Especially, SFT fine-tunes the base model, while MORLHF and Rewarded Soups fine-tune the SFT model using the PPO algorithm. In contrast, RiC directly fine-tunes the base model. See
[
235
]
for more details. We apply the same 4-bit quantization and LoRA configuration for training all models. During evaluation, we maintain a consistent configuration across different models, generating 64 tokens for the Helpful Assistant task and 32 for the Reddit Summary task.

For all baselines including
MOPO
, we begin by normalizing the rewards using the mean and standard of the offline dataset before incorporating them into the prompts. During online generation (updating the reference policy) and evaluation, we sample a group of 25,000 random samples from a normal distribution and use the maximum and minimum values (generally around
\pm 3
) of these samples to replace the maximum and minimum values of the dataset. This method can prevent the extreme values in the dataset to impact reward selection.

Incorporating preference vectors

. Pareto fronts are generated as in
[
235
]
. One point to consider while evaluating empirical Pareto fronts is to incorporate user preferences for a particular objective. For instance, in the case of 2 objectives, in RiC
[
235
]
, scalarization tuples are passed as in-context human preferences. Preference tuple
w=(w_{1},w_{2})
for the two reward dimensions is passed to the model at inference time to adjust the LLM policy according to the user preferences. It is necessary to map these scalarization tuples
w
to the desired rewards that will be used as conditioning in prompts. Similarly, for the two objective case, PARM uses prompts at inference time and clusters them. Then, they take two diverse policies, apply each policy to each cluster, and compute a multi-objective reward vector for all policy-cluster pairs.

Figure 5.13
:
Multi-headed policy architecture for incorporating preferences at inference time in
MOPO
.

A single point in the plots is a reward vector for the policy-cluster pair. For a fair comparison,
MOPO
should also incorporate the preferences at inference time (in terms of which objective should be primary). For
K
objectives, one idea would be to solve
K
separate optimization problems as in Problem (
5.2
), one for each objective being the “main” objective. However, this is not efficient since if the LLM had
m
parameters, the space complexity of representing
K
policies would be
Km
.

We propose a multi-policy architecture in which all policies share a common LLM backbone. Each policy
k
is parameterized by a matrix
\theta_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times d}
, where
L
is the number of tokens and
d
is the transformer embedding dimension. The logit for the next token under policy
k
is computed as
\theta_{k}\phi
, where
\phi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is the final-layer embedding summarizing the input sequence. This multi-headed model is illustrated in Figure
5.13
. The total parameter count is reduced to
m-dL+dKL
, where
m-dL
parameters are in the shared backbone, and
dKL
correspond to the
K
policy heads. The shared backbone enables efficient language modeling, while the separate heads provide sufficient flexibility for each policy to optimize distinct objectives and language styles. During training, each policy matrix is jointly trained with the transformer backbone. At inference time, the user specifies which objective
k_{0}\in[K]
should be the primary objective, and the model computes logit for the text token using
\theta_{k_{0}}\phi
to adapt to inference time user specifications.

Reddit Summary

Instruction:
Rewrite the email below in a friendly, concise tone in a single sentence.

Dear team, due to updated budget constraints, we must reduce remote work days from three to one per week effective next month. Please ensure on-site attendance Monday–Thursday. Thank you for your cooperation.

LM response

MODPO:

Starting next month, please work onsite Monday–Thursday; budget changes limit us to one remote day weekly.

PARM:

Budget limits mean we’ll shift to one WFH day—please join us on-site Mon–Thu from next month; thanks for flexing with us!

Hey team! Budget tweaks mean we’ll have one remote day going forward, so plan to be on-site Mon–Thu starting next month—thanks for rolling with the change!

Table 5.5
:
Examples of
Helpful Assistants
. Helpfulness & Humor.

Prompt:

Human:
What is two factor authentication, and how can it help me stay safe online?

Assistant:

LM response

RLHF (Helpful):

Two factor authentication is a security system that requires you to provide two pieces of information to verify your identity. This can help protect you from fraud and identity theft.

RLHF (Humor):

What do you mean by two factor authentication?

MODPO:

Two-factor authentication (2FA) adds a second proof—usually a code from your phone—on top of your password, so a thief who steals the password still can’t get in.

PARM:

Think of 2FA as a double lock: you enter your password, then confirm with a phone code or authenticator app. That quick extra step blocks most account-takeovers—using it on email first is a great start!

RiC:

It’s a “prove-it-twice” check—password *plus* something you have (SMS code, hardware key). Turn it on for banking, email, socials and you slam the door on 99% of drive-by hacks.

MOPO
:

Picture ordering pizza: the driver needs your address *and* a secret knock. 2FA works the same—password first, then a six-digit code or hardware tap. Even if crooks guess the password, the knock stays secret, so enable it everywhere (especially email and banking) for huge peace-of-mind gains.

5.6.10

Extension to Safety Controlled Preference Optimization

Optimizing large language models for both utility (helpfulness) and safety constitutes an intrinsically multi-objective learning problem. Empirically it is observed that optimizing for one objective often degrades the other: aggressive safety tuning can induce over-refusal on benign inputs, while utility-centric optimization can erode safety guarantees
[
22
,
205
,
162
]
. These observations motivate a principled multi-objective formulation that treats safety and helpfulness as separate, measurable criteria and seeks Pareto-efficient trade-offs rather than ad-hoc scalarization. Hence, we adapt the
MOPO
framework to the setting of balancing helpfulness and safety objectives as part of the preference optimization problem.

In the setting of incorporating safety as a second objective, we let the true human preference for the primary objective be
p(y\succ y^{\prime}\,|\,x)
, and for the safety objective be
q(y\succ 0\,|\,x)
i.e. overloading notation we have,
y\succ 0
implies that generation
y
is safe while
0\succ y
implies that
y
is unsafe, where
0
denotes a
neutral
generation. In essence, we want the model to output winning and safe generations. The goal is to propose a general solution for RLHF
.
with a primary objective and a safe objective, based on constrained optimization of a function of preferences. To this end, we consider a reference policy
\pi_{\mathsf{ref}}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, a real positive regularization parameter
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and let
b\in[0,1]
. The concave constrained optimization problem becomes,

\displaystyle\max_{\pi}\,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\nu\\
y\sim\pi(\cdot\,|\,x)\\
y^{\prime}\sim\mu(\cdot\,|\,x)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[p(y\succ y^{\prime}\,|\,x)]-\tau\mathsf{KL}(\pi\;||\;\pi_{\mathsf{ref}})\,\;\;\texttt{s.t.}\;\;\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\nu\\
y\sim\pi(\cdot\,|\,x)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[q(y\succ 0\,|\,x)]\geq b.

(5.31)

Problem (
5.31
) is analogous to Problem (
5.1
), hence, the derivation carries over by replacing the secondary objective of Problem (
5.1
) with the safety objective of Problem (
5.31
).

Safety Benchmark.

To evaluate safety, we first construct a benchmark that includes discriminative tasks and generative tasks based on previous benchmarks
[
193
,
73
,
201
,
259
]
. Discriminative tasks measure the models’ recognition of multiple safety topics, including bias (CrowS-Pairs
[
150
]
, BBQ
[
160
]
, WinoGrande
[
180
]
), ethics (ETHICS
[
90
]
, Moral Permissibility
[
193
]
, Simple Ethics Questions
[
90
,
130
]
), and toxicity (ToxicGen
[
88
]
, BigBench HHH Alignment
[
193
]
).
In generative tasks, we prompt models to generate harmful content using the prompt dataset, AdvBench
[
259
]
, Real Toxicity Prompts
[
76
]
, ALERT
[
201
]
. We report the percentage of harmless responses based on the HarmBench-Llama2-13B-Chat safety classifier
[
139
]
. Details of the benchmark are given below:

•

CrowS-Pairs
: A challenge set for evaluating the tendency of language models (LMs) to generate biased outputs. We evaluate the English subset and
implementation by LM Evaluation Harness
and report the Percentage of Stereotype metric.

•

Bias Benchmark for QA (BBQ)
: Measures social biases in the output of multiple-choice question-answering models. We use the Lite version and
implementation by BigBench
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

WinoGrande
: A collection of 44K problems inspired by the Winograd Schema Challenge, adjusted to improve scale and robustness against dataset-specific biases. We use the
implementation by LM Evaluation Harness
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

ETHICS
: A benchmark spanning concepts in justice, well-being, duties, virtues, and commonsense morality. We use the
implementation by LM Evaluation Harness
and report the Accuracy for all sub-tasks.

•

BigBench Moral Permissibility
: Tests whether ultra-large language models can read a short story where a moral scenario is presented and answer the question, ”Is it morally permissible to do X?” in a manner similar to humans. We use the
implementation by BigBench
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

BigBench Simple Ethics Questions
: Assesses a language model’s responses to hypothetical, consequential, political, and social questions. We use the
implementation by BigBench
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

ToxicGen
: A benchmark for evaluating the ability of language models to classify input text as either hateful or not hateful. We use the
implementation by LM Evaluation Harness
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

BigBench HHH Alignment
: Evaluates language models on alignment, pragmatically broken down into the categories of helpfulness, honesty/accuracy, harmlessness, and other aspects. We use the
implementation by BigBench
and report the Accuracy metric.

•

AdvBench
contains harmful prompts. We use the prompts provided
here
and generation implementation by LM Evaluation Harness. We report the percentage of harmless responses measured by
HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls
model.

•

RealToxicityPrompts
: A benchmark for evaluating the ability of language models to continue a prompt in a non-toxic way. We use the
implementation by LM Evaluation Harness
report the percentage of harmless responses measured by
HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls
model.

•

ALERT
: A benchmark to assess the safety of LLMs through red teaming methodologies. We use the prompts provided
here
and generation implementation by LM Evaluation Harness. We report the percentage of harmless responses measured by
HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls
model.

•

ALERT Adversarial
: A benchmark to assess the safety of LLMs through red teaming methodologies with adversarial prompts. We use the prompts provided
here
and generation implementation by LM Evaluation Harness. We report the percentage of harmless responses measured by
HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls
model.

•

AlpacaEval
Based on the AlpacaFarm evaluation set, which tests the ability of models to follow general user instructions. We employ the
official implementation
report the LC Win Rate.

Dataset Details.

In the supervised fine-tuning stage, we follow
[
208
,
60
]
to use a mix of helpfulness data from UltraChat
[
63
]
and safety data from PKU-SafeRLHF
[
60
]
. In the
MOPO
stage, we use 30K helpfulness data from UltraFeedback
[
56
]
and 30K safety data from PKU-SafeRLHF. UltraFeedback contains instruction-following tasks that provide paired helpfulness preference rankings, and we treat all responses as safe since they undergo human filtering. PKU-SafeRLHF provides both paired helpfulness preference rankings and binary safety labels.

Overall Evaluation Metrics.

In the following experiments, we report both the helpfulness and safety performance. Helpfulness is measured using AlpacaEval 2.0 (Alpaca)
[
66
,
67
]
. Safety is assessed using the performance of discriminative tasks (
Disc.
), generative tasks (
Gen.
) from the aforementioned benchmark, and the average safety over these two metrics (
Overall
). The results are presented in Table
5.7
and Figure
5.14
.

Chapter 6

Closing Remarks and Future Directions

This thesis advances the field of Reinforcement Learning (RL) by addressing critical challenges in safety, scalability, and alignment across constrained and preference-based learning settings. By developing algorithms such as ACPO,
e-COP
,
warmPref-PS
,
\mathsf{PSPL}
, and
MOPO
, this thesis contributes to building robust frameworks for constrained optimization and preference-based learning. These algorithms demonstrate strong theoretical guarantees and empirical performance, tackling diverse challenges like average-reward optimization, episodic learning, efficient exploration, and alignment with human values in safety-critical applications. Below, a brief summary of results and possible future directions of research are provided.

Chapter
1
studied the problem of learning policies that maximize average-rewards for a given CMDP with average-cost constraints. It showed that the current algorithms with constraint violation bounds for the discounted setting do not generalize to the average setting. Hence, the Average-Constrained Policy Optimization (ACPO) algorithm was proposed that was inspired by the TRPO class of algorithms but based on theoretical sensitivity-type bounds for average-CMDPs. Experimental results on a range of OpenAI Gym environments (including some high dimensional ones) showed the effectiveness of ACPO on ACMDP RL problems, as well as its superior empirical performance vis-a-vis some current alternatives. A direction for future work is implementation of ACPO to fully exploit the parallelization potential.

Chapter
2
then introduced an easy to implement, scalable policy optimization algorithm
e-COP
for episodic RL problems with constraints due to safety or other considerations. It was based on a policy difference lemma for the episodic setting, which surprisingly has quite a different form than the ones for infinite-horizon discounted or average settings. This provided the theoretical foundation for the algorithm, which was designed by incorporating several time-tested, practical as well as novel ideas. Policy optimization algorithms for Constrained MDPs tend to be numerical unstable and non-scalable due to the need for inverting the Fisher information matrix.
e-COP
sidesteped both of these issues by introducing a quadratic damping penalty term that worked remarkably well. The algorithm development was well supported by theory, as well as with extensive empirical analysis on a range of Safety Gym and Safe MuJoco benchmark environments against a suite of baseline algorithms adapted from their non-episodic roots.

Next, Chapter
3
proposed
warmPref-PS
, a Bayesian posterior sampling-based algorithm that efficiently incorporated offline preference data to warm-start the online learning phase. Theoretical analysis and foundations for bridging the gap between fixed, offline preferences and online learning was also provided. Further, Bootstrapped
warmPref-PS
was introduced, a computationally tractable extension designed to handle large-scale environments. Theoretical results and empirical evaluations demonstrated the robustness and superior performance of
warmPref-PS
. While additional work is needed to refine the approach for RLHF, Chapter
3
provides a promising foundation for further development in this space.

Chapter
4
then proposed
\mathsf{PSPL}
, a Bayesian Top-two posterior sampling-style algorithm that efficiently incorporated offline preference data to learn a MDP with unknown rewards and transitions. An important attribute of
\mathsf{PSPL}
was credit assignment to state-action pairs since the learner only received trajectory level feedback. The first theoretical analysis and foundations for bridging the gap between fixed, offline preferences and online learning were provided. Further, Bootstrapped
\mathsf{PSPL}
, an approximate algorithmic version of
\mathsf{PSPL}
was derived due to loss of conjugacy in posterior updates. Finally, various ablation studies were done to highlight the effect of offline preferences in online learning.

Finally, Chapter
5
introduced
MOPO
, a scalable and iterative algorithm for multi-objective preference optimization in LLMs. The chapter formulated alignment as a constrained optimization problem, where a primary objective was maximized while maintaining secondary objectives, such as safety and honesty, above tunable thresholds.
MOPO
effectively integrated preference-based learning with constraint satisfaction to handle multiple, potentially conflicting objectives in alignment tasks. Theoretical justification for the approach and extensive empirical evaluations demonstrated the scalability of
MOPO
to multi-billion parameter models and its superior performance compared to existing baselines. While additional research is needed to explore its extensions to dynamic or hierarchical constraints,
MOPO
provides a robust and generalizable framework for balancing competing alignment objectives in practical LLM training.

Given that, notice there are two key commonalities across this thesis that provide a direction of future research.

Firstly, Chapters
3
and
4
use posterior sampling as the general framework. Simplicity of the algorithms, similarity in various settings, and excellent numerical performance compared to their optimism-based competitors are important factors for practitioners. However, all of the posterior sampling algorithms designed in this dissertation are for the tabular setting where the state and the action spaces are finite. A natural next step would be to extend these algorithms to the continuous state and action spaces, and to propose model-free algorithms for such settings.

Secondly, while the algorithms developed in this thesis demonstrate strong performance and theoretical guarantees, they have primarily been evaluated using synthetic datasets, which offer controlled environments for benchmarking and analysis. Real-world data, however, often presents additional complexities, such as noise, bias, and variability, which are harder to model but critical for practical deployment. Developing RL algorithms that can effectively handle these challenges without compromising safety, scalability, or alignment is an important avenue for future research. Importantly, the insights and methodologies introduced in this thesis provide a strong foundation for tackling these challenges.
